    Onboarding guide

    4. Domain & Integration Events + Outbox Dual-Dispatch

    What this chapter covers. This group is the codebase's event spine: how an aggregate says "something happened", how that fact is persisted so it cannot be lost, and how it eventually reaches every interested handler, whether that handler lives in the same process or in an extracted microservice across a broker. Three questions drive the whole design. How do we publish events reliably when persistence and dispatch are separate steps that can each fail independently? How do we keep application code identical whether a module ships inside the monolith or as its own service? And how does a small single-process application avoid paying for machinery it does not need? The answer to the first is the transactional outbox with an at-least-once background drainer (ADR-003); the answer to the second is a transport-agnostic message bus plus a consumer-side inbox (ADR-006, ADR-008, ADR-021); the answer to the third is a pair of resolved posture flags that turn the outbox and the inbox on for a broker and off for the in-process transport, each stating its choice once at startup. The types here implement all three: the event contracts, the in-process dispatcher, the outbox and inbox tables with their background services and admin surface, and the swappable in-process/broker buses. Two opt-in capabilities are built on top of that spine and live here too: outbound webhooks, which carry an integration event to an external subscriber as a signed HTTP POST (ADR-142), and the process-manager primitive, which drives a long-running process from domain events and timeouts (ADR-143).

    If you have not yet met the Result pattern, aggregate roots and domain events, or the database-per-service rule, skim primer §2 first: this chapter builds directly on them.

    The two kinds of event

    Everything starts with two marker interfaces in the Domain layer. IDomainEvent is the base contract: a DateOccurred timestamp (when the business action happened, not when it was dispatched, MMCA.Common.Domain/Interfaces/IDomainEvent.cs:10) and a MessageId GUID used for consumer-side deduplication (MMCA.Common.Domain/Interfaces/IDomainEvent.cs:13). IIntegrationEvent extends IDomainEvent and adds no members (MMCA.Common.Domain/Interfaces/IIntegrationEvent.cs:15): it is a pure role marker. The distinction is semantic and load-bearing. A domain event is intra-module (raised and handled inside one bounded context); an integration event is inter-module (one module publishes, others react, for example Identity's UserRegistered consumed by another module). Because integration events are domain events, they ride the exact same outbox machinery; the system never needs a second capture pipeline. What differs is only how they are delivered after capture, which the routing rules below make precise.

    The base records supply the defaults. BaseDomainEvent stamps DateOccurred at construction (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:28) and mints a fresh MessageId (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:35), both init so a deserialized event keeps the values it was created with. Its remarks call out the trap that follows from being a record: structural equality is not a deduplication mechanism, because those two defaults differ per instance, so two logically identical events are never equal (BaseDomainEvent.cs:9-17). Dedup is the inbox's job, keyed on MessageId. A second remark defends the creation-time stamp as a domain-modelling choice rather than an untestable clock: an event's occurrence instant is the moment the aggregate raises it, and threading a TimeProvider through every aggregate to move that stamp would not improve the model (BaseDomainEvent.cs:18-25).

    BaseIntegrationEvent adds a virtual SchemaVersion defaulting to 1 (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:33). Additive or optional field changes keep the version, but a breaking change (a renamed, removed, or retyped field) requires a NEW event type plus an upcaster, never a silent reshape of an existing contract (ADR-010). That upcaster is a registration, not a convention (ADR-090): the module calls services.AddEventUpcaster<FooV1, FooV2, FooUpcaster>(), and a host still receiving the retired contract over a broker adds RegisterUpcastedIntegrationEventConsumer<FooV1>() beside its RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<FooV2>() (BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:22-31). Handlers are then written once, against the newest contract. Declaring SchemaVersion virtual with a default is what kept adding the member a non-breaking change for every event that already existed.

    Two reusable shapes sit on top of those bases. EntityChangedEvent<TIdentifierType> is a CRUD-lifecycle event carrying a DomainEntityState (Added/Updated/Deleted) and the affected EntityId (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/EntityChangedEvent.cs:24-27), so entities do not each hand-roll three near-identical event records; its own remarks reserve it for generic lifecycle events and send state-machine transitions such as OrderPaid back to BaseDomainEvent (EntityChangedEvent.cs:16-20). OutputCacheEvictionRequested is the framework's one concrete integration event (MMCA.Common.Domain/IntegrationEvents/OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:29): a tag list broadcast to every host that serves output-cached responses, because ASP.NET Core's output cache is per host and a write in the owning service otherwise leaves a stale response in front of every other replica (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:12-17). Its Tags list defaults to empty rather than being required, so a message arriving without the field deserializes into a harmless no-op instead of faulting a consumer (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:37), and it carries an explicit [EventName("Common.OutputCacheEvictionRequested.v1")] identity (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:28).

    This split (markers and base records in Domain, all dispatch and persistence machinery in Application/Infrastructure) is textbook [Rubric §3, Clean Architecture] (the domain declares what an event is; outer layers decide how it travels) and [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (an explicit, first-class event model rather than implicit side effects). The SchemaVersion plus upcaster convention is the [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] angle: an event on the wire is a versioned contract like any API surface.

    Stored identity: what a row remembers an event as

    An event in memory is a CLR type; an event in a table is a string. Which string it is decides whether a row survives a refactoring, and EventNameResolver is the single cached lookup that answers it for both storage sites (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/EventNameResolver.cs:19). An event that declares EventNameAttribute is stored under that declared name, which no rename, namespace move, or assembly move changes. An event without one keeps exactly the identity it had before this type existed: GetStorageName falls back to the assembly-qualified name for the outbox (EventNameResolver.cs:47-51) and GetInboxName to the short type name for the inbox (EventNameResolver.cs:59-60). That fallback is what makes adoption opt-in and leaves rows already in flight unaffected. The declared name is cached per type, including the null "no attribute" answer, so the common unannotated case pays one reflection lookup per type per process rather than one per event (EventNameResolver.cs:26,35-38). The reverse direction, resolving a stored name that is not a CLR name, scans loaded assemblies lazily and degrades to the types that did load when a dependency is missing (EventNameResolver.cs:75-81,90-100).

    Raising and capturing: where the outbox is written

    Aggregates raise events by calling AddDomainEvent() (see AuditableAggregateRootEntity<TIdentifierType> in G02), which simply buffers them on the entity. Nothing is dispatched yet; the events ride along until the next save. The actual capture happens in EF Core's save pipeline, in DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor (G07). On SavingChanges it snapshots the change tracker's aggregate roots that have buffered events and calls OutboxMessage.FromDomainEvent(...) on each, serializing the event to JSON with cycle-ignoring options, resolving its stored EventType through EventNameResolver, capturing the current W3C trace and span IDs, and copying the caller's ambient OutboxOrigin (user, roles, permission claims, tenant, correlation id) onto the row (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxMessage.cs:149-173). Those OutboxMessage rows are Added to the same DbContext, so the outbox row and the aggregate change land in one atomic transaction (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Interceptors/DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:268-288). This is the single most important guarantee in the chapter: if the business data committed, the event is durably recorded; if the transaction rolled back, neither exists. There is no window where they disagree. [Rubric §8, Data Architecture] (transactional integrity) and [Rubric §6] both hinge on this atomicity. Crucially, the rows go to the same physical database as the aggregate: every relational source owns its own OutboxMessages table, never a shared one (ADR-006; the table, its origin columns, and its three filtered indexes, pending, processed, and ordering, are configured in MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/ApplicationDbContext.cs:816-873, with the inbox's table and unique index at ApplicationDbContext.cs:721-731; see the primer on database-per-service).

    Two details of the capture exist to stop duplicate rows. The interceptor snapshots exactly the events it captured and removes exactly those again after dispatch, so anything a handler raises mid-dispatch survives to a later capture instead of being wiped (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:424). And a save that started but never completed (a failed save followed by an execution-strategy retry) has its still-Added outbox rows detached before the next capture, so the retry does not write a second row per event (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:230,338).

    The routing split: local events dispatch in-process, integration events wait for the bus

    Here is the detail that most people get wrong, and it is the heart of the design. After the transaction commits (SavedChanges), the interceptor does not treat all captured events the same. Pure domain events (the local events) are dispatched in-process through IDomainEventDispatcher and their outbox rows are then marked processed. Integration events are deliberately not dispatched in-process at all: their outbox rows stay unprocessed, and the background OutboxProcessor later publishes them through IMessageBus, so the registered transport (in-process for the monolith, broker for an extracted service) decides delivery (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:274-286,369-381). That routing is what makes AddDomainEvent(someIntegrationEvent) broker-correct: without it, such an event would be dispatched locally and marked processed, silently never reaching the wire. On the asynchronous save path the local rows are also inserted already leased: each save stamps them with a LockedUntil of LeaseSeconds ahead under a fresh LockToken, so the processor cannot claim a local row while its in-process dispatch is still running (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:98,280,304-315, OutboxSettings.cs:80-90); the synchronous path never dispatches in-process, so its rows go in unleased (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:108). When the context has no outbox table (Cosmos) or the host turned the outbox off, the interceptor falls back to dispatching everything in-process, since nothing would carry integration events to a processor anyway (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:64,292-298). One more subtlety: when the save runs inside a Transactional command's transaction, all this post-save work is deferred until after commit (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:358), flushed by DbContextFactory once the commit succeeds and dropped on rollback, so handler side effects never act on state that could still roll back.

    The mark-processed step is not a second nested SaveChanges. It goes through OutboxFinalizer, which stamps every row in the batch with a single set-based ExecuteUpdate and then re-syncs the change tracker (original value first, then clearing IsModified) so a later save does not re-issue the statement (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxFinalizer.cs:27-54), keeping the hottest write path (every event-raising command) free of an extra full save. The ProcessedOn stamp comes from an injected TimeProvider, so a test's fake clock moves it together with the processor's lease, backoff, and retention arithmetic (OutboxFinalizer.cs:21-25,36). Its sibling ReleaseLeaseAsync is the failure path: when in-process dispatch throws, the interceptor clears the lease on that save's local rows, guarded by the save's own token so a row a processor has since claimed is left alone, and then signals the processor, which can take the rows once the processing delay has elapsed instead of waiting out the full lease (OutboxFinalizer.cs:57-98, DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:383-394,402-422). That release is best effort: a failure there is logged and the rows simply wait out the lease.

    The dispatcher itself (DomainEventDispatcher) is a small, performance-conscious piece of machinery. For each event it resolves every registered IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent> and, if the event is also an integration event, every IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> (MMCA.Common.Application/Services/DomainEventDispatcher.cs:46-67), invoking each through a compiled expression-tree delegate cached per (event type, handler interface) so the generic HandleAsync call avoids reflection and boxing at runtime (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:41-43,97-117), relevant to [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]. The integration branch runs the event through IEventUpcasterRegistry first, so a retired contract (including one deserialized from an outbox row written before an upgrade) reaches the handlers written against its successor (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:62); the domain-event branch is deliberately untouched, because intra-module handlers keep receiving the original type and instance.

    Handlers that perform side effects (email, downstream writes) derive from one of two bases, and the contract of both is the opposite of what "safe" suggests. SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent> logs the failure with handler and event context and then lets the exception propagate unchanged, through an exception filter so the log write precedes any unwinding and the stack trace stays intact (MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:36-47,61-70); OperationCanceledException passes straight through with no log line, because host shutdown is not a delivery failure (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:42). Swallowing made the "the outbox will retry it" promise false: a handler that reported success got its outbox row marked processed, so nothing ever retried and the side effect was lost (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:13-20). The consequence to design for is batch redelivery: the interceptor dispatches every local event of one save in a single call, so one rethrowing handler skips the mark-processed step for that whole local batch and the processor redelivers all of them, not just the event that failed (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:21-29). ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent> is its cross-module sibling and adds the one thing integration handlers all need: because they are registered as singletons they cannot constructor-inject a scoped service, so the base opens an AsyncServiceScope per delivery and hands the subclass that scope's provider (MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:51-55), with the same log-and-rethrow envelope and an overridable LogHandlerFailure for source-generated logging (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:87-92). Subclasses of either base must be idempotent about their own event and about every sibling event raised by the same save.

    Who the delivery runs as: the captured origin

    A delivered event runs a poll cycle later, on another thread and possibly in another process, so without help it would run as an anonymous, tenant-less system call: a handler reading ICurrentUserService would see nobody, its writes would carry the system audit sentinel, a shared-database host would read across tenants, and the delivery could not be joined back to the interaction that produced it. OutboxOrigin is the record struct that closes that gap: four positional values (user id, roles as a comma-separated list, tenant id, correlation id, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxOrigin.cs:30-34) plus the user's permission claims as a JSON array, carried as an init property rather than a fifth parameter so the shipped constructor is unchanged (OutboxOrigin.cs:35-40). It is deliberately the mirror of InternalCommandOrigin so the two background hops capture the same values through the same helper and cannot drift (OutboxOrigin.cs:14-16). It carries no trace context, because OutboxMessage's TraceId/SpanId already do and are stamped from the ambient Activity rather than from a scope (OutboxOrigin.cs:16-18). A default value means "nothing was captured", which is exactly what a seeder, a design-time write, or a row written before these columns existed reads back as (OutboxOrigin.cs:20-24); FromDomainEvent's origin parameter is defaulted for the same reason, so a caller with nothing to capture keeps the single-argument call it always made (OutboxMessage.cs:149).

    Capture is one read per save, not one per event. The interceptor is a singleton and cannot reach a scoped service, so DbContextFactory attaches a live accessor to the scoped context (DbContextFactory.cs:156-158) and the interceptor reads it once through CurrentOutboxOrigin (ApplicationDbContext.cs:158,164, DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:268); the two event buses do the same once per batch (InProcessEventBus.cs:91, BrokerEventBus.cs:81). One save is one scope's work, so every row of that save carries the same origin by construction. The values land on five nullable columns of the row (OutboxMessage.cs:104-131): tenant, roles, and correlation id are mapped with explicit widths, while the permissions column is unbounded and never truncated (OutboxMessage.cs:124, ApplicationDbContext.cs:682-689).

    Restore is per row, immediately before delivery, and on a scope of the row's own. The processor creates a FRESH DI scope for each row, for the batch's target, and hands the row's five values to the shared AmbientOrigin helper, which writes them onto that scope before the payload is even deserialized, stamping the rebuilt identity with an Outbox authentication type (OutboxProcessor.cs:568). A fresh scope is what lets the tenant change between rows of one shared-target batch, since the tenant context refuses a change once resolved, and it means one row's identity can never answer for another's; the cycle scope's context still holds the claimed rows and writes the lease renewals and the outcomes, so only delivery moves to the row scope (OutboxProcessor.cs:490-492). The in-process path then gets the right ambient context for free, and on the broker path BrokerMessageBus reads those same scoped services back out to stamp tenant, user, roles, permissions, and correlation headers on the outgoing message, collecting only values that are actually present so an absent header keeps meaning "the publisher had nothing to say" rather than becoming a false answer (BrokerMessageBus.cs:126-154). On the far side, IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> applies the mirror step through ConsumerOriginRestore before the inbox is touched, because under database-per-tenant the restored tenant is what routes the store to the right database, and a message from an older publisher carries no headers and restores nothing (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:76, the helper itself in G14). The restore handle stays open for the whole consume, so a handler that opens its own scope from the root factory still runs as the publisher's origin (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:76). The identity rebuilt from those headers is stamped with an IntegrationEvent authentication type, which is what makes it count as authenticated and names the hop it came back from (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:51). One mechanism therefore carries the authorization principal, the audit stamps, the tenant routing, and the correlation id across both asynchronous hops: [Rubric §17, Security & Compliance] and [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] at the same time.

    The safety net: how the processor schedules itself

    The OutboxProcessor is a BackgroundService and the most intricate type in the group; most of its complexity is about not wasting work. It exists because the steps between commit and mark-processed can be interrupted: the process can crash, or in-process dispatch can throw. When that happens the row stays unprocessed (and the interceptor releases its lease and signals the processor on its failure path, DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:383-394) and the processor catches it on a later cycle. This is the at-least-once guarantee of ADR-003. Its unavoidable cost is that the same event may be delivered more than once, so handlers must be idempotent. That is not a wart; it is the documented contract and a healthy discipline regardless.

    The processor never blindly polls on a fixed clock. Its loop is the shared PollingLoop (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:124-136): after a five second startup delay (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Polling/PollingLoop.cs:18,59) it drains every outbox target once per cycle and aggregates the per-target results into an OutboxCycleResult (OutboxProcessor.cs:171), then waits on IOutboxSignal for whichever comes first: a signal (a writer called Signal() after persisting a row; OutboxSignal is a thin wrapper over the shared WakeUpSignal, a SemaphoreSlim(0, 1) whose single-permit cap deliberately absorbs a burst of surplus signals, since one batch drains everything anyway, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxSignal.cs:10-23, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Polling/WakeUpSignal.cs:18-30), the moment the earliest pending-but-not-yet-eligible row becomes eligible (the smart wait), or the fallback PollingIntervalSeconds (PollingLoop.cs:94, arithmetic in ComputeWaitTime at PollingLoop.cs:117-130 with a one second floor so an overdue row cannot hot-loop the service, PollingLoop.cs:21). A target is one owned relational source against the shared database, plus one extra unit per tenant that keeps its own copy of a source, because a tenant database has its own OutboxMessages table that nothing else would ever open; the processor asks the injected FrameworkTableTargets for that list (OutboxProcessor.cs:66,161). Each target is visited in its own DI scope with the tenant set before the context is asked for, since the tenant is what routes the factory to the right database (the same per-target scoping the admin surface documents, OutboxAdministration.cs:22-24).

    Rows are eligible ProcessingDelaySeconds after creation, and that delay defaults to 0 (range 0 to 600), so an integration event is published as soon as the post-save signal wakes the processor, tens of milliseconds after the save (OutboxSettings, OutboxSettings.cs:33-45). The fetched batch is split against a cutoff computed once per cycle (OutboxProcessor.cs:206,221, fetch at OutboxProcessor.cs:311-320). That delay sets eligibility only; the property's own remarks are explicit that it does not bound the duplicate-dispatch window, because local rows of an asynchronous save are inserted under the LeaseSeconds lease, so the in-process pipeline (save, dispatch, mark processed) races the processor only once that lease expires, and handlers must be idempotent regardless. A host raises it only to hold rows back deliberately, for example to give a failed in-process local dispatch a pause before the processor retries it (OutboxSettings.cs:36-43). The smart wait means that even when the fallback interval is raised in a deployed environment (the default is 2s and the property's own remarks name 300 as the deployed value to cut idle polling, OutboxSettings.cs:23-31), a signalled event still goes out promptly and a held-back row goes out as soon as its delay elapses. Batches are 50 rows (OutboxSettings.cs:17). One unreachable database cannot starve the others: the targets are drained through the shared PollingLoop.DrainAllAsync with a per-source error callback, and a failing target simply contributes nothing (and zero backlog) to this cycle (OutboxProcessor.cs:171).

    Claiming, scale-out, and ordered delivery

    Because a deployment may run more than one replica, each cycle claims its eligible prefix with a lease (LockedUntil plus LockToken, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxMessage.cs:54-67) via a conditional ExecuteUpdate before dispatching (OutboxProcessor.cs:372-376), and the poll query skips rows under an unexpired lease (OutboxProcessor.cs:311-320). Two replicas therefore can never double-dispatch the same row, and a replica that dies mid-batch releases its rows when the lease expires (LeaseSeconds, default 300, OutboxSettings.cs:90). When the claim comes back partial, the processor works only the ids that carry its own token. That is scale-out safety by construction, not merely by the minReplicas: 1 deployment convention. The claim is not the end of it, because one lease covers the whole batch and a slow batch could outlive it: before each row the remaining lease is checked against the clock, and once less than half of LeaseSeconds is left the processor flushes its buffered stamps and renews every not-yet-dispatched row of the batch in ONE statement guarded on the batch's lock token; a row that renewal finds under another token is skipped (OutboxProcessor.cs:474-479,547,707). Outcomes are written two ways. A failed row's retry or dead-letter state is written, token-guarded, as soon as that row is done (OutboxProcessor.cs:1015). Delivered rows are buffered and stamped processed together, by one guarded set-based update per flush: at the end of the batch, before a lease renewal, and on every early exit (a shutdown, a failed write) (OutboxProcessor.cs:38-41,912), in chunks small enough to stay under SQL Server's parameter limit (OutboxProcessor.cs:89-92). A replica that lost a row mid-dispatch therefore drops its stale stamp instead of overwriting the new owner's. A shutdown signalled while a row is in flight still records the outcomes of the rows already done, under a five second budget of its own (OutboxProcessor.cs:102,790,951); what a crash (or a failed exit flush) costs is the rows delivered since the last flush, which are redelivered once their lease expires (OutboxProcessor.cs:44-48).

    The claim is also where ordered delivery is enforced. An event that opts in by implementing IHasOrderingKey has its key copied onto the outbox row at capture (OutboxMessage.cs:97,172), and the claim predicate then refuses that row while any earlier unprocessed, non-dead-lettered row shares the key, expressed as a correlated NOT EXISTS inside the update itself so a racing replica loses on the row rather than on a check made before the race (OutboxProcessor.cs:437-451). Within one cycle, SelectOrderedCandidates keeps at most the first row per key (OutboxProcessor.cs:357,405), and a cycle that deferred such key-mates asks for an immediate re-poll once it delivers anything, rather than serializing a key at one row per polling interval (OutboxProcessor.cs:260,359-361). A batch containing no keyed row skips that predicate entirely, so hosts that never declare a key pay nothing for the feature (OutboxProcessor.cs:372). The cost is head-of-line blocking, documented on the interface itself: a retrying keyed row blocks its successors until it succeeds or exhausts its retries, so keys must be as narrow as the requirement really is (one per aggregate, never a constant).

    Failures, dead-letters, and keeping the table (and telemetry) bounded

    Delivery failures split into outcomes worth keeping straight. A transient failure (a handler or broker publish throwing) increments the row's RetryCount, records LastError, and re-leases the row for an explicit exponential backoff rather than leaving this cycle's claim on it (OutboxProcessor.cs:660-661). The backoff is RetryBackoffBaseSeconds * 2^(n-1) (base 10s, OutboxSettings.cs:107) multiplied by a random jitter factor in [0.8, 1.2] and capped at the lease (OutboxProcessor.cs:1107-1108, delegating to PollingLoop.cs:197); the jitter is what stops fifty rows that failed together on one dependency outage from retrying in lockstep against that same dependency. The poll query only ever selects rows with RetryCount < MaxRetries (5 by default, OutboxProcessor.cs:316, OutboxSettings.cs:21), so once a row exhausts its retries it stops being fetched, stalls unprocessed with its last error, and is counted on the outbox.dead_letter.count counter with reason=retries_exhausted plus one loud Error log line at the moment of exhaustion (OutboxProcessor.cs:681-689,1197). A row whose stored EventType resolves to nothing is treated more gently: the FIRST such attempt is retried through the normal backoff with a Warning naming the fix (give the event an [EventName]), because the declaring assembly may simply not be loaded yet; only the second attempt is terminal, and it dead-letters the row rather than marking it processed (RetryCount set to MaxRetries, lease cleared, ProcessedOn left null), counting it with reason=type_unresolvable (OutboxProcessor.cs:1065,1080-1097). The row therefore leaves the poll, so an undeliverable payload cannot block the queue behind it, yet stays a dead letter an operator can find and replay. A host configured with MaxRetries of 1 gets no retry here either (OutboxProcessor.cs:1078-1080). Bus publishes additionally run inside a circuit breaker built from BrokerResilienceDefaults and carrying no retry strategy of its own, since the outbox already owns retry (OutboxProcessor.cs:112-113,1111-1125). The breaker wraps only the IMessageBus publish of an integration event: that is the broker hop on a host with broker messaging, but on a monolith the default InProcessMessageBus runs the integration-event handlers inside the breaker, so failing handlers can open it too. The pure domain-event branch and every database call stay outside it, since a breaker on those would open exactly when the processor most needs to persist retry state (OutboxProcessor.cs:812-821). An open circuit is reported as its own fact: one BrokerMetrics.CircuitOpenCounter increment per row and one Warning per batch rather than fifty identical lines (OutboxProcessor.cs:540,669,1203), and a row skipped by an open circuit is re-leased for a backoff derived from the breaker's break duration, capped at the lease, instead of the per-attempt exponential one (OutboxProcessor.cs:660, PollingLoop.cs:219).

    The instruments themselves live in OutboxMetrics, a single OpenTelemetry meter named MMCA.Common.Outbox (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxMetrics.cs:19) carrying the dead-letter counter (OutboxMetrics.cs:41-44), a success counter (OutboxMetrics.cs:47-50), an end-to-end delivery lag histogram in seconds, the number that answers "how far behind is eventual consistency right now" (OutboxMetrics.cs:57-60, recorded at OutboxProcessor.cs:990), an observable backlog depth gauge (OutboxMetrics.cs:75-79), and an oldest-pending age gauge per data source, which is what an alert on a wedged outbox fires on (OutboxMetrics.cs:98-102). Both gauges are per instance, not fleet-wide, and both are derived from the fetch wherever they can be: the poll is already ordered by OccurredOn, so its first row is the oldest (OutboxProcessor.cs:216,318), and a short batch is the whole backlog, so only a saturated batch pays for a COUNT (OutboxProcessor.cs:280-292). This is dense [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] and [Rubric §31, Cost/FinOps] territory: both the poll and that count run inside a named OutboxPoll activity (OutboxProcessor.cs:78,285,311) which OutboxPollFilterProcessor (G16) suppresses from telemetry export, so a fleet of idle services polling around the clock does not flood Application Insights, and the per-message success line is Debug for the same reason (OutboxProcessor.cs:1182). Each dispatched message also re-parents an OutboxProcess consumer activity onto the trace context stored on its row, so the broker hop stays linked to the request that raised the event. The parent is rebuilt with the row's stored W3C trace flags, the request's sampling decision, so a parent-based sampler exports the delivery span only when the request itself was sampled, and a row with no stored flags reads as not sampled (OutboxMessage.cs:78-85, OutboxProcessor.cs:1136,1152-1156).

    A sibling OutboxCleanupService keeps the tables bounded. Every CleanupIntervalHours (default 6, OutboxSettings.cs:78) it purges processed rows older than RetentionDays (default 7, OutboxCleanupService.cs:60,91, OutboxSettings.cs:70), then purges dead-lettered rows on their own DeadLetterRetentionDays window, falling back to RetentionDays when that setting is left at its default of zero (OutboxCleanupService.cs:160-164, OutboxSettings.cs:118), since those rows never get a ProcessedOn and would otherwise accumulate forever inside the pending index that every poll re-scans; every dead-letter deletion is logged at Warning with its count (OutboxCleanupService.cs:26-28,217). When the inbox is enabled it purges processed inbox rows on the same cutoff (OutboxCleanupService.cs:53,189). Setting RetentionDays to 0 disables the sweep entirely (OutboxCleanupService.cs:73). Because payloads may contain personal data, this sweep is also part of the privacy posture of ADR-005. Both background services take an injected TimeProvider (OutboxProcessor.cs:790, OutboxCleanupService.cs:49,57) so tests can drive an hour-scale loop deterministically instead of waiting on wall-clock time, a small but real [Rubric §14, Testability] win.

    Dead letters are not only swept, they are operable. OutboxAdministration is the scoped EF-backed admin surface behind IOutboxAdministration: it lists dead letters with merged paging across every target and a hard 500-row page cap (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Administration/OutboxAdministration.cs:45,74,83), counts pending rows (OutboxAdministration.cs:280), and replays dead letters as one set-based UPDATE per target followed by a Signal() so the processor picks them up immediately rather than on the next interval (OutboxAdministration.cs:138,169,190). Its fourth operation, RepublishProcessedAsync, re-publishes the integration events processed within a UTC window of at most 7 days: it asks each source for the distinct stored names in the window, resolves them as the processor would, updates only the names that resolve to an IIntegrationEvent, and signals the processor (OutboxAdministration.cs:53-63,196-206,273). It refuses to run unless the consumer inbox is enabled, because the inbox is what turns a republish into a harmless duplicate (OutboxAdministration.cs:34-35,210-214). It visits the same tenant-expanded targets in the same per-scope way the two background services do (OutboxAdministration.cs:16-27), and its validation failures come back as Result errors rather than exceptions (OutboxAdministration.cs:48-63).

    The pluggable transport, and the posture flags that decide it

    Here is the boundary that makes a module extractable without rewriting its handlers. Application code that wants to publish an integration event depends on IEventBus (or on the lower-level IMessageBus, both defined in Application, so neither ever sees a broker client). Infrastructure supplies two interchangeable implementations of each, selected by registration:

    • Monolith mode (the defaults, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:360,366). InProcessEventBus writes the events to the outbox in one save, dispatches them in-process, and marks them processed through the same OutboxFinalizer path as the interceptor (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessEventBus.cs:78-104), falling back to a plain dispatch when the context has no outbox support or the host disabled the outbox (InProcessEventBus.cs:81-85). InProcessMessageBus just hands the event straight to the dispatcher (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessMessageBus.cs:21-32); it is what the OutboxProcessor calls when draining an integration-event row in monolith mode.
    • Broker mode (AddBrokerMessaging, which replaces both registrations, DependencyInjection.cs:771,777). BrokerEventBus writes the whole batch to the outbox in ONE save and then signals the processor without dispatching in-process (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerEventBus.cs:65-94), because the consumers live in other processes, so an in-process dispatch would be wrong; a data source with no outbox support throws loudly here rather than silently dropping events (BrokerEventBus.cs:70-76). The OutboxProcessor then drains the row and publishes it through BrokerMessageBus, which serializes the event as its runtime type and publishes it on that type's topic, so routing binds to the concrete event class rather than the IIntegrationEvent base interface (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerMessageBus.cs:58-62,83-87). The bytes go out through the IBrokerPublisher of whichever transport MessageBus:Provider selects: RabbitMQ and PostgreSQL are built in, and a vendor adapter such as MMCA.Common.Azure or MMCA.Common.Aws registers its own (BrokerMessageBus.cs:14-18,46-51). The bus starts a Producer span and writes its W3C traceparent and tracestate as message headers, so distributed traces stay connected across the broker hop (BrokerMessageBus.cs:70-81,106-118).

    Whether the outbox exists at all is itself configuration, and it resolves from the transport. MessageBusSettings exposes IsOutboxEnabled and IsInboxEnabled, each defaulting to ON for a broker provider and OFF for InProcess (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:136,170). A single-process application dispatches every event inside the process that raised it, so store-and-forward buys it two background services, a table and a poll loop and nothing else; a broker deployment cannot deliver at all without it. An explicit value wins in both directions for the in-process transport, so a monolith that wants at-least-once delivery across a crash sets MessageBus:EnableOutbox=true (MessageBusSettings.cs:151), but turning the outbox OFF under a broker is refused at registration with a startup failure rather than honored, because it would drop every cross-service event silently (DependencyInjection.cs:862). Neither posture is allowed to be invisible: with the outbox off, OutboxDisabledNoticeService logs one startup Information line naming the changed guarantee (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Administration/OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:22-38, registered at DependencyInjection.cs:190-197), and with the inbox explicitly off under a broker, InboxDisabledWarningService logs one Warning (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:20-36, DependencyInjection.cs:795). The level difference is deliberate: the first is the default posture of a small application, the second is an opt-out of a safety feature.

    The selection between transports is a pure DI swap: no application or domain code changes. That is the whole point of [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]: transport choices live at the edges, and the architecture transport-boundary rule forbids Application/Domain/Shared from referencing a broker client at all (RabbitMQ, Azure Service Bus, Amazon SQS and SNS, and MassTransit, which is banned outright) (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Layering/ArchitectureRules.Transport.cs:14-23, MMCA.Common/Tests/Architecture/MMCA.Common.Architecture.Tests/Layering/MicroserviceExtractionTests.cs:7, ADR-007 and ADR-008). Note the deliberate division of labor: the *EventBus types own outbox persistence (write and signal); the *MessageBus types own delivery only and are invoked by the processor when draining already-persisted rows.

    Consuming from the broker: the inbox and the generic consumer

    On the receiving side of a broker hop, application code keeps writing plain IIntegrationEventHandler<TEvent> implementations; there is no broker-specific consumer class to author per event. The generic IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> is the single adapter that bridges a broker-delivered TEvent to all the registered in-process handlers (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:10-17,61), registered per event type with RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>() on the IBrokerConsumerBuilder that the configureConsumers callback of AddBrokerMessaging receives (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Broker/IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:6-8,26). Each call adds one subscription that binds the event's topic to the host's single queue, and registering the same event (or the same event name) twice throws (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/BrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:47-62). Because a host has one queue, two services consuming the same event never compete for one queue and there is no per-consumer endpoint configuration; no separate fault consumer is registered either, since a message that exhausts its in-process retries and its redeliveries is dead-lettered by BrokerReceivePipeline, which logs it once at Error and adds one to the broker.fault.count counter (IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:9-14, BrokerReceivePipeline.cs:152-176). Two siblings sit beside it: RegisterUpcastedIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>() drains a retired contract through UpcastingIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> into the handlers of its successor (IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:29-40, ADR-090); the builder picks the closed consumer type from the subscription kind (BrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:36-45), and registering both kinds for one event is exactly the duplicate it rejects (BrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:51-57). And IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions adds RegisterOutputCacheEvictionConsumer(), an extension(IBrokerConsumerBuilder) member that is the named shorthand for the framework's own eviction broadcast, forwarding to RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<OutputCacheEvictionRequested>() (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:13,28-33). A handler that throws is logged with the failing handler's type and rethrown, after its staged inbox row is abandoned, so the framework receive pipeline retries it in a fresh scope (exponential backoff, MessageBusSettings.RetryLimit retries) and then redelivers or dead-letters it (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:113-120), and a message with no registered handler in this process is acked with a log line rather than being retried forever (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:126).

    Because broker delivery is also at-least-once, the consumer guards against duplicates with the inbox (ADR-021), and the shape of that guard is the subtle part. IInboxStore is a three-step contract: TryBeginAsync before the handlers, CompleteAsync after they all succeed, Abandon when one fails (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/IInboxStore.cs:38-63); the older AlreadyProcessedAsync/MarkProcessedAsync pair remains on the interface and supplies the default implementations of the three, so an external implementation keeps working unchanged (IInboxStore.cs:19-22). What TryBeginAsync adds is staging: EfInboxStore does not write the InboxMessage row after the handlers run, it stages it into the same scoped ApplicationDbContext the handlers write through (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/EfInboxStore.cs:52,68,143). A handler's own SaveChangesAsync therefore commits the dedup row in the same transaction as its mutations, closing by construction the window where a crash between "handler committed" and "inbox written" reprocessed the whole event; CompleteAsync saves the staged row only when nothing else already did, which covers events whose handlers write nothing (EfInboxStore.cs:78-102). The consumer calls Abandon on a handler failure so the failed attempt leaves neither a rejected insert on the scope's context nor a row that would make the redelivery look like a duplicate (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:113, EfInboxStore.cs:109). Concurrent duplicate deliveries are absorbed by the unique index on MessageId (ApplicationDbContext.cs:724-726), whichever save hits it. The dedup key is EventNameResolver.GetInboxName(...), so an unannotated event keeps matching the rows it already wrote (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:82). When the inbox is disabled the no-op NoOpInboxStore is registered so behavior is unchanged (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/NoOpInboxStore.cs:7-14, DependencyInjection.cs:790). The outbox is the producer-side idempotency mechanism; the inbox is its consumer-side mirror. Together they make the cross-service event flow effectively-once on top of at-least-once transport ([Rubric §6], [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]).

    Outbound webhooks: the spine reaching outside the system

    Integration events can also leave the system entirely, as signed HTTP POSTs to subscriber URLs (ADR-142). The feature is opt-in twice: a host calls AddWebhooks(configuration) and sets Webhooks:Enabled, which defaults to false (WebhookSettings, MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookSettings.cs:5-17); with it off the fan-out stays inert, every administration endpoint answers 404 and no context maps the WebhookSubscriptions table. Turning it on also takes pipeline.AddWebhookDelivery(), one AddWebhookEvent<TEvent>(name) per published event, the migration on the Webhooks:DataSourceName database, and a controller deriving from WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase granted webhooks:manage (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:23-39).

    WebhookSubscription is the aggregate: a target URL plus the webhook types it lists (or every type), with its signing secret stored as the host's data-protection layer protected it and returned in plaintext only once, at create or rotate. After a rotation the previous secret keeps signing beside the new one until it expires, so a receiver can switch without dropping deliveries (MMCA.Common.Domain/Webhooks/WebhookSubscription.cs:7-20; the grace is PreviousSecretGraceHours, default 24, WebhookSettings.cs:58-60). Its tenant is a plain column the store filters explicitly, not an ITenantEntity (WebhookSubscription.cs:21-24). DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector implements IWebhookSecretProtector under the purpose MMCA.Common.Webhooks.Secret.v1; a multi-replica host needs the shared key ring, and a secret one replica cannot read surfaces as Webhooks.SecretUnreadable, never as an exception (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:9-14). The admin surface, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase, lists, lists the publishable event types, creates, updates, rotates, disables, enables and deletes, every call scoped to the caller's tenant, with refusals such as Webhooks.TargetNotAllowed (400), Webhooks.NotFound (404, also for another tenant's id) and Webhooks.LimitReached (409) (MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:12-37); that limit is MaxSubscriptionsPerTenant, 25 by default and soft under concurrent creates (WebhookSettings.cs:62-67). Behind it sit IWebhookSubscriptionService / WebhookSubscriptionService and the EF store EFWebhookSubscriptionStore. A target is checked at registration by DnsWebhookTargetValidator (the default IWebhookTargetValidator), which applies the outbound address policy and, unless insecure targets are allowed, refuses a host whose DNS answer is empty or contains ANY address that is not public; that is an early answer for the operator, and the connect-time check stays authoritative (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:11-16).

    Delivery rides two pipelines this chapter already described. WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> is an ordinary ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent> subclass registered by AddWebhookEvent<TEvent>(name), so it receives the event through whichever transport the host runs (MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:17-20,35). It returns at once unless webhooks are on and WebhookEventCatalog knows the event's webhook name (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:62-67), lists the enabled subscriptions of the event's tenant that subscribe to that name, skips with one warning an event whose serialized payload exceeds MaxPayloadBytes (65536 by default), because redelivering it cannot make it smaller (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:69-86, WebhookSettings.cs:51-56), and otherwise schedules one DeliverWebhookInternalCommand per matching subscription inside ONE transaction (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:88-115). The WebhookDeliveryId is derived from the event's MessageId and the subscription id, so a redelivered event schedules rows with an unchanged webhook-id; a failed schedule throws so the outbox or the broker redelivers the event (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:28-31,91,117-120). Every delivery is therefore an internal-command row, and its retries and dead-lettering come from the InternalCommands settings rather than a webhook-specific budget (WebhookSettings.cs:5-10).

    Those rows are drained not by the shared internal-command loop but by WebhookDeliveryLane, a dedicated InternalCommandLaneProcessor keyed by subscription, so a slow or failing receiver cannot delay the other internal commands, a failing subscription is cooled down on its own, and two deliveries to one subscription never run at once (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:14-20). The lane sends up to DeliveryConcurrency (default 4) deliveries at once per replica, starts no delivery after TimeoutSeconds (default 10) into a batch, and cools a failed subscription for ReceiverCooldownSeconds (default 60), during which each delivery that comes due spends one attempt (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:29-48, WebhookSettings.cs:34,41,49). It is registered only when both Webhooks:Enabled and InternalCommands:Enabled are true (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:51-64). DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler runs each row: it reads the subscription under the tenant the processor restored for the row, posts the signed envelope, and maps the outcome onto the subscription's last-outcome columns and onto the row. A 2xx, a 410 and a refused target complete the row, because retrying cannot change them; everything else fails it so the processor retries with backoff (MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:12-17,73-77).

    HttpWebhookSender, the default IWebhookSender, sends one POST through the MMCA.Webhooks named client, signed once per readable secret with the current one first, and maps the outcome from the status code or the exception alone, never reading the response body; a target that fails the address policy is refused before anything is sent (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:14-19,33,62). The signature is the Standard Webhooks scheme in WebhookSignature: a whsec_-prefixed secret of 32 random bytes, the webhook-id, webhook-timestamp and webhook-signature headers, and a v1,-prefixed HMAC-SHA256 over {id}.{timestamp}.{body} that a receiver verifies with Verify or any Standard Webhooks library (MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookSignature.cs:8-32). The client's primary handler follows no redirect, uses no proxy and keeps no cookies; every host-default handler (a resilience pipeline, service discovery) is cleared so the internal-command queue is the only retry policy; its default loggers are removed so the target URL, which often carries the receiver's token, is never logged by the client stack; and no traceparent, tracestate or baggage header reaches the receiver (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:41-55). The authoritative guard against server-side request forgery is WebhookConnectCallback: the client's connect callback resolves the host itself (through IWebhookDnsResolver) and, unless insecure targets are allowed, throws WebhookTargetRefusedException before opening a socket when any resolved address is not public, and it never dials an address it did not check, which is what holds the policy against DNS rebinding (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookConnectCallback.cs:7-15,48). The development switch that allows insecure targets is honored only in a Development environment, decided in one place by WebhookInsecureTargets (WebhookInsecureTargets.cs:6-10), and InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService is the startup notice for that switch when it is ignored (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:24-27). This is the [Rubric §17, Security & Compliance] face of the event spine, with its delivery reliability inherited from the internal-command queue ([Rubric §29]).

    Process managers: a long-running process as an aggregate

    The spine also drives multi-step processes (ADR-143). ProcessManagerAggregateRoot<TIdentifierType> is the base for an aggregate that IS the state of a process: its own status columns decide every step, and it declares no property and no field, so deriving from it changes neither the EF model nor any migration snapshot (MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:5-17). Its one notion of time is GetLastTransitionOn(), LastModifiedOn or else CreatedOn, a method rather than a property for that same reason (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:20-31). Its one decision is DecideTimeout, which an override implements by matching the fired ProcessTimeout by name and re-deriving the due instant from stored state, so a stale or re-armed timeout is a no-op by construction (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:33-44). A ProcessTimeout is a named, strictly positive window measured from the last transition (MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeout.cs:3-21), and the answer is a ProcessTimeoutDecision built only through its factories, NotApplicable (the default), NotYetDue(dueAt), Applied, or Rejected(errors) with at least one error, whose outcome is a ProcessTimeoutOutcome (MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:10-54).

    Steps run on domain events through ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase<TDomainEvent, TProcess, TProcessId>, an IDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent> that opens one scope, runs one transaction on its unit of work, loads the correlated process tracked, and hands it with a ProcessStepContext to the subclass's HandleStepAsync (MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:31-71). The step owns its saves: the base never calls SaveChangesAsync, and the transaction refuses to commit unsaved changes, so a step that forgets to save fails loudly (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:17-18). A missing process leaves the event handled (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:59-63); a failed Result rolls the unit back and the default OnStepFailed throws ProcessStepFailedException so the outbox redelivers the event, unless an override returns normally (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:21-25,120-124). The context carries the event's scope, its unit of work, and the timeout scheduler, which is null unless the host called AddProcessManagers() (MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessStepContext.cs:6-33).

    Timeouts are internal commands, not timers. A step arms one through IProcessTimeoutScheduler; the default ProcessTimeoutScheduler refuses a zero or negative delay with ProcessManagers.TimeoutNotInFuture and otherwise writes one internal-command row due at the injected clock's now plus the delay, the same clock the handler later decides with (MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:6-39). The row is a consumer-owned IProcessTimeoutCommand<TProcessId> (MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:5-17), handled by a sealed subclass of ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> that the module scan registers as a closed command handler inside the decorator pipeline (MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:25-28). The handler loads the process tracked, lets it decide against the injected TimeProvider, and saves once, only when the timeout applied (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:49-98). Every outcome completes the row with success except a save failure that is not a concurrency conflict, which propagates so the row retries with backoff; a lost concurrency race means another writer already moved the process (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:13-19,86-94). A NotYetDue decision completes the row without scheduling another, so a step that still wants the timeout arms it again and a host keeps a reconciliation sweep for what no step covers (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:20-24). Each handled command yields a ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId> tagged with a ProcessTimeoutHandling value for an OnHandled override to log (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:113-125). The primitive adds no table and no configuration key (ADR-143); it is testable because every decision runs against an injected clock ([Rubric §14, Testability]) and correct under redelivery because every decision is re-derived from stored state ([Rubric §6]).

    Putting it together, one event's life

    To see the whole spine at once, follow a single integration event from a producer service to a consumer service in broker mode. (1) A command mutates an aggregate, which raises an integration event via AddDomainEvent(...); the interceptor captures it into an OutboxMessage in the same transaction, stores its identity through EventNameResolver, and, because it is an integration event, deliberately does not dispatch it in-process: it only signals the processor. (2) Once the row is eligible (after ProcessingDelaySeconds) and unblocked by any earlier row sharing its OrderingKey, the OutboxProcessor claims it under a lease, deserializes it, sees it is an IIntegrationEvent, and publishes it through IMessageBus behind the broker circuit breaker, which in broker mode is BrokerMessageBus to the selected transport's publisher to the broker, then stamps the row processed and records its delivery lag on OutboxMetrics. (3) In the consumer service, IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> receives it, asks the IInboxStore to begin (which skips the message outright if that MessageId was already handled, and otherwise stages the dedup row), runs every IIntegrationEventHandler<TEvent>, and completes so a redelivery is skipped. (4) Back on the producer, OutboxCleanupService eventually purges the processed row, and anything that dead-lettered stays visible to OutboxAdministration until its own retention window closes. In monolith mode steps 2 and 3 collapse: with the outbox on, the registered IMessageBus is InProcessMessageBus, which hands the event to the same DomainEventDispatcher that local events already flow through, and application code that publishes directly uses InProcessEventBus to write, dispatch, and finalize in one call; with the outbox off (the in-process default) that same call is a straight synchronous dispatch and no row is written at all. The contracts the application code touches never change, which is exactly the property that lets a module graduate to its own service without a rewrite (ADR-008). For the mechanics of why each design choice was made, ADR-003 (outbox and at-least-once), ADR-006 (per-service outbox), ADR-010 (event versioning), ADR-090 (upcaster registration), ADR-021 (consumer inbox), and ADR-007 with ADR-008 (transport at the edge) are the primary references.

    ProcessTimeoutHandling

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandling.cs:4 · Level 0 · enum

    • What it is: the six outcomes ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> can reach for one fired timeout: ProcessMissing, NotApplicable, NotYetDue, Applied, Rejected, LostRace.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: an application-layer widening of the aggregate's own verdict. The domain's ProcessTimeoutOutcome only knows what the aggregate decided; this enum adds the two outcomes only the handler can observe (no process row at all, and a save that lost a concurrency race). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses whether a system tells an operator what it did; every handled timeout ends in exactly one of these values, handed to the OnHandled logging hook inside a ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId>.
    • Walkthrough: explicitly numbered members (ProcessTimeoutHandling.cs:7,10,13,16,19,22). Only Applied = 3 means a save happened (ProcessTimeoutHandling.cs:16); every other member's doc comment records that nothing was saved, and Rejected adds that the row is not retried (ProcessTimeoutHandling.cs:18-19).
    • Why it's built this way: none of the six outcomes fails the internal-command row. The handler returns Result.Success() for all of them (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:63,83,93,97) because each is decided by stored state, so a retry could not change it (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:13-18). The enum is therefore a reporting vocabulary, not a retry signal. Recorded in ADR-143.
    • Where it's used: mapped from the domain outcome in the handler's switch (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:69-76) and carried as the first positional member of ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId> (ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:17).

    WebhookErrorCodes

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookErrorCodes.cs:4 · Level 0 · class (static)

    • What it is: the eleven stable Webhooks.* error codes the outbound-webhook feature returns inside an Error.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party (string constants only).
    • Concept: a per-feature error-code catalog, the same convention the Result pattern uses everywhere (see Error). [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] assesses whether clients get stable, machine-readable failures; each constant's doc comment pins both the error type and the HTTP status it surfaces as, so the code is the contract a subscriber-management client can switch on.
    • Walkthrough: nine codes surface to API callers: NotFound (404, WebhookErrorCodes.cs:7); the validation set UrlInvalid, TargetNotAllowed, EventTypesRequired, TooManyEventTypes (more than 20), EventTypeUnknown, DescriptionTooLong (over 200 characters), SecretRequired (400, WebhookErrorCodes.cs:10,13,16,19,22,25,28); and LimitReached (409, WebhookErrorCodes.cs:31). Two are queue-only failures that never reach a caller: DeliveryFailed (WebhookErrorCodes.cs:34), which makes the internal-command processor retry, and SecretUnreadable (WebhookErrorCodes.cs:37), raised when the stored signing secret cannot be unprotected on the current replica.
    • Why it's built this way: one class keeps the code strings in one place for the Application service, the Infrastructure validators and the delivery handler, which live in different assemblies. See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: WebhookSubscriptionService (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:70,192,225,240), DnsWebhookTargetValidator (DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:58), DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector (DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:54), WebhookSignature (WebhookSignature.cs:128) and DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:47,118,122).

    WebhookEventRegistration

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookEventRegistration.cs:6 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: one positional record pairing an integration-event CLR type with the webhook event type name subscribers list (for example ticket.opened, WebhookEventRegistration.cs:3-5).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; System.Type.
    • Concept: a DI-registered descriptor. Instead of a mutable static registry, each AddWebhookEvent<TEvent>(eventType) call adds one singleton instance of this record (DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:79), and WebhookEventCatalog receives the whole set through IEnumerable<WebhookEventRegistration>. See ADR-142.
    • Walkthrough: two members, EventClrType and EventType (WebhookEventRegistration.cs:6).
    • Why it's built this way: registrations live in the service collection, so AddWebhookEvent can inspect the earlier ones before adding another: the same type under the same name is a no-op, and a name reused for a different type, or a type registered under a second name, throws InvalidOperationException (DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:52-77).
    • Where it's used: written by AddWebhookEvent (DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:79) and read by the WebhookEventCatalog constructor (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:20).

    WebhookSettings

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookSettings.cs:11 · Level 0 · class

    • What it is: the outbound-webhook options bound from the Webhooks configuration section; nothing is delivered and the WebhookSubscriptions table is not mapped until Enabled is true (WebhookSettings.cs:5-10).

    • Depends on: nothing first-party; System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations for [Range].

    • Concept: fail-fast options. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses whether one bad dependency can degrade the rest; here the knobs that bound a slow receiver (timeout, per-replica concurrency, cooldown) are explicit, range-checked and defaulted low. Infrastructure binds the section with ValidateDataAnnotations() (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:71-73), so an out-of-range value fails startup (ADR-070).

    • Walkthrough:

      Member Line Default Range / note
      SectionName WebhookSettings.cs:14 "Webhooks" const
      Enabled WebhookSettings.cs:17 false master switch
      DataSourceName WebhookSettings.cs:23 "Default" database holding WebhookSubscriptions
      TimeoutSeconds WebhookSettings.cs:34 10 1 to 30 (:33); also the HttpClient timeout (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:88)
      DeliveryConcurrency WebhookSettings.cs:41 4 1 to 16 (:40); never two deliveries to one subscription at once
      ReceiverCooldownSeconds WebhookSettings.cs:49 60 5 to 120 (:48); a cooling subscription's due deliveries each spend one attempt
      MaxPayloadBytes WebhookSettings.cs:56 65536 1024 to 1048576 (:55); a larger event is skipped with a warning
      PreviousSecretGraceHours WebhookSettings.cs:60 24 0 to 168 (:59); rotated-out secret keeps signing
      MaxSubscriptionsPerTenant WebhookSettings.cs:67 25 1 to 500 (:66); soft limit, concurrent creates can exceed it
      AllowInsecureTargetsInDevelopment WebhookSettings.cs:73 false honoured only in Development, else one startup warning

    • Why it's built this way: retries are deliberately absent. Each delivery is one internal-command row, so retry counts come from the InternalCommands:* settings (WebhookSettings.cs:8-9); this class only owns what is webhook-specific. The TimeoutSeconds remarks explain why the timeout is kept low: one slow receiver holds one lane slot for up to that many seconds per attempt (WebhookSettings.cs:26-32). See ADR-142.

    • Where it's used: WebhookDeliveryLane (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:43,47), WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> for the payload cap (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:82), WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions, WebhookInsecureTargets, and the startup check WebhookRegistrationValidator.

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookEventCatalog.cs:10 · Level 1 · class (sealed partial)

    • What it is: the host's two-way map between webhook event type names and integration-event CLR types, built from every registered WebhookEventRegistration; empty when nothing is registered (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:6-9).
    • Depends on: WebhookEventRegistration; BCL GeneratedRegex, NotNullWhen.

    • Walkthrough:
      • Two dictionaries, _byName (ordinal comparer) and _byType (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:15-16,24-25). The constructor fills both with TryAdd (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:26-30), so a duplicate is ignored here; AddWebhookEvent already rejected conflicting ones.
      • EventTypes is the ordinal-sorted name list (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:32,36).
      • Contains(eventType) is null-safe (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:41); TryGetEventType(Type, out string?) maps the other way (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:47).
      • IsValidEventTypeName (internal) accepts 1 to MaxEventTypeLength = 100 characters (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:13,56-57) matching ^[a-z][a-z0-9]*([._-][a-z0-9]+)*$ with a 1000 ms regex timeout (WebhookEventCatalog.cs:59-60).

See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: WebhookSubscriptionService exposes EventTypes and checks Contains (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:37,234); WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> resolves the published name with TryGetEventType (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:64); Infrastructure also registers it (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:82).

    ProcessStepFailedException

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessStepFailedException.cs:10 · Level 2 · class

    • What it is: the exception the default OnStepFailed of ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase<TDomainEvent, TProcess, TProcessId> throws when a step returned a failed result, so the outbox redelivers the event (ProcessStepFailedException.cs:5-9).
    • Depends on: Error; BCL Exception.
    • Concept: the one deliberate bridge from Result back to an exception. Domain-event handlers return Task, not Result, so the only way for a handler to tell the outbox "this did not work, try again" is to throw. See the outbox at ADR-003 and ADR-143.
    • Walkthrough: the three standard exception constructors (ProcessStepFailedException.cs:13,19,27) plus a process constructor (ProcessStepFailedException.cs:36) that sets ProcessType, ProcessId and Errors (ProcessStepFailedException.cs:45,48,51, Errors falls back to empty at line 41). BuildMessage joins the error codes, or says no error code (ProcessStepFailedException.cs:53-59).
    • Why it's built this way: the message carries codes only, never error descriptions, so a log line names the failure without copying step detail. ProcessId is a string formatted with the invariant culture by the caller (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:122), which keeps the exception non-generic.
    • Where it's used: thrown only by ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.OnStepFailed (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:120-124).

    ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:16 · Level 2 · record

    • What it is: what ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> did with one fired timeout, handed to its OnHandled hook for logging (ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:5-8).
    • Depends on: ProcessTimeoutHandling, Error.
    • Concept: see ProcessTimeoutHandling. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]: the record gives a consumer one structured value to log per timeout instead of a set of ad-hoc overrides.
    • Walkthrough: six positional members (ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:16-22): Handling, ProcessId, TimeoutName, DueAt (filled for NotYetDue only), Errors (filled for Rejected only) and StateBefore (the state described before the aggregate decided, ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:13-15). TProcessId : notnull (ProcessTimeoutReport.cs:23).
    • Why it's built this way: the handler builds the decision-bearing report directly from the aggregate's decision (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:82) and every other one through a private helper that leaves DueAt null and Errors empty (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:120-125).
