    IProcessTimeoutCommand<TProcessId>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:17 · Level 4 · interface

    • What it is: the contract for a consumer-owned internal command that fires a named timeout on one process aggregate (IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:5-9).
    • Depends on: IInternalCommand; InternalCommandNameAttribute by convention.
    • Concept: a process timeout is not a new mechanism; it is an ordinary durable internal command (ADR-114) with a due time. This interface only adds the correlation (ProcessId) the generic handler needs. The doc comment asks every implementation for a stable [InternalCommandName] and a handler derived from ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> (IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:6-8), and the convention test base ProcessTimeoutConventionTestsBase in MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture checks it ([Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]).
    • Walkthrough: one member, ProcessId (IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:21). It may be an explicit interface implementation over the command's own id property; System.Text.Json does not serialize explicit implementations, so adopting the interface does not change what stored rows carry (IProcessTimeoutCommand.cs:10-15).
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the payload shape stable lets an existing internal command become a process timeout without a data migration. See ADR-143.
    • Where it's used: the parameter type of IProcessTimeoutScheduler.ArmAsync and the TCommand constraint of the handler base (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:41).

    IProcessTimeoutScheduler

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/IProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:9 · Level 5 · interface

    • What it is: the one service of the process-manager primitive: arm a timeout command to run after a delay.
    • Depends on: IProcessTimeoutCommand<TProcessId>, Result.
    • Concept: see IProcessTimeoutCommand<TProcessId>. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses whether long-running workflows are modelled explicitly; a step arms its next deadline through this interface instead of a timer, so the deadline survives a restart as a stored row.
    • Walkthrough: ArmAsync<TProcessId>(timeout, after, cancellationToken) returns Task<Result<Guid>> (IProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:24-28): the scheduled row id, a ProcessManagers.TimeoutNotInFuture validation failure for a zero or negative delay, or the internal command scheduler's own failure (IProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:20-23). The write inherits that scheduler's atomicity: enrolled in the active transaction when there is one, saved outright otherwise (IProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:11-15).
    • Why it's built this way: arming inside the step's transaction means the state change and its deadline commit or roll back together. See ADR-143 and the earlier deferral in ADR-086.
    • Where it's used: registered scoped by AddProcessManagers() (DependencyInjection.ProcessManagers.cs:26) and exposed to steps as ProcessStepContext.Timeouts.

    ProcessTimeoutScheduler

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:13 · Level 6 · class (sealed)

    • What it is: the IProcessTimeoutScheduler implementation: a guard plus one call to the internal command scheduler.
    • Depends on: IInternalCommandScheduler, Error; BCL TimeProvider.
    • Concept: see IProcessTimeoutScheduler. [Rubric §14, Testability]: the clock is the injected TimeProvider (ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:15), so a test fixes "now" and checks the due time exactly.
    • Walkthrough: TimeoutNotInFutureCode = "ProcessManagers.TimeoutNotInFuture" (ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:18). ArmAsync (ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:21) null-checks the command (:27), returns a validation failure for after <= TimeSpan.Zero (ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:29-35), and otherwise calls ScheduleAsync(timeout, timeProvider.GetUtcNow() + after, cancellationToken) (ProcessTimeoutScheduler.cs:38).
    • Why it's built this way: no state of its own, so transactional behavior is exactly the internal command scheduler's (ADR-114). AddProcessManagers() also adds TimeProvider.System with TryAddSingleton, so a host's own clock wins (DependencyInjection.ProcessManagers.cs:26-27).
    • Where it's used: resolved through ProcessStepContext by process steps.

    WebhookRegistrationValidator

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:13 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: an IValidateOptions<WebhookSettings> that fails startup when AddWebhookDelivery() was called but no IWebhookSubscriptionStore is registered (WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:7-11).
    • Depends on: WebhookSettings, IWebhookSubscriptionStore; IServiceProviderIsService, IValidateOptions<T>.
    • Concept: a wiring check expressed as options validation. [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]: the Application layer cannot reference the Infrastructure store, so it asks the container whether one exists and turns a per-row runtime failure into one startup error (ADR-070).
    • Walkthrough: Validate (WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:17) returns Success when IsService(typeof(IWebhookSubscriptionStore)) (:18-19), else a failure naming services.AddWebhooks(configuration) from MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks (WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:20-22).
    • Why it's built this way: it ignores Enabled on purpose: a missing store is a mistake whatever the switch says (WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:9-10). ValidateOnStart() makes it run at host start (DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:98-99).
    • Where it's used: registered only by AddWebhookDelivery() (DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:99).

    ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:36 · Level 8 · class (abstract)

    • What it is: the generic handler for a fired process timeout: load the process tracked, let the aggregate decide against the injected clock, save once when it applied (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:8-11).
    • Depends on: ICommandHandler<in TCommand, TResult>, IUnitOfWork, IConcurrencyConflictDetector, ProcessManagerAggregateRoot<TIdentifierType>, ProcessTimeout, ProcessTimeoutOutcome, ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId>; BCL TimeProvider.
    • Concept introduced, the process-manager timeout. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses whether long-running workflows have explicit state and deadlines. The aggregate owns the rule (DecideTimeout, ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:67); this base owns only loading, saving and classifying. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: every outcome decided by stored state completes the row with success, and only a save failure that is not a concurrency conflict propagates so the row retries with backoff (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:13-18).
    • Walkthrough:
      • Includes defaults to none (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:46).
      • HandleAsync (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:49) resolves the timeout first (:53), loads with asTracking: true (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:56-58), and reports ProcessMissing when the row is gone (:60-64).
      • It captures DescribeState before deciding (:66-67), maps the outcome with a switch whose default arm is NotApplicable (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:69-76), and for anything but Applied reports without saving (:78-84).
      • On Applied it calls SaveChangesAsync once (:88); a concurrency conflict becomes LostRace (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:90-94), otherwise Applied (:96).
      • Extension points: abstract ResolveTimeout (:103), virtual DescribeState returning null (:111), virtual no-op OnHandled (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:116).
    • Why it's built this way: a NotYetDue decision schedules nothing new, so each step that still wants the timeout re-arms it and the host keeps a reconciliation sweep for the rest (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:20-23; compensation and reconciliation in ADR-054). One sealed subclass per command lets the module scan register a closed ICommandHandler<TCommand, Result> the decorator pipeline wraps (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:24-27). See ADR-143.
    • Where it's used: consumer subclasses found by the module scan (DependencyInjection.ProcessManagers.cs:19-22); the architecture rules in ArchitectureRules.ProcessTimeouts.cs check the convention.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: no subclass exists in MMCA.ADC/Source today; the in-repo subclasses are test fixtures. Which consumer, if any, derives it is not determinable from the Common and ADC sources read here.

    ProcessStepContext

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessStepContext.cs:11 · Level 11 · class (sealed)

    • What it is: the bundle a process step receives: the event's scope provider, its unit of work, and the timeout scheduler when registered.
    • Depends on: IUnitOfWork, IProcessTimeoutScheduler; BCL IServiceProvider.
    • Concept: a parameter object. See ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase<TDomainEvent, TProcess, TProcessId> for the step model.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor null-checks both arguments (ProcessStepContext.cs:16-19) and resolves the scheduler with GetService, not GetRequiredService (ProcessStepContext.cs:23). Services, UnitOfWork (the step owns every save) and nullable Timeouts (ProcessStepContext.cs:27,30,33).
    • Why it's built this way: Timeouts is null when the host never called AddProcessManagers() (ProcessStepContext.cs:32), so a process that needs no deadlines works without that registration. Passing one object keeps the abstract HandleStepAsync signature stable if the context grows.
    • Where it's used: constructed per step inside the transaction (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:68).

    ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase<TDomainEvent, TProcess, TProcessId>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:31 · Level 12 · class (abstract)

    • What it is: runs one process step per domain event: one scope, one transaction, the correlated process loaded tracked, then the consumer's HandleStepAsync (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:11-14).
    • Depends on: IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent>, IDomainEvent, ProcessManagerAggregateRoot<TIdentifierType>, IUnitOfWork, ProcessStepContext, ProcessStepFailedException; IServiceScopeFactory.
    • Concept: the event-driven half of the process manager (the timeout half is ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId>). [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven]: a domain event moves a stored process aggregate one step, inside its own transaction (ADR-107). [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]: a failed step throws by default, so the outbox redelivers the event (ADR-003).
    • Walkthrough:
      • HandleAsync (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:41) calls OnStepStarting before any scope (:45), opens an async scope (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:47-48) and resolves that scope's IUnitOfWork (:50).
      • Inside ExecuteInTransactionAsync (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:52) it loads by CorrelateProcessId with asTracking: true (:55-57); a missing process calls OnProcessMissing and succeeds (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:59-63); otherwise it returns HandleStepAsync with a new ProcessStepContext (:65-69).
      • A failed result calls OnStepFailed after the rollback (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:73-76), whose default throws ProcessStepFailedException with the process type name and the id formatted invariantly (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:120-124).
      • Extension points: abstract CorrelateProcessId and HandleStepAsync (:83,94), virtual Includes (:38), OnStepStarting, OnProcessMissing (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:102,108) and OnStepFailed.
    • Why it's built this way: the base never saves; the transaction refuses to commit unsaved changes, so a step that forgets to save fails loudly (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:16-18). A step's own exception propagates unchanged and cancellation is never caught (ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:20-24). An override of OnStepFailed that returns normally keeps the event handled. See ADR-143, ADR-054 and ADR-086.
    • Where it's used: consumer subclasses found by the module scan (DependencyInjection.ProcessManagers.cs:19-22); AddApplication() registers none of this (:22).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: no subclass exists in MMCA.ADC/Source today; the in-repo subclasses are test fixtures in ProcessDomainEventHandlerBaseTests.cs.

    IDomainEvent

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.Interfaces · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/Interfaces/IDomainEvent.cs:7 · Level 0 · interface

    • What it is: the marker contract for domain events: something meaningful happened inside an aggregate boundary, to be dispatched after successful persistence.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party (BCL DateTime/Guid only).
    • Concept introduced, domain events + idempotency keys. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (assesses reliable events, idempotent consumers, and events carrying enough context) and [Rubric §4, DDD] (aggregates raise events on state change). An aggregate does not call other modules directly; it records that something happened (for example "SessionScored") as an IDomainEvent, and the framework dispatches it after the data is safely saved, the basis of the Outbox pattern (ADR-003).
    • Walkthrough: two properties, and the whole file is 14 lines. DateOccurred (IDomainEvent.cs:10) is when the business action happened, not when it was dispatched, and the XML doc says exactly that. MessageId (a Guid, IDomainEvent.cs:13) is a unique per-instance id used for consumer-side idempotency (inbox dedup), so a redelivered event is processed once. That MessageId is what makes consumers safe under at-least-once delivery: it is minted at event creation by BaseDomainEvent (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:35, alongside the DateOccurred default on line 28), survives outbox serialization inside the JSON payload, travels through the broker, and lands in an InboxMessage as the dedup key (ADR-021).
    • Why it's built this way: a minimal marker keeps the domain free of dispatch mechanics, and the two members are exactly what the outbox/inbox machinery needs (ordering and eligibility by occurrence time, dedup by id). Note what is not here: no Version, no routing key, no transport metadata. Those live one layer out, on OutboxMessage and on BaseIntegrationEvent.
    • Where it's used: implemented by concrete domain events in each module; raised by AuditableAggregateRootEntity<TIdentifierType> (G02), captured from aggregates during SaveChangesAsync by DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor (G07), serialized into an OutboxMessage by OutboxMessage.FromDomainEvent (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxMessage.cs:110), and dispatched by IDomainEventDispatcher or the OutboxProcessor.

    ProcessTimeout

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeout.cs:7 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: a named time window measured from a process's last transition. A process aggregate matches a fired timeout by its Name and computes when it is due with DueAfter (ProcessTimeout.cs:3-6).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party (BCL TimeSpan/DateTime and the ArgumentException guards).
    • Concept introduced, the timeout half of a process manager. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (assesses long-running workflows coordinated through events and commands rather than call chains): a process manager advances on events, but it also has to advance when nothing happens (an unanswered request, an expired hold). This record is the vocabulary for that "nothing happened in time" step; the aggregate that interprets it is ProcessManagerAggregateRoot<TIdentifierType> (ADR-143).
    • Walkthrough: a sealed record with an explicit constructor (ProcessTimeout.cs:14) that rejects a blank name (ProcessTimeout.cs:16) and a zero or negative window (ProcessTimeout.cs:17), so an invalid timeout cannot exist. Name (ProcessTimeout.cs:24) and Window (ProcessTimeout.cs:27) are get-only. DueAfter(lastTransitionOn) (ProcessTimeout.cs:32) is just lastTransitionOn + Window: the due instant is always derived, never stored.
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the due instant a pure function of the stored last-transition time is what lets a stale or re-armed timeout become a no-op (the aggregate re-derives it and answers ProcessTimeoutDecision NotYetDue); the record carries no process id and no state, so it is safe to construct anywhere.
    • Where it's used: passed to ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.DecideTimeout (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:43); produced by the abstract ResolveTimeout(TCommand) of ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:103), whose Name is copied into the ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId> (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:82).

    ProcessTimeoutOutcome

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:4 · Level 0 · enum

    • What it is: what a process aggregate decided about a fired timeout (ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:3).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: the result vocabulary of the process-manager timeout step introduced at ProcessTimeout; [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] applies the same way.
    • Walkthrough: four explicitly numbered members. NotApplicable = 0 (ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:7) is the default: the timeout does not apply to the process in its current state or by its name. NotYetDue = 1 (ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:10): it applies, but a stale or re-armed row fired early. Applied = 2 (ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:13): the aggregate transitioned. Rejected = 3 (ProcessTimeoutOutcome.cs:16): the aggregate refused the transition.
    • Why it's built this way: making the no-op the zero value means an aggregate that never overrides DecideTimeout is safe by default (the base returns NotApplicable, MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:44).
    • Where it's used: carried by ProcessTimeoutDecision; mapped one-to-one onto ProcessTimeoutHandling by the timeout handler base (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:71-74).

    WebhookDeliveryId

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookDeliveryId.cs:9 · Level 0 · class (static)

    • What it is: computes the deterministic webhook-id of one (event, subscription) pair, so a redelivered event schedules the same id and a receiver can dedupe on it (WebhookDeliveryId.cs:5-8).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; BCL System.Security.Cryptography.SHA256.
    • Concept introduced, outbound webhooks and receiver-side idempotency. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] (assesses at-least-once delivery paired with an idempotency key the other side can use): MMCA.Common can push integration events to tenant-registered HTTP endpoints (ADR-142). Delivery is at-least-once, so every attempt for the same event and subscription must carry the same id; this type is the inbound IDomainEvent MessageId idea turned outward.
    • Walkthrough: one method, For(eventId, subscriptionId) (WebhookDeliveryId.cs:18). It writes the two GUIDs into a 32-byte stack buffer (WebhookDeliveryId.cs:20-22), hashes it with SHA-256 into a second stack buffer (WebhookDeliveryId.cs:24-25), and returns the first 16 hash bytes as a Guid (WebhookDeliveryId.cs:26). No allocation beyond the result.
    • Why it's built this way: a hash of the pair is stable across retries, replicas and redeliveries without storing anything, and it does not expose the raw event id to the receiver.
    • Where it's used: WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> stamps it into each DeliverWebhookInternalCommand (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:91), and DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler sends it as the webhook-id header value (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:62).

    WebhookDeliveryRequest

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookDeliveryRequest.cs:8 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: the input to IWebhookSender: what one POST sends (WebhookDeliveryRequest.cs:3).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party at the type level (BCL Uri, IReadOnlyList<string>).
    • Concept: part of the outbound webhook delivery path introduced at WebhookDeliveryId.
    • Walkthrough: four positional members (WebhookDeliveryRequest.cs:4-8): Target (the subscription URL), WebhookId (the webhook-id header value), Body (the WebhookEnvelope JSON, sent verbatim), and ProtectedSecrets (the signing secrets still protected, current first; one signature is sent per readable secret).
    • Why it's built this way: carrying the secrets protected keeps the plaintext key out of the Application layer; only the sender unprotects them (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:123). [Rubric §11, Security] (assesses secret handling) applies to that choice.
    • Where it's used: built by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:60-64) and consumed by HttpWebhookSender.

    WebhookEnvelope

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookEnvelope.cs:11 · Level 0 · class (static)

    • What it is: builds the delivery body in the Standard Webhooks payload shape {"type":...,"timestamp":...,"data":...}, with the event payload carried verbatim (WebhookEnvelope.cs:7-10).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; BCL StringBuilder, System.Text.Json.JsonSerializer.
    • Concept: [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] (assesses stable, documented external contracts): the receiver-facing body follows a published shape instead of an MMCA-specific one, so any Standard Webhooks consumer can parse it.
    • Walkthrough: one method, Build(eventType, occurredOn, payloadJson) (WebhookEnvelope.cs:18). It rejects a blank type or payload (WebhookEnvelope.cs:20-21), normalizes occurredOn to UTC (Local is converted, Unspecified is relabelled as UTC, WebhookEnvelope.cs:23-29), then concatenates with a pre-sized StringBuilder (WebhookEnvelope.cs:31): type is JSON-escaped via JsonSerializer.Serialize (WebhookEnvelope.cs:32-33), timestamp is the round-trip "O" format in the invariant culture (WebhookEnvelope.cs:34-35), and data is the payload string appended without re-serialization (WebhookEnvelope.cs:36-37).
    • Why it's built this way: appending the payload verbatim avoids a parse-and-reserialize round trip and keeps the signed body byte-identical to what the fan-out serialized.
    • Where it's used: called by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:63).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: payloadJson is not validated as JSON here; the method trusts its caller (the fan-out's JsonSerializer.Serialize, WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:80).

    WebhookSendStatus

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookSendStatus.cs:4 · Level 0 · enum

    • What it is: how one webhook POST ended, classified by what the delivery handler should do next.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] (assesses retry with a clear line between transient and permanent failure): every member's doc comment states whether it is retried.
    • Walkthrough: six explicitly numbered members. Delivered = 0 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:7): 2xx. Rejected = 1 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:10): any other status, redirects included; retried. Gone = 2 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:13): 410, the subscription is disabled. TargetRefused = 3 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:19): a non-public address at connect time or a scheme the host does not accept; retrying cannot change it. TransportFailed = 4 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:22): transport failure, timeouts included; retried. SecretUnreadable = 5 (WebhookSendStatus.cs:25): the current signing secret could not be unprotected, nothing was sent; retried.
    • Why it's built this way: the sender classifies, the handler decides; keeping the classification in one enum lets DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler map each status to complete-or-retry in one place.
    • Where it's used: produced by HttpWebhookSender (for example 2xx versus other at MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:109, 410 at HttpWebhookSender.cs:106); carried by WebhookSendOutcome.

    IIntegrationEvent

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.Interfaces · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/Interfaces/IIntegrationEvent.cs:15 · Level 1 · interface

    • What it is: a marker interface distinguishing integration events (cross-module or cross-service) from plain domain events (intra-module).
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent (Level 0), which it extends.
    • Concept introduced, domain versus integration events. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (reliable events across module boundaries) and [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness] (loose coupling via events). The doc comment draws the line (IIntegrationEvent.cs:8-13): domain events are intra-module, raised and handled within the same bounded context by IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent>; integration events are facts that other modules, possibly in other processes, need to react to, handled by IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> and transported through IMessageBus. Because it still extends IDomainEvent, an integration event flows through the same outbox pipeline (at-least-once); the marker only tells the OutboxProcessor to route it through the message bus rather than the in-process dispatcher. The interface is empty (public interface IIntegrationEvent : IDomainEvent;, IIntegrationEvent.cs:15): membership itself is the marker.
    • Why it's built this way: making integration events a subtype of domain events means one outbox mechanism serves both, and the routing decision is a single is IIntegrationEvent pattern match in the processor (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:812), with no parallel capture pipeline to keep in step.
    • Where it's used: implemented by integration events across modules and by BaseIntegrationEvent, which adds the SchemaVersion convention; routed by the OutboxProcessor to IMessageBus (OutboxProcessor.cs:812); published by InProcessEventBus and BrokerEventBus; consumed via IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>, whose type parameter is constrained to class, IIntegrationEvent (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:45).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    WebhookSendOutcome

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookSendOutcome.cs:7 · Level 1 · record

    • What it is: the result of one webhook POST (WebhookSendOutcome.cs:3).
    • Depends on: WebhookSendStatus (Level 0).
    • Concept: see WebhookSendStatus for the retry classification.
    • Walkthrough: three positional members (WebhookSendOutcome.cs:4-7): Status, HttpStatusCode (int?, set only when the receiver answered) and Detail (string?, the failure detail in the form "ExceptionType: message" when there is one).
    • Why it's built this way: a plain value instead of an exception lets the handler record transport failures and HTTP rejections on the subscription the same way.
    • Where it's used: returned by IWebhookSender (IWebhookSender.cs:10); read by ApplyOutcome in DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:79), which turns HttpStatusCode into an HTTP {code} reason (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:127-130) and falls back to Detail, then to the status name (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:142-143).

    IWebhookSender

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/IWebhookSender.cs:4 · Level 2 · interface

    • What it is: posts one signed webhook delivery (IWebhookSender.cs:3).
    • Depends on: WebhookDeliveryRequest, WebhookSendOutcome.
    • Concept: [Rubric §3, Clean Architecture] (assesses that the Application layer owns abstractions and Infrastructure owns I/O): the delivery handler in Application depends on this port, and the HttpClient code lives behind it in Infrastructure.

    ProcessTimeoutDecision

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:10 · Level 2 · record

    • What it is: the decision a process aggregate returns from DecideTimeout. Only NotYetDue carries DueAt and only Rejected carries Errors (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:5-9).
    • Depends on: ProcessTimeoutOutcome (Level 0) and Error (G01).
    • Concept: the process-manager timeout step is introduced at ProcessTimeout. The shape here is a closed set of named factories over a private constructor, the same idea as Result: invalid combinations cannot be built.
    • Walkthrough: the constructor is private (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:12). Three get-only properties: Outcome (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:20), DueAt (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:23) and Errors (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:26). Four factories: NotApplicable() (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:30), NotYetDue(dueAt) (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:35), Applied() (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:39) and Rejected(errors) (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:45), which throws when the list is empty (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:50) and stores a copy of it (ProcessTimeoutDecision.cs:53).
    • Why it's built this way: a rejected decision with no reason would be unreportable, so the guard makes it impossible; the defensive copy keeps the decision immutable after the aggregate returns it.
    • Where it's used: returned by ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.DecideTimeout (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:43); read by ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:67), which copies DueAt and Errors into the ProcessTimeoutReport<TProcessId> (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:82).

    WebhookSignature

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookSignature.cs:13 · Level 3 · class (static)

    • What it is: the Standard Webhooks signing scheme: a whsec_-prefixed Base64 secret, and a v1,-prefixed Base64 HMAC-SHA256 over {id}.{timestamp}.{body}; receivers verify with Verify or any Standard Webhooks library (WebhookSignature.cs:8-12).
    • Depends on: Result and Error (G01), WebhookErrorCodes; BCL HMACSHA256, RandomNumberGenerator, CryptographicOperations.
    • Concept introduced, signed outbound webhooks. [Rubric §11, Security] (assesses authenticity, replay protection and constant-time comparison of secrets): a receiver can only trust a webhook it can authenticate, so every delivery is signed with a per-subscription key and stamped with a send time the receiver checks for freshness. Following a published scheme (ADR-142) means the receiver does not need MMCA code to verify.
    • Walkthrough: constants first: SecretPrefix = "whsec_" (WebhookSignature.cs:16) and the three header names webhook-id, webhook-timestamp, webhook-signature (WebhookSignature.cs:19,22,25), plus the private "v1," version prefix and a 32-byte key size (WebhookSignature.cs:27-28). GenerateSecret() (WebhookSignature.cs:32) is the prefix plus Base64 of 32 random bytes. DecodeSecret(secret) (WebhookSignature.cs:38) returns a Result<byte[]>: a missing prefix or an empty or invalid Base64 tail (WebhookSignature.cs:40,47) is a Webhooks.SecretUnreadable failure (WebhookSignature.cs:126-130), never an exception. Compute(webhookId, timestamp, body, key) (WebhookSignature.cs:61) UTF-8 encodes {id}.{timestamp}.{body} in the invariant culture (WebhookSignature.cs:66-67) and returns v1, plus the Base64 HMAC (WebhookSignature.cs:68). Verify(...) (WebhookSignature.cs:84) rejects null or blank inputs (WebhookSignature.cs:93), rejects a timestamp outside the tolerance (WebhookSignature.cs:98), then accepts if any space-separated v1, entry matches the recomputed signature under CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals (WebhookSignature.cs:103-107). The private IsWithinTolerance (WebhookSignature.cs:114) saturates both bounds at the long range so a hostile timestamp cannot overflow the comparison (WebhookSignature.cs:121-122).
    • Why it's built this way: a space-separated list of signatures lets a rotated subscription send one signature per still-valid secret, so a receiver keeps verifying through a rotation; constant-time comparison and the saturated window close timing and overflow attacks on the receiver side.
    • Where it's used: HttpWebhookSender joins one Compute per readable key into the signature header (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:62,70); WebhookSubscriptionService mints secrets with GenerateSecret (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:81,143). Verify has no production caller in MMCA.Common; it is exposed for receivers and exercised by WebhookSignatureTests.

    DeliverWebhookInternalCommand

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommand.cs:17 · Level 4 · record

    • What it is: one webhook delivery, as a durable internal-command row the fan-out schedules per matching subscription. The row is the delivery history (attempts, last error, dead letter, requeue) (DeliverWebhookInternalCommand.cs:5-9).
    • Depends on: IInternalCommand (G14) and InternalCommandNameAttribute (G14).
    • Concept: [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: rather than a bespoke delivery table, each attempt rides the internal-command lane, so retry with backoff, dead-lettering and requeue come from the existing InternalCommandProcessor (ADR-142).
    • Walkthrough: six positional members (DeliverWebhookInternalCommand.cs:18-23): SubscriptionId, DeliveryId (from WebhookDeliveryId), EventType, EventId (the event's MessageId), OccurredOn (its DateOccurred) and PayloadJson (the event serialized by its runtime type with web defaults). The stable storage name is pinned by [InternalCommandName("MMCA.Webhooks.Deliver.v1")] (DeliverWebhookInternalCommand.cs:16), so a rename of the CLR type cannot orphan queued rows.
    • Why it's built this way: the payload is captured at fan-out time, so a delivery replays exactly what was scheduled even if the event type later changes shape.
    • Where it's used: created by WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:88-96); handled by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler, registered at MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:97; its rows are drained by WebhookDeliveryLane, which resolves the storage name and filters on the type (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:27,44).

    ProcessManagerAggregateRoot<TIdentifierType>

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.ProcessManagers · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Domain/ProcessManagers/ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:17 · Level 5 · class (abstract)

    • What it is: the base class for an aggregate that is the state of a long-running process (a process manager, or saga): its own status columns decide every step, and named timeouts are applied by re-deriving the due instant from what is stored (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:5-8).
    • Depends on: AuditableAggregateRootEntity<TIdentifierType> (G02), ProcessTimeout, ProcessTimeoutDecision.
    • Concept introduced, the process manager as an aggregate. [Rubric §4, DDD] (assesses whether aggregates own their invariants) and [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (assesses coordination of multi-step workflows): instead of a separate saga-state row kept in step with the business aggregate, the business aggregate itself is the process state. Event-driven steps run through ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase<TDomainEvent, TProcess, TProcessId> and timeouts through ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase<TCommand, TProcess, TProcessId> (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:9-14; ADR-143; see also ADR-054 and ADR-086).
    • Walkthrough: GetLastTransitionOn() (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:29) returns LastModifiedOn ?? CreatedOn through a private static helper (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:31). DecideTimeout(timeout, utcNow) (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:43) is virtual and returns NotApplicable by default (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:44). Its doc comment (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:33-42) fixes the override recipe: check the timeout's name and the stored state, return NotYetDue when timeout.DueAfter(GetLastTransitionOn()) lies after utcNow, otherwise apply the transition; a stale or re-armed timeout is then a no-op by construction.
    • Why it's built this way: the base declares no property and no field (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:9-11), and GetLastTransitionOn is a method rather than a property on purpose: a property declared here would map into every adopter's EF model (ProcessManagerAggregateRoot.cs:25-28). Deriving from it therefore leaves migrations unchanged.
    • Where it's used: the TProcess constraint of both process handler bases (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:42, MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/ProcessManagers/ProcessDomainEventHandlerBase.cs:34); the timeout base calls DecideTimeout (ProcessTimeoutHandlerBase.cs:67).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether an MMCA.ADC aggregate derives from it today is not determinable from the brief's usage sites, which list only MMCA.Common source and tests.

    DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:24 · Level 6 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: delivers one DeliverWebhookInternalCommand: reads the subscription under the tenant the processor restored for the row, posts the signed envelope, and maps the outcome onto the subscription and onto the row. It is not ITransactional: the outcome is recorded on its own save (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:12-17).
    • Depends on: IWebhookSubscriptionStore, IWebhookSender, ITenantContext (G05), WebhookSettings, WebhookEnvelope, WebhookSubscription, WebhookErrorCodes; implements ICommandHandler<in TCommand, TResult> (G05); BCL TimeProvider, IOptions<T>, ILogger<T>.
    • Concept: [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: success completes the row, failure returns a failed Result so the internal-command processor retries with backoff and dead-letters after its attempt limit (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:113-114). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]: one source-generated log line per outcome (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:145-161).
    • Walkthrough: HandleAsync (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:37) first fails when Webhooks:Enabled is false (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:41-49), so a row queued before webhooks were switched off retries and dead-letters where it stays requeue-able. A missing, deleted or disabled subscription completes the row with nothing sent (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:52-57). Otherwise it builds the WebhookDeliveryRequest with the delivery id formatted "D", the envelope, and the signing secrets valid now (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:59-64), sends it (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:66), applies the outcome (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:67) and saves the subscription (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:69). ApplyOutcome (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:79): Delivered records success (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:85-90); TargetRefused records a fixed reason ("not a public address" or "not an https address", chosen by the stored URL scheme, DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:33-34,137-140) and completes, keeping the address-bearing detail to the server-side warning (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:92-99); Gone records the failure, disables the subscription and completes (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:106-111); Rejected, TransportFailed and SecretUnreadable record the failure and return a failed Result with Webhooks.SecretUnreadable or Webhooks.DeliveryFailed (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:115-124).
    • Why it's built this way: a delivery's own save means the outcome survives even when the row is about to retry; recording a fixed refusal reason keeps resolved internal addresses out of the tenant admin API, which returns LastFailure (DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:94-95).
    • Where it's used: registered scoped as ICommandHandler<DeliverWebhookInternalCommand, Result> (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:97) and invoked by the internal-command processor for rows on the WebhookDeliveryLane.

    WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Delivery · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:35 · Level 8 · class (internal sealed partial)

    • What it is: turns one published integration event into one DeliverWebhookInternalCommand per enabled subscription of the event's tenant that lists its webhook type (or "*"), all scheduled inside one transaction (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:17-21).
    • Depends on: ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent> (its base), IIntegrationEvent, WebhookSettings, WebhookEventCatalog, IWebhookSubscriptionStore, WebhookDeliveryId, ITenantContext (G05), IUnitOfWork (G07), IInternalCommandScheduler (G14); BCL System.Text.Json.
    • Concept: [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture]: the bridge from the internal event stream to external HTTP subscribers. It consumes the same at-least-once integration event as any other handler, and hands each delivery to the durable internal-command lane rather than posting inline (ADR-142).
    • Walkthrough: two constructors: one with a logger (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:45) and one that uses NullLogger for a container with no logging (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:54). HandleScopedAsync (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:60) returns early unless WebhookSettings.Enabled is true and the catalog maps TEvent to a webhook type (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:62-67). It lists the tenant's enabled subscriptions (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:69-72) and keeps those that subscribe to the type (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:74). It serializes the event by its runtime type with JsonSerializerDefaults.Web (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:38,80); a payload over MaxPayloadBytes is logged and skipped (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:81-86), because redelivery cannot shrink it. It builds one command per match (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:88-96), schedules all of them inside IUnitOfWork.ExecuteInTransactionAsync (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:101-115), and throws when any schedule fails (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:117-123).
    • Why it's built this way: throwing on a failed schedule makes the outbox or broker redeliver the event, and the deterministic delivery id keeps the redelivered rows' webhook-id unchanged (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:27-31); one transaction means a partial fan-out is never committed. Being inert without settings (WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:23-26) lets AddWebhookEvent register it in any host.
    • Where it's used: registered per event by AddWebhookEvent<TEvent>(name) as a singleton IIntegrationEventHandler<TEvent> (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/DependencyInjection.Webhooks.cs:80), so it runs alongside the event's other IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> registrations.

    IInboxStore

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Inbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/IInboxStore.cs:16 · Level 0 · interface

    • What it is: the consumer-side idempotency port. It lets a broker consumer detect and skip an integration event that this service has already processed, and it owns the small protocol that brackets a consume: open, run handlers, close (or discard on failure).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party (BCL Guid/Task). Conceptually keyed by IDomainEvent's MessageId, and backed by InboxMessage rows in the EF implementation.
    • Concept introduced, the consumer-side Inbox (ADR-021). [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (idempotent consumers) and [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] (assesses tolerance of duplicate or redelivered messages). The inbox is the consumer-side complement to the outbox. Every reliable broker guarantees at-least-once delivery, so the same message can arrive more than once after a transient failure. The inbox records the message ids it has successfully processed; on redelivery the store answers "already seen" and the consumer discards the duplicate without re-running side effects. The doc comment states the shape of the protocol (IInboxStore.cs:8-14): TryBeginAsync -> handlers -> CompleteAsync, where TryBeginAsync stages the inbox row in the same scoped unit of work the handlers write through, so a handler's own SaveChangesAsync commits the row together with its mutations and a crash between the two becomes impossible.
    • Concept introduced, default interface implementations as a compatibility layer. [Rubric §1, SOLID] (interface segregation and open/closed extension) and [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]. Three of the five members ship bodies on the interface itself: TryBeginAsync (IInboxStore.cs:38-39) is defined as !await AlreadyProcessedAsync(...), CompleteAsync (IInboxStore.cs:48-49) forwards to MarkProcessedAsync, and Abandon (IInboxStore.cs:63) returns true. The <remarks> says why (IInboxStore.cs:33-37): an implementation that only supplies the two abstract members keeps the older write-the-row-after-the-handlers behavior and still compiles, so the staging protocol was added without breaking an external implementation of the port.
    • Walkthrough, in protocol order.
      • AlreadyProcessedAsync(Guid messageId, CancellationToken) (IInboxStore.cs:19): the abstract keyed lookup by the event's MessageId.
      • MarkProcessedAsync(Guid messageId, string eventType, CancellationToken) (IInboxStore.cs:22): the abstract write. eventType is retained for diagnostics only.
      • TryBeginAsync(...) (IInboxStore.cs:38-39): returns false when the message was already processed, in which case the caller must skip its handlers and ack; otherwise it stages the row and returns true (IInboxStore.cs:24-32).
      • CompleteAsync(...) (IInboxStore.cs:48-49): persists the staged row only if a handler's save has not already committed it, and the contract calls itself idempotent (IInboxStore.cs:41-44).
      • Abandon(Guid messageId) (IInboxStore.cs:63): the failure branch. Its return value carries the honest bad news (IInboxStore.cs:57-62): true means the staged row was discarded before it reached the database so the redelivery will reprocess, false means an earlier handler's save had already committed the row, so the redelivery is treated as a duplicate and the remaining handlers will not run again.
    • Why it's built this way: the port lets the no-op and EF-backed implementations be swapped by configuration without touching consumer code, a §1/§6 dependency-inversion win, and putting the protocol (not just the two data operations) on the interface keeps the ordering rules in one place instead of in every consumer. ADR-021 records this inbox as the broker-consume sibling of ADR-003's outbox (producer side) and ADR-017's HTTP-edge idempotency, deduping broker redeliveries by MessageId in the consumer's own database with a unique index as the race guard.
    • Where it's used: consumed by IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> (this group), which takes it as a primary-constructor parameter (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:42), calls TryBeginAsync before invoking handlers (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:82), Abandon on a handler exception (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:113), and CompleteAsync after they all succeed (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:123). Implemented by EfInboxStore and NoOpInboxStore.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: both registrations live inside AddBrokerMessaging (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:63), which returns early when the configured provider is in-process (DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:67-70), so a monolith host that never calls it has no IInboxStore in the container at all. That is consistent (nothing consumes the port without a broker consumer) but it does mean the inbox posture is a broker-mode decision, not a container-wide one.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    InboxDisabledWarningService

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Inbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:20 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial, IHostedService)

    • What it is: a one-shot hosted service that writes a single Warning at startup when a host runs broker messaging with consumer-side inbox deduplication explicitly turned off. It does nothing else: no timer, no per-message work.
    • Depends on: ILogger<InboxDisabledWarningService> via primary constructor (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:20) and Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting.IHostedService (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:21). It names NoOpInboxStore in its doc comment but has no code dependency on it.
    • Concept introduced, making a disabled safety feature audible. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] (assesses whether an operator can tell the running posture of the system from its own output) and [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] (duplicate tolerance). The class comment states the argument (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:6-17): a silently disabled safety feature is indistinguishable from an enabled one until the first duplicate side effect reaches a customer, so the off state is made loud exactly once, at startup, where it costs one log line and nothing per message. Because the inbox now defaults to ON for every broker transport (MessageBusSettings.IsInboxEnabled, MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:136), reaching this service means the host set MessageBus:EnableInbox=false deliberately, so the text reads as an opt-out record rather than a nudge about a default (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:12-17). This is the deliberate opposite of the per-message success log in OutboxProcessor, which is Debug precisely because it recurs.
    • Walkthrough: StartAsync (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:24-28) calls the source-generated LogInboxDisabled and returns Task.CompletedTask; StopAsync (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:31) is a completed task. The message itself is the teaching artifact (InboxDisabledWarningService.cs:33-36): it names the setting and the store that produced the posture, states that the broker default is ON, explains that at-least-once delivery means every redelivered message runs its handlers again and duplicates their side effects until each handler is idempotent on its own, gives the remedy (remove the setting or set it to true), and notes that the InboxMessages table is already part of the model, so turning it back on needs no schema work.
    • Why it's built this way: registering the warning only on the disabled branch means a host that keeps the default never constructs this service at all, so the correct configuration pays nothing. Emitting it from StartAsync rather than from the DI registration puts it in the host's startup log next to the rest of the boot sequence, where an operator reads it.
    • Where it's used: added by AddBrokerMessaging inside the else branch of the IsInboxEnabled check, immediately after the NoOpInboxStore registration (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:142-147, the AddHostedService call at line 117). Covered by InboxDisabledWarningServiceTests (MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Tests/Persistence/Inbox/InboxDisabledWarningServiceTests.cs:11), which is what makes a log-only class testable at all ([Rubric §14, Testability]).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: because the whole registration sits inside AddBrokerMessaging, which returns early for the in-process provider (DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:67-70), a monolith host never sees this warning. That is correct (in-process dispatch does not redeliver) but it does mean the absence of the line is not by itself evidence that dedup is on.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    InboxMessage

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Inbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/InboxMessage.cs:8 · Level 0 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: a deduplication record saying "this service already processed the integration event with this MessageId". It is an EF entity that lives in the consumer service's own database, mirroring the outbox.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party (BCL only). Its MessageId carries IDomainEvent's id semantics, and it is read and written through IInboxStore.
    • Concept introduced, the inbox row (idempotency table). [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (idempotent consumers) and [Rubric §8, Data Architecture] (a deliberate dedup table per service). When IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> opens a consume, the store stages one of these rows; when the consume closes successfully the row is committed. Because the table lives in the consumer's own database, the dedup respects the database-per-service boundary (ADR-006) and can be committed in the same transaction as a handler's own writes.
    • Walkthrough: four init-only properties (InboxMessage.cs:11-20). Id (surrogate Guid PK defaulted to Guid.NewGuid(), InboxMessage.cs:11) is the EF key. MessageId (required, InboxMessage.cs:14) is the event's own id, the deduplication key. EventType (required, InboxMessage.cs:17) is retained for diagnostics. ProcessedOn (InboxMessage.cs:20) is the UTC timestamp stamped at staging time. The shape only makes sense together with its EF configuration (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/ApplicationDbContext.cs:880-894): the table is dbo.InboxMessages (ApplicationDbContext.cs:883), EventType is capped at 500 non-Unicode characters (ApplicationDbContext.cs:885), MessageId carries a unique index named IX_InboxMessages_MessageId (ApplicationDbContext.cs:886-888), and ProcessedOn carries a second, non-unique index IX_InboxMessages_ProcessedOn (ApplicationDbContext.cs:892-893) purely so the age-based retention purge has something to seek instead of scanning the table (ApplicationDbContext.cs:890-891, [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]).
    • Why it's built this way: separating Id (the PK for EF internals) from MessageId (the business dedup key with the unique index) follows the surrogate-key convention used elsewhere in the codebase, and the unique index is what turns a racing duplicate delivery into a catchable DbUpdateException rather than a read-then-write race. Storing it as a plain entity lets the same EF stack purge it (see OutboxCleanupService) and lets a handler's own SaveChangesAsync commit it. ADR-021 governs the mechanism, and the row lives in the consumer's own database (ADR-006).
    • Where it's used: staged, saved, and queried by EfInboxStore (EfInboxStore.cs:59,124); purged by OutboxCleanupService when the inbox is enabled (OutboxCleanupService.cs:173-188, gated on _inboxEnabled at OutboxCleanupService.cs:53 and called at OutboxCleanupService.cs:118-121); configured on every relational context by ApplicationDbContext.ConfigureInbox (ApplicationDbContext.cs:880, called from line 347) (G07).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the type itself has no first-party reference (it is a plain POCO), so the links to IInboxStore/IDomainEvent above are conceptual, not compile dependencies. The configuration is skipped by the Cosmos context, which overrides OnModelCreating (ApplicationDbContext.cs:876-878). There is also no tenant column: a tenant with its own database gets its own InboxMessages table instead (see OutboxCleanupService).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    OutboxDisabledNoticeService

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Outbox.Administration · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Administration/OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:22 · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial, IHostedService)

    • What it is: a hosted service whose entire job is to write one Information log line at startup when the host is running without the transactional outbox, so the changed delivery guarantee is stated out loud rather than inferred from an absent background service.
    • Depends on: ILogger<OutboxDisabledNoticeService> via primary constructor (OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:22) and IHostedService (line 22); nothing else first-party.
    • Concept introduced, announcing a posture instead of leaving it to be discovered. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses whether an operator can tell what mode a host is in without reading its source, and [Rubric §33, Developer Experience] assesses whether the framework's defaults explain themselves. Outbox registration is a transport decision, not a persistence one: MessageBusSettings.IsOutboxEnabled resolves to false for the in-process provider unless MessageBus:EnableOutbox says otherwise (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:170), and in that mode neither OutboxProcessor nor OutboxCleanupService is registered (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:260-268). The delivery guarantee changes with it: events reach their handlers synchronously inside the raising process, and a crash between the commit and the dispatch loses them. The class doc states that this is the right trade for a single-process application and the wrong one to discover from an absent hosted service (OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:7-14).
    • Walkthrough: StartAsync calls the single generated log method and returns Task.CompletedTask (OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:26-30); StopAsync is a no-op (line 32). The message itself is the type's real content (OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:35-38): it names what is not happening (no OutboxMessages rows, neither background service running, a failed handler is not retried, a crash between commit and dispatch loses the event), names the fix (MessageBus:EnableOutbox=true), and closes the obvious follow-up question by noting that the OutboxMessages table is already part of the model, so flipping the flag is never a migration.
    • Why it's built this way: the level choice is the interesting part and the doc argues it explicitly (OutboxDisabledNoticeService.cs:15-19). This is Information, not the Warning that the inbox's InboxDisabledWarningService uses, because a missing outbox is the default posture of an in-process host rather than an opt-out of a safety feature, and a warning on every small application's startup would train operators to ignore the category.
    • Where it's used: registered by AddInfrastructure in the else branch of the outbox gate (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:265-268), so a host either gets the two outbox background services or gets this notice, never both and never neither.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    OutboxOrigin

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Outbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxOrigin.cs:30 · Level 0 · record struct (public readonly)

    • What it is: the pieces of ambient context (UserId, UserRoles, TenantId, CorrelationId, plus the raising user's UserPermissions) captured onto an OutboxMessage row at write time, so a handler running a poll cycle later sees the same principal, tenant and correlation the raising request did.
    • Depends on: nothing first-party beyond the module's UserIdentifierType alias; a plain readonly record struct with four positional properties and one init property.
    • Concept introduced, ambient-context capture for asynchronous delivery. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] (the outbox row carries the caller's context across the process/thread boundary) and [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] (a crash-and-retry replay still restores the original principal rather than an anonymous one). A synchronous handler reads ICurrentUserService and ITenantContext directly; an outbox-delivered handler runs on a background poll with neither populated, so OutboxMessage.FromDomainEvent captures this struct at write time and the delivery path restores it before the handler runs.
    • Walkthrough: four positional properties, all nullable (OutboxOrigin.cs:30-34): UserId (UserIdentifierType?), UserRoles (string?, a comma-separated list), TenantId (string?) and CorrelationId (string?). A fifth, UserPermissions (string?, a JSON array of the raising user's permission claims, null when they hold none), is an init property in the struct body (OutboxOrigin.cs:41) rather than a positional parameter, so the shipped constructor is unchanged. Every field is optional because the struct's own default value (all nulls) is exactly what a caller with nothing to capture, or every row written before these columns existed, reads back as.
    • Why it's built this way: a single positional struct groups the four values so FromDomainEvent(domainEvent, origin = default) takes one extra optional parameter instead of four, and a defaulted struct argument costs a caller nothing when there is no ambient context to capture (an unauthenticated background job, for instance). Keeping it a record struct rather than a class avoids a heap allocation on the hot save path for what is, per row, four small fields copied once.
    • Where it's used: constructed by DbContextFactory.AttachScopeAccessors (DbContextFactory.cs:156-160) from ICurrentUserService, AmbientOrigin.FlattenRoles, the tenant context and the correlation context, and exposed through ApplicationDbContext.OutboxOriginAccessor/CurrentOutboxOrigin (ApplicationDbContext.cs:259,265); read by OutboxMessage.FromDomainEvent to populate the row's five capture columns (including UserPermissions, OutboxMessage.cs:166).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the struct itself does not restore anything, it only carries the values; the restore side (putting the captured user/tenant/correlation back onto a delivery scope) is the outbox processor and AmbientOrigin helper's job, not this type's.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    NoOpInboxStore

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Inbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/NoOpInboxStore.cs:7 · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the IInboxStore used when a broker host explicitly disables the inbox. It never dedups and records nothing, so consumer behavior is exactly what it would be with no inbox at all.
    • Depends on: IInboxStore (the port it implements); BCL Task/Guid only.
    • Concept reinforced, the Null Object pattern. [Rubric §2, Design Patterns] (assesses idiomatic use of patterns) and [Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]. AlreadyProcessedAsync always returns Task.FromResult(false) (NoOpInboxStore.cs:9-10) and MarkProcessedAsync returns Task.CompletedTask (NoOpInboxStore.cs:12-13). The consumer pipeline is written against IInboxStore and runs identically whether or not dedup is enabled: the Null Object removes a runtime if (inbox enabled) branch from every consumer.
    • Walkthrough: the class implements only the two abstract members, so the three protocol members come from the interface defaults and compose into exactly the right no-op behavior. TryBeginAsync inherits !await AlreadyProcessedAsync(...) (IInboxStore.cs:38-39), which is always true, so handlers always run; CompleteAsync inherits the forward to MarkProcessedAsync (IInboxStore.cs:48-49), which writes nothing; Abandon inherits true (IInboxStore.cs:63), meaning "nothing was committed, a redelivery will reprocess". Nothing about the staging protocol had to be restated here, which is the payoff of putting the defaults on the port.
    • Why it's built this way: the inbox resolves ON for a broker transport (MessageBusSettings.IsInboxEnabled, MessageBusSettings.cs:136), so this store is the deliberate opt-out path for a host that cannot query the InboxMessages table yet, not a quiet default. Note that it is registered as a singleton while EfInboxStore is scoped (DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:134,142): a stateless no-op needs no per-request lifetime, an EF-backed store that stages rows in the scope's unit of work does. The same else branch also registers InboxDisabledWarningService (DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:147), so choosing the Null Object is never silent (ADR-021).
    • Where it's used: registered as IInboxStore inside AddBrokerMessaging on the else branch of settings.IsInboxEnabled, that is when MessageBus:EnableInbox=false is set explicitly (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:132-148); consumed by IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    OutboxSettings

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Outbox.Administration · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Administration/OutboxSettings.cs:10 · Level 2 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the Outbox configuration section, tuning the outbox background processor and its cleanup companion. Every property carries a default (OutboxSettings.cs:17-118), so the section is optional and a host with no Outbox configuration still runs a working outbox. Note the division of labour with MessageBusSettings: that class decides WHETHER the outbox runs, this one decides HOW.

    • Depends on: DataSource (the engine enum) and DataSourceKey (for its DefaultName constant), both imported through MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces.Infrastructure (OutboxSettings.cs:53, :62). Externals: System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations for the [Range] attributes.

    • Concept introduced, options binding with a static SectionName. Note the convention that runs through every settings class in the framework: public static readonly string SectionName = "Outbox"; (OutboxSettings.cs:13) is the single source of truth for the section name, referenced at the bind call instead of duplicating the literal (DependencyInjection.cs:151). The properties are init-only, so once materialized from configuration they are immutable for the process lifetime.

      [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses how reliably state changes turn into dispatched events. This is the knob set for the at-least-once outbox (ADR-003): MaxRetries (:21) caps attempts, and ProcessingDelaySeconds (:45) is the eligibility delay after message creation. It defaults to 0, so an integration event is published as soon as the post-save signal wakes the processor (tens of milliseconds) instead of several seconds later (:35-36). The delay does not bound the duplicate-dispatch window: on the async save path, local domain-event rows are inserted under the LeaseSeconds lease, so the in-process pipeline (save, dispatch, mark processed) races the processor only once that lease expires, and sync-path rows never dispatch in-process. Handlers must be idempotent regardless, and the doc reserves a value above 0 for holding rows back deliberately, for example to give a failed in-process local dispatch a pause before the processor retries it (:36-42).

      [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses behavior under replication and repeated failure. Three properties carry the weight. LeaseSeconds (:90) claims a batch for a replica so concurrent replicas never double-dispatch, and expires so a dead replica's rows become claimable again (:80-88, applied at OutboxProcessor.cs:234). The lease is checked before each row is dispatched and renewed for the rows not yet dispatched only once less than half of it remains, so half the lease must comfortably exceed the time to dispatch one message, not a whole batch; local domain-event rows are also inserted under it while their in-process dispatch runs (:84-87). RetryBackoffBaseSeconds (:107) makes the retry cadence explicit: attempt n waits base * 2^(n-1), multiplied by a jitter factor in [0.8, 1.2] so rows that failed together do not retry in lockstep, then capped at LeaseSeconds (:92-97, implemented at OutboxProcessor.cs:1102-1108). The remark is worth reading as a design lesson: before this setting existed the claim was simply never cleared on failure, so the real retry cadence was an accident of the lease (300s) rather than a decision (:98-105).

      [Rubric §31, Cost & FinOps] assesses cost-relevant defaults. PollingIntervalSeconds (:31) is a fallback, not a hot loop (:23-29): with signal-based wakeup the processor wakes immediately on new entries and otherwise smart-waits until the earliest pending message becomes eligible, so deployed environments set it high (300 in this workspace) to cut idle SQL polling without adding latency for real traffic.

      [Rubric §8, Data Architecture] assesses how deliberately data is routed. The DataSource / DatabaseName pair (:53, :62) names where integration events published via IEventBus are written, defaulting to the top-level connection strings so single-database behavior is preserved. It is a per-write target, not a global switch: the doc is explicit that the PROCESSOR still drains the outbox table of every relational physical source in use (:58-60, and see OutboxProcessor.cs:153-159).

    • Walkthrough: one static field then eleven init properties, nine of them [Range]-validated.

      • SectionName (OutboxSettings.cs:13): static readonly "Outbox", the bind key.
      • BatchSize (:16-17): [Range(1, 1000)], default 50; messages per cycle, used both to size the fetch (OutboxProcessor.cs:320) and to decide whether more eligible work remains (OutboxProcessor.cs:267, :361).
      • MaxRetries (:20-21): [Range(1, 20)], default 5; attempts before a message is treated as dead-lettered and excluded from the poll (OutboxProcessor.cs:290, :424, :669). The first failure is only re-scheduled when MaxRetries > 1, so 1 is honored as "the host asked for no retries at all" (OutboxProcessor.cs:1080).
      • PollingIntervalSeconds (:30-31): [Range(1, 3600)], default 2; the fallback interval.
      • ProcessingDelaySeconds (:44-45): [Range(0, 600)], default 0 (no initializer, so the int default); the eligibility delay, applied as a cutoff on the message timestamp (OutboxProcessor.cs:206, :275).
      • DataSource (:53): default DataSource.SQLServer; must be a relational provider that supports the outbox table (SQL Server, PostgreSQL or SQLite, :47-52), since the outbox is a table.
      • DatabaseName (:62): default DataSourceKey.DefaultName; the logical source name paired with DataSource.
      • RetentionDays (:69-70): [Range(0, 3650)], default 7; days a PROCESSED message is kept before purge, with 0 disabling purging entirely (OutboxCleanupService.cs:73, cutoff at :91).
      • CleanupIntervalHours (:77-78): [Range(1, 168)], default 6; the purge sweep cadence, ignored when RetentionDays is 0 (OutboxCleanupService.cs:60).
      • LeaseSeconds (:89-90): [Range(10, 3600)], default 300; the claim window, renewed for the remaining rows once less than half of it is left.
      • RetryBackoffBaseSeconds (:106-107): [Range(1, 3600)], default 10; the exponential-backoff base described above.
      • DeadLetterRetentionDays (:117-118): [Range(0, 3650)], default 0, which falls back to RetentionDays. Set it higher to keep exhausted payloads around for diagnosis and manual replay; the cleanup service resolves the fallback explicitly before computing its cutoff (OutboxCleanupService.cs:160-162). The window runs from the last failed attempt, the row's LockedUntil stamp falling back to OccurredOn, so a replay resets it (:113-115).

    • Why it's built this way: the defaults encode the framework's out-of-the-box posture (ADR-003 outbox, ADR-006 database-per-service): a host with no Outbox section that has the outbox switched on gets a working at-least-once processor writing to its single default database, while a multi-service deployment overrides PollingIntervalSeconds, DataSource and DatabaseName to tune cost and routing. The [Range] guards give fail-fast validation at bind time rather than a bad value surfacing mid-cycle (ADR-070).

    • Where it's used: bound with .ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart() in AddInfrastructure (DependencyInjection.cs:150-153). Consumed by OutboxProcessor (OutboxProcessor.cs:64, :66) and OutboxCleanupService (OutboxCleanupService.cs:46, :52) for batching, retry pacing and retention; by both event buses to pick the write target when publishing an integration event (InProcessEventBus InProcessEventBus.cs:38, :78; BrokerEventBus BrokerEventBus.cs:35, :67); and by EfInboxStore, which deliberately reuses the same DataSource/DatabaseName pair so the inbox lands in the consumer's own database (EfInboxStore.cs:46, :217).

    • Caveats: BrokerEventBus throws when the resolved target does not support the outbox table, naming both configuration keys in the message (BrokerEventBus.cs:76), so an outbox pointed at Cosmos fails on first publish rather than at bind time; the [Range] attributes cannot express "relational engines only".

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    OutboxMessage

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Outbox · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/OutboxMessage.cs:15 · Level 2 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: a row in an OutboxMessages table: a JSON-serialized domain event persisted in the same database transaction as its aggregate, ready for reliable asynchronous dispatch, plus all the bookkeeping the processor needs (retry state, claim lease, trace context, ordering key).
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent and IHasOrderingKey (G02), EventNameResolver, OutboxOrigin; BCL System.Text.Json, System.Diagnostics.Activity (trace capture) and System.Collections.Concurrent (the type cache).
    • Concept introduced, the Transactional Outbox pattern. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (reliable at-least-once delivery), [Rubric §8, Data Architecture] (the event is written in the same transaction as the aggregate) and [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] (the delivery guarantee survives a crash). ADR-003 is the governing decision. The problem it solves: if you save an aggregate and then publish an event, a crash between the two loses the event. The fix is to write the event to an OutboxMessages row in the same database transaction as the aggregate change; the OutboxProcessor then reads unprocessed rows and dispatches them, re-dispatching after a crash (at-least-once). Each service owns its own outbox table (ADR-006), so there is no cross-service race.
    • Walkthrough
      • Static SerializerOptions (OutboxMessage.cs:17-20), a JsonSerializerOptions with ReferenceHandler.IgnoreCycles, so an event referencing a cyclic entity graph still serializes; the same instance is reused on the read side (line 194) so payloads round-trip symmetrically.
      • Static EventTypeCache (OutboxMessage.cs:30), a ConcurrentDictionary<string, Type?> keyed ordinally by the stored name, memoizing the reflection that DeserializeEvent would otherwise run per row. Only a successful resolution is cached (lines 22-29): an unresolvable name is re-scanned on its retry, so an assembly that loads late can still resolve it before the row dead-letters.
      • Identity and payload (OutboxMessage.cs:33-46): Id (Guid, defaulted to Guid.NewGuid(), line 33); EventType (required, the stored identity, line 40); Payload (required, the JSON string, line 43); OccurredOn (the business timestamp copied from IDomainEvent.DateOccurred, line 46). All init-only. EventType is documented as the EventNameAttribute name when the event declares one and the assembly-qualified type name otherwise (lines 35-39).
