      • Lease renewal, once per batch when due, and guarded outcomes. The claim lease covers the whole batch, so a slow batch could outlive it and let another replica claim a later row while this one is still busy (remarks, lines 471-480). Renewal is not a statement per row. RenewLeaseIfDueAsync (OutboxProcessor.cs:707-721) runs before each row is dispatched (line 547), subtracts the clock from the batch's LeaseUntil (line 713) and writes nothing while at least half of LeaseSeconds remains (line 714). Once less remains it flushes the buffered stamps and calls RenewRemainingLeasesAsync (lines 716-717; lines 842-881). That method pushes LockedUntil out to now plus LeaseSeconds (line 848) for every not-yet-dispatched, not-known-lost row of the batch (lines 853-856), in one ExecuteUpdateAsync per chunk of MaxIdsPerStatement ids (500, line 93, well under SQL Server's 2100-parameter limit), each filtered on Id and this batch's LockToken (lines 858-863). When fewer rows renewed than asked for, one query finds the ids still carrying the token and the rest join LostIds (lines 865-877), and the batch's LeaseUntil is advanced (line 880). A row found in LostIds is skipped with a Warning (LogLeaseLostBeforeDispatch, lines 547-551), so a lease that lapsed during one long dispatch is caught by the next check before another row goes out. A failed row's outcome is written the same guarded way as soon as the row is done: StampAsync (OutboxProcessor.cs:1035-1056) runs the update against Id == message.Id && LockToken == lockToken (lines 1041-1042), so a replica that lost the row mid-dispatch matches nothing, logs LogLeaseLost, detaches the tracked row and drops its stale outcome instead of overwriting the new owner's (lines 1047-1052). A match syncs the tracked row's original values to the current ones and marks it Unchanged, so nothing later writes the outcome through the change tracker without the guard (lines 1054-1055). An expired lease nobody re-claimed still carries this token, so its outcome is stamped (doc, lines 1022-1029).
      • Dispatching, and delivered rows stamped in batches. DispatchRowsAsync (OutboxProcessor.cs:529-618) walks the claimed batch by index and opens a per-message activity (line 553). A row whose payload will not deserialize goes to HandleUnresolvableType (lines 577-581). Otherwise DeliverAsync (lines 810-832, called at line 584) publishes an IIntegrationEvent through IMessageBus and sends a pure domain event to IDomainEventDispatcher. After each row, RecordOutcomeAsync (called at line 610; lines 728-742; the comment at lines 604-608 notes this sits outside the try on purpose, so a database failure while recording is not charged to the row as a retry) does one of two things. A failed row goes to RecordFailureOutcomeAsync and its retry state is written now. A delivered row only joins batch.PendingStamps (line 740). FlushDeliveredAsync (lines 895-935) later stamps the buffer: per chunk of MaxIdsPerStatement it takes one ProcessedOn value from the flush time (line 908) and issues one guarded ExecuteUpdateAsync filtered on the ids and the batch's LockToken (lines 910-913). When fewer rows were stamped than buffered, one query finds the rows that carry the token (lines 918-926). SyncDelivered (lines 970-995) then handles each row: an unmatched row logs LogLeaseLost and is detached (lines 973-978), while a stamped row is accepted as Unchanged (lines 980-982), increments ProcessedCounter (line 985), records DispatchLagHistogram as the seconds between OccurredOn and ProcessedOn, clamped at zero because the two timestamps come from different hosts and clock skew must not publish a negative duration (lines 987-992), and writes the success log. Each chunk leaves the buffer once synced, so a retried flush never stamps or counts a row twice (line 933; doc 883-894). Flushes happen at three points: the end of the batch (line 510), before a lease renewal (line 716), and on any early exit (see Graceful shutdown). The per-message success log is deliberately Debug, not Information, and the comment prices the difference: it would otherwise be the single noisiest line in steady state, a real telemetry-ingestion cost, while failures stay loud (lines 1179-1183).
      • Dead-lettering an unresolvable type, with one grace attempt. HandleUnresolvableType (OutboxProcessor.cs:1074-1100) records LastError (line 1076) and treats the first failure to resolve as transient, retrying it through the normal backoff path, because the assembly declaring the type may simply not be loaded yet, a module assembly resolved lazily or a host still coming up, and a name that resolves one cycle later was never a dead letter (doc, lines 1058-1067). Only the second attempt is terminal, which is also the point at which an operator has already had a Warning naming the row (lines 1080-1086, LogTypeUnresolvableRetry at 1191-1192, whose message names the fix: give the event an EventName). A host that set MaxRetries to 1 asked for no retries at all, so that case skips the grace attempt rather than scheduling one the poll's filter would never pick up (lines 1078-1080). The terminal path dead-letters the row the way exhausted retries do: it sets RetryCount to MaxRetries and stamps LockedUntil with the current time rather than clearing it, leaving ProcessedOn null (lines 1089-1093). The comment gives the reason (lines 1091-1092): the dead-letter retention window runs from LockedUntil, the last failed attempt, and the row already leaves the poll through the RetryCount filter, so the stamp holds no claim. It then increments the dead-letter counter with reason=type_unresolvable and logs at Error (lines 1094-1098). The doc names the effect (lines 1064-1067): the row leaves the poll but stays listed and replayable by the outbox administration and is kept for the dead-letter retention window, instead of being purged as delivered.
      • The circuit breaker around the message-bus publish. Only the integration-event publish is wrapped, and the doc states precisely what that hop is. _brokerPublishPipeline (OutboxProcessor.cs:121) is a Polly ResiliencePipeline built by BuildBrokerPublishPipeline(timeProvider) (lines 1118-1129) from BrokerResilienceDefaults (failure ratio, minimum throughput, sampling and break durations, lines 1122-1125), on a ResiliencePipelineBuilder whose TimeProvider is the processor's own clock (line 1119), so a test driving the clock drives the break duration too (doc, lines 1115-1116). The integration-event branch of DeliverAsync executes the publish through it (lines 821-825). The guarded call is the IMessageBus publish: the broker hop on a host that registers broker messaging, but on a monolith the default InProcessMessageBus runs the integration-event handlers in-process inside the breaker, so failing handlers can open it too (field doc, lines 106-120; comment, lines 816-820). Three deliberate choices sit in the doc comments. It never guards the database calls, because a breaker on those would open exactly when the processor most needs to persist retry state (lines 110-111). It carries no retry strategy, because the outbox already owns retry through RetryCount and ComputeRetryBackoffSeconds (lines 112-113). And it is an instance field rather than a static one, so breaker state cannot leak across the many processors a test assembly constructs in parallel (lines 114-119). OperationCanceledException is excluded from the handled set (lines 1126-1127), because a host shutdown cancelling a batch is not evidence that the broker is unhealthy (doc, lines 1112-1115). The pure domain-event branch is left unwrapped on purpose: it is a direct method call into this same process with no transport to be dead (comment, lines 818-820).
      • Failure handling. A cancellation during dispatch is rethrown rather than treated as a delivery failure, and the comment explains the bug that guard prevents (lines 588-595): falling into the generic handler would increment RetryCount and stamp LastError on this message and, since every later await fails the same way, on the whole remainder of the batch, so a graceful restart could dead-letter messages that were never attempted. Any other exception is caught (with an inline CA1031 justification, lines 596-598) and handed to ApplyDeliveryFailure (lines 600, 629-698), which writes the retry state onto the row in memory and returns the batch's circuit-open log latch. A genuine failure bumps RetryCount (line 644), records LastError as ExceptionChain.Describe(ex) truncated to the 4000-character column width (MaxErrorLength, line 87, through ColumnWidth.Truncate, lines 647-648) and re-leases the row for an explicit backoff (lines 658-661); the comment notes that simply keeping the original claim made every retry wait the full LeaseSeconds no matter what the polling interval or a signal said, turning the retry cadence into an accident of the lease (lines 650-657). These retry fields are persisted by RecordFailureAsync (lines 1001-1020), which writes RetryCount, LastError and LockedUntil through the token-guarded StampAsync update. A BrokenCircuitException is treated differently: an open circuit is a rejection, not a delivery attempt, so it does not consume a retry and cannot dead-letter the row, which would otherwise walk every undelivered row through its whole retry budget during a long outage without one real attempt; real publish failures, and the failure of the single half-open probe, keep counting (comment, lines 636-640; lines 641-645). The rejected row is re-leased for the break instead of a count-keyed backoff, through PollingLoop.ComputeCircuitOpenBackoffSeconds (line 660; PollingLoop.cs:211-220): the break duration plus up to a quarter of it as jitter, capped at LeaseSeconds, so a whole batch does not become claimable in the same instant the circuit half-opens, and the lease keeps it from hot-spinning. It is counted on BrokerMetrics.CircuitOpenCounter (lines 669-671) and logged once per batch through a local latch (lines 541, 673-676), because an open circuit rejects every remaining row in the same instant, and "the broker refused 50 messages" and "we did not try, the broker is known-dead" are different operational facts (comment, lines 667-668); the method then returns before the exhaustion check (line 678). The log is a Warning because nothing is lost, only deferred (comment, lines 1200-1202). When a genuine failure brings RetryCount to MaxRetries (5 by default, OutboxSettings.cs:21) the dead-letter counter is incremented with reason=retries_exhausted and it logs at Error (lines 681-691), otherwise it logs a retry Warning (lines 692-695); the exhausted row then leaves the poll through the RetryCount filter and is eventually purged by OutboxCleanupService after the dead-letter retention window (comment, lines 683-685), unless an operator replays it first through OutboxAdministration.
      • Backoff. OutboxProcessor.ComputeRetryBackoffSeconds (OutboxProcessor.cs:1107-1108) is a one-line forward to PollingLoop.ComputeRetryBackoffSeconds (PollingLoop.cs:197-209), passing _settings.RetryBackoffBaseSeconds and _settings.LeaseSeconds as the base and cap. The shared implementation is RetryBackoffBaseSeconds * 2^(retryCount - 1) (default base 10, OutboxSettings.cs:107) with the exponent clamped to at most 16 before it reaches Math.Pow (PollingLoop.cs:202), multiplied by a random jitter factor in [0.8, 1.2] (PollingLoop.cs:206) and capped at LeaseSeconds, the cap this processor passes in (PollingLoop.cs:208). Jitter is applied before the cap so a capped backoff sits exactly at the lease bound (comment, PollingLoop.cs:205). The doc names the reason for the jitter (PollingLoop.cs:188-191): a batch that failed together, one dependency outage failing all 50 rows in the same instant, would otherwise retry in lockstep and re-hammer that dependency on a single shared schedule. The CA5394 suppression on the shared NextJitter helper (PollingLoop.cs:222-223) is justified inline: the randomness feeds no security, token, key or cryptographic decision.
      • Graceful shutdown. Delivered rows are buffered, so shutdown handling covers two writes: the failed row in flight and the buffered stamps. DispatchMessagesAsync tracks a batchCompleted flag and, if the batch ends any way but normally, calls FlushDeliveredOnExitAsync from a finally (lines 505-520). That method flushes the buffer under its own 5-second budget (ShutdownStampTimeout, line 102; the CancellationTokenSource is built on the injected TimeProvider, line 951) rather than CancellationToken.None, so an uncancellable write against a dead connection cannot hold host shutdown open until the command timeout (lines 944-962). A flush failure is logged, never thrown (LogExitStampFailed, line 960 and 1215-1216: delivered messages will be redelivered when their lease expires), so it cannot replace the exception already propagating, which the polling loop uses to recognize shutdown. For a failed row, RecordFailureOutcomeAsync (lines 757-778) writes normally while the batch token is live; if the token is already cancelled, or the shutdown lands during the write itself (the guarded update is idempotent, so it is simply written again, lines 770-774), it falls to RecordFailureOnShutdownAsync (lines 784-803), which writes under the same budget (line 790), logs a failure as LogShutdownStampFailed (lines 796-800 and 1212-1213) and ends by rethrowing the cancellation (line 802). After each recorded row the loop also calls cancellationToken.ThrowIfCancellationRequested() (line 614), stopping the batch after the row's outcome is safe, so the finally stamps the rows already delivered. Without a shutdown, a database failure propagates and fails the source for the cycle (comment, lines 604-608).
      • Trace continuity. StartOutboxActivity (OutboxProcessor.cs:1142-1165) rebuilds the original request's ActivityContext from the row's TraceId/SpanId, carrying the stored trace flags (null reads as none) and marked remote (lines 1149-1153), so a parent-based sampler records the delivery span exactly when the raising request was sampled instead of exporting an orphan span for a request the sampler dropped (doc, lines 1135-1140). It starts a Consumer-kind OutboxProcess activity tagged with the message id, event type and data source (lines 1155-1162), returning null when no trace context was captured (lines 1144-1147), so traces span the asynchronous hop.
    • Why it's built this way: ADR-003 makes the outbox the durability guarantee behind every integration event; the per-source design follows from ADR-006 and the per-tenant expansion from ADR-073. The smart wait, the derived backlog count and the suppressed poll activity are all cost and latency work: an idle fleet polling around the clock would otherwise dominate telemetry ingestion. Dead-lettering unresolvable types stops one poison message from blocking the queue, the progress requirement on re-poll (see OutboxCycleResult) prevents a fully-failing batch from hot-spinning, the circuit breaker keeps a known-dead broker from being hammered once per row per cycle (and, by not charging its rejections as retries, keeps an outage from dead-lettering rows that were never attempted), the lease-plus-token pair is what makes running more than one replica safe, the ordering guard inside the claim is what makes ordered delivery survive both batching and scale-out, and the per-row scope is what lets one batch deliver rows belonging to different tenants.
    • Where it's used: registered as a hosted service by AddInfrastructure whenever the transport enables the outbox (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:260-264), so every broker-backed service host runs exactly one. The producer side is the two IEventBus implementations (InProcessEventBus and BrokerEventBus) plus the SaveChanges capture in DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor; its companion sweeper is OutboxCleanupService and its operator surface is OutboxAdministration.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the outbox.pending.depth gauge is per instance by design (see OutboxMetrics); the lease makes multiple replicas correct, but the gauge still must not be summed across them.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest

    MMCA.Common.Shared · MMCA.Common.Shared.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Webhooks/CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:17 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: the wire body of "create a webhook subscription for the caller's tenant": a target Url, an optional Description and the list of EventTypes to subscribe to (CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:5-8,17).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party; System.Diagnostics.CodeAnalysis for the suppressions.
    • Concept: a contract DTO in the Shared layer, so the API controller and any typed client share one shape. [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] assesses whether request contracts are explicit and stable; this one documents its own limits in the XML doc rather than in a separate spec: the URL must be an absolute https target (http and local targets only in Development when the host allows them), the description is up to 200 characters, and the event list holds 1 to 20 registered types or "*" for every one (CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:6-8).
    • Walkthrough: a single positional sealed record (CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:17). The two SuppressMessage attributes (CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:9-16) keep Url a string against CA1054/CA1056, with the justification that the URL is untrusted operator input validated as text before anything parses it, and that System.Uri would re-normalize it.
    • Why it's built this way: carrying the raw string means the validator judges exactly what the operator sent; the address policy itself runs later in DnsWebhookTargetValidator. The design is recorded in ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: IWebhookSubscriptionService.CreateAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:28, see IWebhookSubscriptionService) and the [FromBody] parameter of CreateAsync on WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:87-88). The answer is a WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.

    IWebhookDnsResolver

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/IWebhookDnsResolver.cs:9 · Level 0 · interface

    • What it is: the internal abstraction that resolves a webhook target's host name to its IP addresses, used both when a subscription is validated and when a delivery connects (IWebhookDnsResolver.cs:5-8).
    • Depends on: BCL System.Net.IPAddress. Implemented by SystemWebhookDnsResolver.
    • Concept: a test injection point over a static BCL call. [Rubric §14, Testability] assesses whether nondeterministic dependencies can be replaced; DNS is exactly that, and the doc says tests register a stub first (IWebhookDnsResolver.cs:6-7), which works because the default registration is a TryAddSingleton (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:81).
    • Walkthrough: one member, ResolveAsync(string host, CancellationToken) returning Task<IPAddress[]>, "possibly none" (IWebhookDnsResolver.cs:15). An empty array is a legal answer and both callers handle it explicitly.
    • Why it's built this way: one resolver feeds both the early check and the authoritative connect-time check, so a test can model a DNS answer that changes between them (the rebinding case the connect callback exists for). The interface is internal: hosts do not replace DNS resolution.
    • Where it's used: DnsWebhookTargetValidator (DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:21,45) and WebhookConnectCallback (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:23,40); tests in MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Tests/Webhooks/ (DnsWebhookTargetValidatorTests.cs, WebhookConnectCallbackTests.cs, AddWebhooksTests.cs) supply stubs.

    UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest

    MMCA.Common.Shared · MMCA.Common.Shared.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Webhooks/UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:17 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: the wire body that replaces an existing subscription's target URL, description and event types (UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:5).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: same shape, limits and CA1054/CA1056 suppressions as CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest (UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:6-17); see that section. It is a full replacement, not a patch: every field is required positionally, with only Description nullable.
    • Walkthrough: sealed record UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest(string Url, string? Description, IReadOnlyList<string> EventTypes) (UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest.cs:17).
    • Why it's built this way: a separate type from the create request keeps the two operations free to diverge without a breaking change, while today they carry identical fields. The update does not issue a secret, which is why its answer is the plain WebhookSubscriptionResponse.
    • Where it's used: IWebhookSubscriptionService.UpdateAsync(Guid id, UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest request, ...) (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:35) and the controller's UpdateAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:112-114).

    WebhookSubscriptionResponse

    MMCA.Common.Shared · MMCA.Common.Shared.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionResponse.cs:26 · Level 0 · record

    • What it is: one subscription as the administration surface lists it, without its signing secret (WebhookSubscriptionResponse.cs:5-8).
    • Depends on: nothing first-party.
    • Concept: a read model that deliberately omits a sensitive field. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether secrets leak through read paths; here the secret is simply not a member, and the doc points to WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse as the only carrier, at create or rotate time (WebhookSubscriptionResponse.cs:6-7).
    • Walkthrough: nine positional members (WebhookSubscriptionResponse.cs:26-35): Id, Url, Description, EventTypes (ordinal-sorted, "*" for all, line 12), IsEnabled, CreatedOn, and the delivery health fields LastDeliveredOn, LastFailedOn and LastFailure (lines 15-17), all UTC. The same CA1054/CA1056 suppressions as the request records apply (lines 18-25).
    • Why it's built this way: surfacing the last-success and last-failure stamps on the list lets an operator see a broken receiver without reading logs, a small [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] touch.

    WebhookTargetRefusedException

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookTargetRefusedException.cs:7 · Level 0 · class

    • What it is: the exception the webhook client's connect callback throws, before any socket opens, when a target host resolves to a non-public address; the sender maps it to TargetRefused (WebhookTargetRefusedException.cs:3-6).
    • Depends on: BCL HttpRequestException (its base, line 7).
    • Concept: a typed refusal distinct from a transport failure. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether delivery failures are classified so retries are spent sensibly; a generic socket error is retryable, while a refused target is a policy outcome, and this type is what lets HttpWebhookSender tell them apart.
    • Walkthrough: the three conventional constructors (parameterless, message, message plus inner exception) at WebhookTargetRefusedException.cs:10,16,24; no extra state.
    • Why it's built this way: deriving from HttpRequestException means a throw from inside SocketsHttpHandler.ConnectCallback travels through HttpClient the way any connect failure does. The sender then catches it both directly and as the InnerException of a wrapping HttpRequestException (HttpWebhookSender.cs:82,86), so the classification holds either way.
    • Where it's used: thrown by WebhookConnectCallback (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:51), caught by HttpWebhookSender (HttpWebhookSender.cs:82-89); exercised by HttpWebhookSenderTests.cs, WebhookConnectCallbackTests.cs and MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Application.Tests/Webhooks/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandlerTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether HttpClient surfaces the callback's exception wrapped or bare is runtime behavior of the BCL, not visible here; the double catch exists so the code does not depend on it.

    SystemWebhookDnsResolver

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/SystemWebhookDnsResolver.cs:6 · Level 1 · class

    • What it is: the default IWebhookDnsResolver, a one-line pass-through to Dns.GetHostAddressesAsync(host, cancellationToken) (SystemWebhookDnsResolver.cs:9-10).
    • Depends on: IWebhookDnsResolver; BCL System.Net.Dns.
    • Concept: see IWebhookDnsResolver for why DNS sits behind an interface.
    • Walkthrough: ResolveAsync is an expression-bodied forward (SystemWebhookDnsResolver.cs:9-10); no caching, so every validation and every connect resolves afresh, which is what the connect-time check relies on.
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the production implementation trivial leaves all policy in the callers (DnsWebhookTargetValidator and WebhookConnectCallback).
    • Where it's used: registered as a singleton by WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:81).

    WebhookConnectCallback

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookConnectCallback.cs:16 · Level 1 · class (static)

    • What it is: the SocketsHttpHandler.ConnectCallback of the MMCA.Webhooks client. It resolves the host itself, refuses before opening a socket when any resolved address is not public, then dials only the addresses it checked (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:7-15).
    • Depends on: IWebhookDnsResolver, WebhookTargetRefusedException, OutboundAddressPolicy; BCL SocketsHttpHandler, Socket, NetworkStream.
    • Concept introduced, connect-time SSRF defense. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether server-side request forgery is closed, not just discouraged. A webhook target is operator-supplied, so a check at subscription time alone is defeated by DNS rebinding (the name resolves publicly when validated and privately when delivered) or by any handler that rewrites the request URI. Doing the resolution inside the connect callback means the address that is checked is the address that is dialled, on every delivery (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:11-14).
    • Walkthrough:
      • Create(resolver, allowInsecure) (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:22-29) returns a closure over context.DnsEndPoint.
      • ConnectAsync (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:31-71): an IP literal is used as-is, otherwise the resolver is asked (lines 38-40). An empty answer throws SocketException(HostNotFound), commented as not a refusal because it may be transient and so is retried (lines 42-46). Unless allowInsecure, the first address failing OutboundAddressPolicy.IsPublicAddress throws WebhookTargetRefusedException naming host and address (lines 48-53). Then each checked address is tried in resolver order; the first that connects wins, and when none does the last SocketException surfaces (lines 55-70).
      • ConnectToAsync (WebhookConnectCallback.cs:73-87) opens a NoDelay TCP socket, wraps it in a NetworkStream with ownsSocket: true, and nulls the local so the finally disposes only on failure (lines 75-85).
    • Why it's built this way: dialling the checked addresses itself, rather than letting the handler resolve again, removes the window between check and use. The refusal versus transport-failure split decides whether the internal-command queue retries. See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: wired as the primary handler's ConnectCallback in WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:95-99); covered by MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Tests/Webhooks/WebhookConnectCallbackTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: allowInsecure is fixed when the primary handler is built (it is read at WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:97-99); how long that handler lives is governed by IHttpClientFactory handler rotation, which is not configured in this code.

    WebhookInsecureTargets

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookInsecureTargets.cs:11 · Level 1 · class (static)

    • What it is: the single decision behind Webhooks:AllowInsecureTargetsInDevelopment: the flag is honoured only when a registered IHostEnvironment reports Development (WebhookInsecureTargets.cs:6-10).
    • Depends on: WebhookSettings; Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting.IHostEnvironment.
    • Concept introduced, a dev-only relaxation that fails closed. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses whether convenience switches can leak into production. AreAllowed returns true only when the flag is set AND an environment is registered AND it is Development (WebhookInsecureTargets.cs:17-18); a host with no registered environment reads as production, the rule of ADR-122.
    • Walkthrough: one method, AreAllowed(WebhookSettings settings, IHostEnvironment? environment) (WebhookInsecureTargets.cs:17-18).
    • Why it's built this way: four call sites must agree on the same switch (validator, sender, connect callback wiring, startup notice); one helper means a production host cannot accept an http target at validation while refusing it at send, or the reverse.
    • Where it's used: DnsWebhookTargetValidator (DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:30), HttpWebhookSender (HttpWebhookSender.cs:45), InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService (InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService.cs:27) and the connect callback wiring (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:97-99).

    WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse

    MMCA.Common.Shared · MMCA.Common.Shared.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.cs:13 · Level 1 · record

    • What it is: a WebhookSubscriptionResponse plus the plaintext signing Secret just issued for it, the answer to a create or a rotate (WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.cs:3-5,13).
    • Depends on: WebhookSubscriptionResponse.
    • Concept: show-once secret delivery. [Rubric §11, Security]: only the protected secret is stored, so this response is the only place the plaintext ever appears, and a receiver that loses it rotates (WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.cs:6-9). The secret is whsec_ followed by the Base64 key (line 12), the format WebhookSignature decodes.
    • Walkthrough: sealed record WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse(WebhookSubscriptionResponse Subscription, string Secret) (WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.cs:13).
    • Why it's built this way: composing the plain response instead of adding a nullable Secret to it keeps the secret out of every list and update payload by construction.
    • Where it's used: CreateAsync and RotateSecretAsync on IWebhookSubscriptionService (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:28,41) and the controller actions (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:87,140).

    InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService.cs:18 · Level 2 · class

    • What it is: an IHostedService that logs one startup Warning when Webhooks:AllowInsecureTargetsInDevelopment is set in a host that is not Development, so the operator learns the flag is ignored at startup rather than from refused subscriptions (InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService.cs:9-14).
    • Depends on: WebhookSettings, WebhookInsecureTargets; IOptions<T>, IHostedService, ILogger with [LoggerMessage] source generation.
    • Concept: the observable half of a fail-closed relaxation. [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability] assesses whether misconfiguration is surfaced; ignoring the flag silently would be safe but confusing, and this service makes the ignored state visible once (ADR-122).
    • Walkthrough: StartAsync (InsecureWebhookTargetsNoticeService.cs:24-33) reads the optional environment through IServiceProvider.GetService (line 26), and logs when the flag is set but WebhookInsecureTargets.AreAllowed is false (lines 27-30), naming the environment or "(none registered)". StopAsync is a no-op (line 36). The Warning text, generated at lines 38-41, states that targets must be https and resolve to public addresses only.
    • Why it's built this way: resolving the environment optionally rather than as a constructor dependency lets the service run in a host that registers none, which is exactly the production case the rule must cover.

    DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:16 · Level 4 · class

    • What it is: the default IWebhookSecretProtector, encrypting signing secrets at rest with the host's ASP.NET Core data-protection key ring under the purpose MMCA.Common.Webhooks.Secret.v1 (DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:9-14).
    • Depends on: IWebhookSecretProtector, Result, Error, WebhookErrorCodes; Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection.
    • Concept: secrets encrypted at rest, with an unreadable secret as a value rather than an exception. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses secret storage; [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses graceful degradation. A replica without the shared key ring (ADR-069) cannot read another replica's secret, and that surfaces as Webhooks.SecretUnreadable, never as a throw (DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:11-13).
    • Walkthrough: the Purpose constant is part of the stored format and never changes (DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:18-19); the protector is created once from it (line 21). Protect rejects null or empty and forwards (lines 24-28). Unprotect returns the failure for blank input (lines 33-36), otherwise Result.Success of the plaintext (line 40), mapping CryptographicException and FormatException to the same failure (lines 42-49). Unreadable() builds an Error.Failure with code WebhookErrorCodes.SecretUnreadable (lines 52-56).
    • Why it's built this way: returning Result lets HttpWebhookSender refuse to send unsigned instead of crashing a delivery; see ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: registered as a singleton (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:80, with AddDataProtection() at line 85); consumed by HttpWebhookSender.ReadKeys (HttpWebhookSender.cs:123); covered by DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtectorTests.cs.

    DnsWebhookTargetValidator

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:20 · Level 4 · class

    • What it is: the default IWebhookTargetValidator, the early answer to "may this URL be a webhook target?". The connect-time check stays authoritative; this one tells the operator at subscribe time (DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:11-16).
    • Depends on: IWebhookDnsResolver, WebhookSettings, WebhookInsecureTargets, OutboundAddressPolicy, Result, Error, WebhookErrorCodes.
    • Concept: defense in depth against SSRF, the early layer in front of WebhookConnectCallback. [Rubric §11, Security].
    • Walkthrough: ValidateAsync (DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:26-55) computes allowInsecure (line 30), then refuses any URI failing OutboundAddressPolicy.IsAcceptableTargetUri (lines 31-34). With insecure targets allowed, or an IP-literal host already judged by the policy, it succeeds without resolving (lines 36-40). Otherwise it resolves the host; any non-cancellation exception becomes a refusal (lines 42-50). It succeeds only when the answer is non-empty and every address is public (lines 52-54). Refused is an Error.Validation with code WebhookErrorCodes.TargetNotAllowed on the Url field (lines 57-58).
    • Why it's built this way: a validation error tied to Url becomes a field-level 400 for the operator, where a connect-time refusal would only show later as a failed delivery. Requiring ALL addresses public matches the connect callback, which refuses on any non-public address. See ADR-122 and ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: registered scoped (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:78) and called from WebhookSubscriptionService through its injected IWebhookTargetValidator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:26,250); covered by DnsWebhookTargetValidatorTests.cs.

    HttpWebhookSender

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:25 · Level 4 · class

    • What it is: the default IWebhookSender: one Standard Webhooks POST through the MMCA.Webhooks client, signed once per readable secret, mapped to a WebhookSendOutcome from the status code or the exception alone; the response body is never read (HttpWebhookSender.cs:14-19).
    • Depends on: IWebhookSecretProtector, WebhookSignature, WebhookDeliveryRequest, WebhookSendStatus, WebhookInsecureTargets, WebhookTargetRefusedException, OutboundAddressPolicy; IHttpClientFactory, TimeProvider.
    • Concept: outcome classification for an at-least-once delivery. [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration] assesses whether a sender tells retryable from terminal failures; this class turns every path into one of Delivered, Gone, Rejected, TransportFailed, TargetRefused or SecretUnreadable, and never throws for a receiver's behavior.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Constants: ClientName = "MMCA.Webhooks", UserAgent = "MMCA-Webhooks/1", JSON media type (HttpWebhookSender.cs:33-36).
      • SendAsync (HttpWebhookSender.cs:39-99): re-applies the scheme rule with the same switch as the connect callback, so a stored http:// row reaching a production host is refused, never sent in plaintext (lines 43-52). Reads the keys; none readable means SecretUnreadable, because an unsigned delivery is never sent (lines 54-59). Stamps a Unix timestamp from TimeProvider and joins one signature per key with spaces (lines 61-62). Builds the POST with the id, timestamp and signature headers and the user agent (lines 64-71), sends with ResponseHeadersRead (lines 75-78) and classifies (line 80). Catches map a refusal (bare or as inner exception) to TargetRefused, any other HttpRequestException to TransportFailed, and a client timeout (an OperationCanceledException while the caller's token is still live) to TransportFailed (lines 82-98).
      • Classify (HttpWebhookSender.cs:102-110): 410 is Gone, 2xx is Delivered, anything else, redirects included, is Rejected.
      • ReadKeys (HttpWebhookSender.cs:118-136): current secret first, then any readable previous one; an unreadable current secret yields no keys, an unreadable previous one is dropped.
    • Why it's built this way: signing with every readable secret lets a receiver verify across a secret rotation; never reading the body keeps a hostile receiver from streaming data into the host. The dev-only switch is shared with the validator and the callback (ADR-122, ADR-142).
    • Where it's used: registered scoped (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:79), its named client configured at lines 87-102; called by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:26,66); covered by HttpWebhookSenderTests.cs.

    WebhookDeliveryLane

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:20 · Level 15 · class (static)

    • What it is: the dedicated internal-command lane that drains webhook delivery rows (DeliverWebhookInternalCommand) with its own InternalCommandLaneProcessor, never the shared loop, so a slow or failing receiver cannot delay the other internal commands (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:14-19).
    • Depends on: InternalCommandLane, InternalCommandLanes, InternalCommandLaneProcessor, InternalCommandProcessor, InternalCommandNameResolver, InternalCommandsSettings, IInternalCommandSignal, FrameworkTableTargets, WebhookSettings.
    • Concept introduced, bulkhead lane over the durable job queue. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses failure isolation; webhook receivers are third-party and can be slow for minutes, so they get their own processor (a bulkhead) on top of the internal-command queue of ADR-114. Keying rows by subscription means a failing subscription is cooled down on its own and two deliveries to one subscription never run at once (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:17-18).
    • Walkthrough:
      • LaneName = "webhooks", the lane metric tag and log value (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:23); CommandType is the stored command name computed from the type (lines 26-27).
      • Create(settings) (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:36-49): cooldown from ReceiverCooldownSeconds (line 43), receiver key = SubscriptionId.ToString("N") (lines 44-46), MaxConcurrency from DeliveryConcurrency (line 47), and BatchStartBudget from TimeoutSeconds (line 48).
      • Register(services, configuration) (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:58-81): reads both settings sections straight from configuration (lines 60-62) and returns unless Webhooks:Enabled and InternalCommands:Enabled are both true; an existing InternalCommandLanes descriptor makes a second call a no-op (line 64). It registers the lane registry (line 69) and the processor as a plain factory singleton IHostedService, commented as needed because AddHostedService uses TryAddEnumerable, which refuses a factory registration (lines 71-80).
    • Why it's built this way: reading configuration directly at registration mirrors how the queue reads its own flag (WebhookDeliveryLane.cs:53-55), so a disabled feature registers no hosted service at all. See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: called from AddWebhooks (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:106); covered by WebhookDeliveryLaneTests.cs, MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.SQLServer.Tests/Webhooks/WebhookDeliveryLaneSqlServerTests.cs and MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.PostgreSQL.Tests/InternalCommandLanePostgreSqlTests.cs.

    WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Webhooks · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:19 · Level 16 · class (static, extension block)

    • What it is: the public DI entry point services.AddWebhooks(configuration), registering settings, the subscription store and service, the target validator, the signed sender and its client, the secret protector, the DNS resolver, the event catalog and the startup notice (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:23-28,67).
    • Depends on: every type in this part plus EFWebhookSubscriptionStore, WebhookSubscriptionService, WebhookEventCatalog and WebhookSettings; IHttpClientFactory, SocketsHttpHandler, Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection.
    • Concept: an extension(IServiceCollection) block as the public DI surface (C# extension(T) types, ADR-106). [Rubric §11, Security]: the client is locked down in one place. [Rubric §33, Developer Experience]: the remarks are an adoption checklist.
    • Walkthrough:
      • Settings bound, data-annotation validated and validated on start (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:71-74, ADR-070).
      • TryAdd registrations, so a host or test can pre-register a replacement: scoped store, service, validator and sender; singleton protector, resolver, catalog and TimeProvider.System (lines 76-83); AddDataProtection() (line 85).
      • The MMCA.Webhooks client (lines 87-102): Timeout from TimeoutSeconds; a SocketsHttpHandler with no redirects, no proxy, no cookies, ActivityHeadersPropagator = null and the WebhookConnectCallback (lines 89-100); every additional handler cleared (line 101) and all loggers removed (line 102).
      • The notice hosted service (line 104) and WebhookDeliveryLane.Register (line 106).
    • Why it's built this way: the remarks (WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:31-66) give the reasons. Calling this alone turns nothing on: with Webhooks:Enabled false the fan-out is inert and every administration endpoint answers 404; turning it on also needs pipeline.AddWebhookDelivery(), one AddWebhookEvent<TEvent>(name) per event, the migration, and a controller deriving from WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase granted webhooks:manage (lines 32-40). Clearing host-default handlers makes the internal-command queue the only retry and failure policy, removing loggers keeps target URLs (often carrying a receiver token) out of logs, and no traceparent/tracestate/baggage reaches the receiver (lines 47-55, ADR-009). A multi-replica host needs the shared key ring (ADR-069, lines 42-46). Lane defaults, including Webhooks:DeliveryConcurrency (default 4) per replica, are documented at lines 57-65 (ADR-142).
    • Where it's used: no call site outside the defining file in MMCA.Common/Source, and none in MMCA.ADC/Source (no AddWebhooks( or WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase match); exercised by AddWebhooksTests.cs.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the default of 4 stated in the remarks (line 59) matches its declaration on WebhookSettings (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookSettings.cs:41). The remark notes the HTTP client trace still records the URL path, so a host exporting traces to a shared backend must filter MMCA.Webhooks spans itself (lines 53-55).

    IDomainEventDispatcher

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces.Events · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Events/IDomainEventDispatcher.cs:8 · Level 1 · interface

    • What it is: the dispatch port for in-process domain-event delivery. A single method, DispatchAsync(IEnumerable<IDomainEvent>, CancellationToken) (IDomainEventDispatcher.cs:16), takes a batch of events and routes each to its registered handlers after an aggregate persists changes (doc comment, lines 5-7).
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent (Level 0).
    • Concept introduced, the dispatcher/handler split for domain events. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses whether events are dispatched after persistence rather than from inside aggregates, and whether handlers are discoverable. The dispatcher is the port half of the pair; the handler half is IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent>. [Rubric §1, SOLID]: the dispatcher depends only on the abstract handler contract (dependency inversion), so adding a reaction never edits the dispatcher.
    • Walkthrough: a one-method port taking a batch rather than a single event, which lets the implementation resolve handlers once per event type for a whole save. The only implementation is DomainEventDispatcher (Level 3), which fans each event out to every registered IDomainEventHandler<T> and, for integration events, additionally to every IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent>.
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the contract in Application (a port) and the implementation behind it follows the Clean Architecture ports-and-adapters split, and the outbox (ADR-003) reuses the same dispatcher for both the synchronous in-process copy and the background re-dispatch of persisted events, so a handler cannot behave differently depending on which path delivered its event.
    • Where it's used: the DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor collects domain events from aggregates, writes them as OutboxMessage rows, then calls DispatchAsync for the immediate in-process reactions (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Interceptors/DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:379); the background OutboxProcessor routes non-integration events through it (OutboxProcessor.cs:830), as do InProcessMessageBus (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessMessageBus.cs:24,31) and InProcessEventBus (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessEventBus.cs:85,101).

    IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces.Events · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Events/IDomainEventHandler.cs:10 · Level 1 · interface

    • What it is: the contract a domain-event reaction implements, with a single HandleAsync(TDomainEvent, CancellationToken) (IDomainEventHandler.cs:19).
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent (Level 0).
    • Concept: the handler half of the dispatcher/handler split introduced by IDomainEventDispatcher. IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent> is contravariant on TDomainEvent (the in keyword, IDomainEventHandler.cs:10), constrained where TDomainEvent : IDomainEvent (line 11); contravariance means a handler written against a base event type is usable where a handler for a more derived event is required. Per the doc comment (lines 5-8), implementations are auto-discovered by Scrutor assembly scanning and resolved from DI during dispatch, which the framework wires through ScanModuleApplicationServices<T>. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] and [Rubric §5, Vertical Slice]: a new reaction is a new file in the owning module, never an edit to shared dispatch code.
    • Walkthrough: a one-method port. Handlers that must succeed atomically with the primary transaction (a read model in the same database, say) implement it directly and let exceptions propagate; handlers that want their own failure context logged first extend SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent>, which logs and then lets the exception continue so the outbox can redeliver.
    • Where it's used: resolved and invoked by DomainEventDispatcher (Level 3) for every dispatched event; the dispatcher closes this open generic over the concrete runtime event type to find the right handlers.

    BaseDomainEvent

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.DomainEvents · MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:26 · Level 1 · record class (abstract)

    • What it is: the abstract base record for every domain event, supplying default values for both IDomainEvent properties so a concrete event type is a one-liner.
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent (Level 0). No externals beyond DateTime and Guid.
    • Concept introduced, record semantics for domain events. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses whether events carry enough context and whether consumers can stay idempotent. Declaring the base as a record class (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:26) gives structural equality, which is useful for value-based assertions in tests. Two properties are initialized inline at construction. DateOccurred = DateTime.UtcNow (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseDomainEvent.cs:28) captures when the business action happened, not when the event was dispatched, a distinction the summary comment draws explicitly (BaseDomainEvent.cs:5-8). MessageId = Guid.NewGuid() (BaseDomainEvent.cs:35) mints a unique per-instance id at construction time. Because MessageId is serialized with the payload it survives the outbox to broker to consumer round trip, which is what makes consumer-side deduplication through the InboxMessage table reliable (property doc, BaseDomainEvent.cs:30-34).
    • Walkthrough: two init properties with inline defaults, and nothing else. The type is abstract, so a consumer must declare a concrete event; derived events add whatever domain payload they carry (entity id, state change, and so on) as positional or init members. The first <remarks> block (BaseDomainEvent.cs:9-17) exists to head off a trap: structural equality is not a deduplication mechanism here. Both MessageId and DateOccurred default to a fresh value per instance, so two logically identical events raised separately are never equal, and anything relying on that comparison to spot a duplicate would silently never match. Deduplication is the inbox's job (ADR-021), keyed on MessageId.
    • Why it's built this way: inline defaults mean a concrete event record needs zero boilerplate, public sealed record SessionCreated(SessionIdentifierType SessionId) : BaseDomainEvent; is the complete type. Minting MessageId at construction rather than at serialization keeps the id stable even if the event is serialized more than once, which is the consumer-idempotency half of the at-least-once story in ADR-003. The creation-time default on DateOccurred is documented as a deliberate domain-modelling choice rather than an oversight (second <remarks> block, BaseDomainEvent.cs:18-25): a domain event's occurrence instant is by definition the moment the aggregate raises it, so stamping it at construction is the correct event-sourcing and audit semantic, and it is intentionally distinct from infrastructure timestamps that must be deterministically testable (audit fields, notification read-time), which are stamped from an injected TimeProvider. [Rubric §14, Testability] is the tension being resolved here, and the comment records that resolving it the other way (threading a clock through every aggregate) would not improve the model.
    • Where it's used: the base of every domain event across MMCA.Common, MMCA.ADC, and MMCA.Store; subclassed by BaseIntegrationEvent and by EntityChangedEvent<TIdentifierType>; it is the generic constraint on SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent> (MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:33); instances are captured into OutboxMessage rows and routed by DomainEventDispatcher.

    IEventBus

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces.Events · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Events/IEventBus.cs:13 · Level 2 · interface

    • What it is: the abstraction application code publishes IIntegrationEvents through. Two PublishAsync overloads (IEventBus.cs:20 and :25): a single event and a batch.
    • Depends on: IIntegrationEvent (Level 1).
    • Concept introduced, integration events versus domain events at the publish call site. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses reliable events, at-least-once delivery and idempotent consumers. A domain event is raised inside an aggregate, captured by the save-changes interceptor and dispatched after that save; an integration event is an intentional signal to other bounded contexts that may cross a service boundary. IEventBus is where that distinction shows up in a caller's code: you publish an IIntegrationEvent and the infrastructure decides how to route it. The doc comment (lines 5-10) is precise: the default implementation dispatches in-process through IDomainEventDispatcher with outbox persistence for at-least-once delivery, while alternative implementations (Azure Service Bus, RabbitMQ) can be substituted via DI. The "persist first, then act" guarantee lives in the concrete implementations, not in this interface.
    • Why it's built this way: two overloads rather than one keeps the batch case a single save and a single signal in the implementations, which is exactly the atomicity argument BrokerEventBus documents; a loop over the single-event overload would produce one transaction and one wake-up per event.
    • Where it's used: implemented by InProcessEventBus (the monolith default) and BrokerEventBus (the extracted-service path), both Level 13. Contrast it with the transport-agnostic IMessageBus that the OutboxProcessor drains through: IEventBus is the producer's API and writes the outbox row, IMessageBus is the transport's API and moves the row's payload onward.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Interfaces.Events · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Events/IIntegrationEventHandler.cs:17 · Level 2 · interface

    • What it is: the handler contract for receiving integration events. One method, HandleAsync(TIntegrationEvent, CancellationToken) (IIntegrationEventHandler.cs:26).
    • Depends on: IIntegrationEvent (Level 1).
    • Concept: mirrors IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent> (Level 1) but for cross-module notifications, and the doc comment contrasts the two directly (IIntegrationEventHandler.cs:5-15): a domain-event handler reacts to intra-module events, an integration-event handler reacts to cross-module ones, for example a Sales module handling UserRegistered from the Identity module. It is contravariant (in, line 15), constrained where TIntegrationEvent : IIntegrationEvent (line 16). Implementations are auto-discovered by Scrutor at singleton lifetime, and the doc states the consequence plainly: handlers create their own DI scopes internally (lines 9-12), which is what ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent> exists to do for you. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] and [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]: a handler written against this contract does not know whether its event arrived in-process or off a broker.
    • Where it's used: implemented by the framework's own OutputCacheEvictionHandler and by application handlers in ADC and Store; invoked in-process by DomainEventDispatcher (Level 3) and, on the extracted-service path, by IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>, which resolves every registered handler for the delivered event and invokes them in order.

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    BaseIntegrationEvent

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.DomainEvents · MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:11 · Level 2 · record class (abstract)

    • What it is: the abstract base for integration events, the events meant to cross module or service boundaries. It inherits BaseDomainEvent for outbox-pipeline compatibility and implements IIntegrationEvent so the dispatcher additionally routes it to integration-event handlers (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:11).
    • Depends on: BaseDomainEvent (Level 1), IIntegrationEvent (Level 1).
    • Concept introduced, explicit integration-event schema versioning. This base adds exactly one member beyond what it inherits: public virtual int SchemaVersion => 1; (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:32). [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] assesses whether contracts evolve without silently breaking consumers, and an integration event is a wire contract the moment it crosses a service boundary. The version is serialized with the payload, so a consumer has an explicit signal to branch or upcast on. The doc comment (BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:13-20) states the discipline precisely: additive or optional field changes keep the same version, while a breaking change (a renamed, removed, or retyped field) requires a new event type (for example FooV2) plus a consumer-side upcaster, never a silent reshape of an existing type. Concrete events bump it by overriding (public override int SchemaVersion => 2;). [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven]: the dual inheritance is the routing mechanism. BaseDomainEvent supplies DateOccurred and MessageId, so the outbox and inbox machinery (which operates on IDomainEvent) treats integration events uniformly, while the IIntegrationEvent marker is what makes DomainEventDispatcher fan the event out to IIntegrationEventHandler<T> as well.
    • Concept introduced, the upcaster is a registration, not a convention. The second doc paragraph (BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:21-30) spells out the migration mechanics from ADR-090. The owning module registers services.AddEventUpcaster<FooV1, FooV2, FooUpcaster>(), and a host that also still receives the retired contract over a broker adds x.RegisterUpcastedIntegrationEventConsumer<FooV1>() (IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:123) beside its plain x.RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<FooV2>(). Handlers are then written once, against the newest contract only. The framework preserves MessageId and DateOccurred across every hop, so inbox deduplication is unaffected by an upcast, and a fitness function requires the upcast target to declare a strictly higher SchemaVersion than its source. That last rule is what keeps the version number from being decorative. [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]: the handler set never has to grow a branch per historical contract shape.
    • Why it's built this way: declaring SchemaVersion virtual with a default keeps adding the member a non-breaking change, so every pre-existing event implicitly stays v1 with no edits (BaseIntegrationEvent.cs:19-20). See ADR-010 for the versioning policy, ADR-090 for the upcaster registration contract, and ADR-003 for why integration events ride the same outbox as ordinary domain events.
    • Where it's used: base of the framework's own OutputCacheEvictionRequested and of every cross-module event in the apps (for example ADC's SpeakerLinkedToUser, SpeakerUnlinkedFromUser, UserRegistered, and Store's ProductVariantChanged).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    EntityChangedEvent<TIdentifierType>

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.DomainEvents · MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/EntityChangedEvent.cs:24 · Level 2 · record (abstract)

    • What it is: the standardized CRUD lifecycle event base. Instead of separate Created, Updated, and Deleted events per entity, one event type carries the State (DomainEntityState) and the affected EntityId. Handlers filter on State to decide which transitions they care about.
    • Depends on: BaseDomainEvent (Level 1), DomainEntityState (Level 0).
    • Concept introduced, consolidated lifecycle events. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven]: one event type per entity avoids a proliferation of near-identical event classes while still carrying enough context to act on. The doc comment (MMCA.Common.Domain/DomainEvents/EntityChangedEvent.cs:5-20) draws the boundary clearly: derive one record per entity and raise it with DomainEntityState.Added from factory methods, DomainEntityState.Updated from mutation methods, and DomainEntityState.Deleted from Delete(); reserve a named event (for example OrderPaid, ShoppingCartCheckedOut), inheriting BaseDomainEvent directly, for business state-machine transitions with unique payloads (EntityChangedEvent.cs:15-19). [Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality] assesses change-amplification cost: collapsing three CRUD events into one keeps the event surface small, so adding an entity adds one record rather than three.
    • Walkthrough: a primary-constructor record (EntityChangedEvent.cs:24) with two positional parameters, State of type DomainEntityState (EntityChangedEvent.cs:25) and EntityId of type TIdentifierType (EntityChangedEvent.cs:26). The where TIdentifierType : notnull constraint (EntityChangedEvent.cs:27) prevents a nullable identifier from reaching the outbox payload. The abstract modifier forces consumers to derive a concrete record (for example CategoryChanged : EntityChangedEvent<ConferenceCategoryIdentifierType>), which may add extra payload of its own. The identifier types themselves come from the module-level global using alias convention (see the primer's conventions section).
    • Where it's used: the base of the generic CRUD events in MMCA.ADC such as CategoryChanged, EventChanged, QuestionChanged, SessionChanged, and SpeakerChanged.

    IMessageBus

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Messaging · MMCA.Common.Application/Messaging/IMessageBus.cs:29 · Level 2 · interface

    • What it is: the transport-agnostic abstraction for publishing integration events across module or service boundaries. Two PublishAsync overloads, single event (MMCA.Common.Application/Messaging/IMessageBus.cs:36) and batch (IMessageBus.cs:43).
    • Depends on: IIntegrationEvent (Level 1). Nothing else, which is the point: this file has one using and it is a Domain namespace (IMessageBus.cs:1).
    • Concept introduced, a transport-agnostic message bus for microservices readiness. [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness] assesses whether the transport is a swappable boundary and whether the business layers stay free of transport coupling. The doc comment (IMessageBus.cs:5-28) enumerates both implementations explicitly: InProcessMessageBus dispatches synchronously through the existing IDomainEventDispatcher path for the modular-monolith deployment (IMessageBus.cs:14-18), and BrokerMessageBus publishes through the selected framework broker transport (an IBrokerTransport, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Transports/IBrokerTransport.cs:22), RabbitMQ in development and Azure Service Bus in production, for the extracted-service mode (IMessageBus.cs:19-25, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerMessageBus.cs:46). The same comment records why transactional-outbox semantics survive the swap: OutboxProcessor drains OutboxMessage rows through this bus instead of dispatching in-process (IMessageBus.cs:21-23). It is also explicit that application code that publishes cross-cutting events should depend on IMessageBus rather than on IEventBus or on a transport-specific client (IMessageBus.cs:6-9). [Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]: the interface lives in Application and both implementations live in Infrastructure, so the dependency arrow points inward.
    • Why it's built this way: transport belongs at the edge (ADR-007, ADR-008). The same application code that called IMessageBus.PublishAsync in the monolith keeps working when the module is extracted and BrokerMessageBus is swapped in; only configuration (MessageBus:Provider, MessageBusSettings) changes. Application, Domain, and Shared must never reference a broker client directly (RabbitMQ, Azure Service Bus, Amazon SQS and SNS, and MassTransit, which is banned outright), and the architecture transport-boundary rule enforces exactly that (MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Layering/ArchitectureRules.Transport.cs:14-20, run by MMCA.Common/Tests/Architecture/MMCA.Common.Architecture.Tests/Layering/MicroserviceExtractionTests.cs:11). The MassTransit ban is a separate constraint enforced by the dependency-version fitness test, which fails the build on any MassTransit* PackageVersion (MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Bases/Governance/DependencyVersionTestsBase.cs:13-18, ADR-118).
    • Where it's used: implemented by InProcessMessageBus, the default scoped registration (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:366, with the rationale comment at DependencyInjection.cs:551-555), and by BrokerMessageBus, which Replaces that registration inside AddBrokerMessaging (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:119). At runtime it is resolved per cycle by the background OutboxProcessor (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:212) and invoked for every integration-event row it drains (OutboxProcessor.cs:590-600).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.DomainEvents · MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:32 · Level 2 · class (abstract)

    • What it is: a base class for domain-event handlers that must log their own failure with handler and event context before the exception continues to the dispatcher. It wraps an abstract HandleSafelyAsync in an exception filter that writes one error line and then lets the exception propagate unchanged (MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:36-47). Despite the name, it does not swallow anything.
    • Depends on: BaseDomainEvent (Level 1), IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent> (Level 1); externally Microsoft.Extensions.Logging.ILogger, taken through the primary constructor (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:32).
    • Concept introduced, log-and-propagate handlers and the at-least-once delivery contract. [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses whether event delivery is reliable end to end, and the class comment (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:13-20) records why the earlier swallow-and-log version was not: a handler that threw still reported success to the dispatcher, so its outbox row was marked processed, nothing ever retried, and the side effect was lost with only a log line to show for it. Propagating hands the decision to the delivery mechanism, which is built for exactly this. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: on the OutboxProcessor path the failed message keeps its retry count, backs off, and dead-letters after Outbox:MaxRetries attempts (default 5, OutboxSettings, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Administration/OutboxSettings.cs:21; the retry-count check that stops fetching an exhausted row is OutboxProcessor.cs:285 and the dead-letter branch is OutboxProcessor.cs:610). [Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]: the one job the base class keeps is the error line naming the concrete handler and the event type, so an operator can tell which handler failed for which event without every subclass hand-rolling that context.
    • Concept introduced, batch redelivery. The consequence subclasses have to design for is in the class comment (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:21-29): DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor dispatches every local event of one save in a single DispatchAsync call (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Interceptors/DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:379) and only then marks that whole batch processed (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:333). One rethrowing handler aborts the dispatch call and so skips MarkProcessedAsync for the WHOLE local batch: every local event written by that save is redelivered by the outbox processor, not just the event whose handler failed. Delivery is therefore at-least-once, and subclasses must be idempotent for their own event and for every sibling event raised by the same save.
    • Walkthrough
      • Primary constructor takes an ILogger (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:32), constrained where TDomainEvent : BaseDomainEvent (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:33).
      • HandleAsync (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:36) awaits HandleSafelyAsync (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:40) inside catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException && LogAndRethrow(ex)) (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:42). OperationCanceledException is excluded from the filter, so host shutdown propagates with no log line, because it is not a delivery failure.
      • LogAndRethrow (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:61) logs the exception with GetType().Name and typeof(TDomainEvent).Name under the message "Domain event handler {HandlerType} failed for event {EventType}. The event is redelivered by the outbox processor when the outbox is on." (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:63-67) and always returns false (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:69), so the filter never matches and the exception keeps propagating. The message's closing clause is conditional on purpose: redelivery happens only when the outbox is enabled for the host, so the log line does not promise a retry the deployment may not run. The throw; inside the catch body is unreachable and is commented as such (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:44-45).
      • Doing the log in a filter rather than a catch block is the point, and the method doc says so (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:56-60): filters run on the first pass, ahead of any unwinding, so the handler context is recorded even if an outer frame wraps or rethrows, and the original stack trace stays untouched.
      • HandleSafelyAsync (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:54) is the abstract method subclasses implement; its doc restates the idempotency obligation (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:49-53).
    • Why it's built this way: it puts the at-least-once contract of ADR-003 where that ADR expects the retry decision to live, in the delivery mechanism rather than in each handler. The handler reports the truth and the outbox decides on retry, backoff, and dead-lettering, and the ADR's matching obligation is that handlers stay idempotent. [Rubric §1, SOLID]: this is a template method, the invariant (log, then propagate, except on cancellation) is sealed in the base and only the varying step is abstract, so no subclass can accidentally re-introduce swallowing. A failed handler still does not roll back the primary save, but that is the caller's doing rather than the base class's: the interceptor's flush runs after the data is committed, catches the propagated exception itself, and signals the processor so the unprocessed rows are picked up (DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:345).
    • Where it's used: reached at runtime through DomainEventDispatcher, whichever caller dispatched the event (the save-changes interceptor after SaveChangesAsync, InProcessEventBus or InProcessMessageBus, or the background OutboxProcessor). The only subclass in the workspace today is TestSafeDomainEventHandler (MMCA.Common/Tests/Core/MMCA.Common.Application.Tests/DomainEvents/SafeDomainEventHandlerTests.cs:124), driven by SafeDomainEventHandlerTests, which pin the three behaviours: log and propagate, the log lands before the caller sees the exception, and OperationCanceledException passes through unlogged. The cross-module sibling for integration events is ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent>, which is the base the applications actually derive from.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the swallow-to-propagate history and the batch-redelivery contract come from the class remarks (SafeDomainEventHandler.cs:13-29), not from anything visible in the current control flow. The base class cannot enforce the idempotency it demands: that stays a subclass obligation with no compile-time or runtime guard. Not determinable from source: how a real side-effect handler behaves under redelivery, because no application (ADC, Store, or Helpdesk) derives from this base class today, so only the Common unit tests exercise it.

    ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.DomainEvents · MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:39 · Level 3 · class (abstract)

    • What it is: the base class for integration-event handlers, the cross-module sibling of SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent>. It supplies the two blocks every such handler would otherwise repeat: the DI scope preamble and the log-and-rethrow envelope (MMCA.Common.Application/DomainEvents/ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:8-11).
    • Depends on: IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> (Level 2), IIntegrationEvent (Level 1); externally Microsoft.Extensions.DependencyInjection.IServiceScopeFactory and Microsoft.Extensions.Logging.ILogger, both taken through the primary constructor (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:39-41), constrained where TIntegrationEvent : IIntegrationEvent (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:42).
    • Concept introduced, why a singleton handler has to open its own scope. [Rubric §29, Resilience, Reliability & Business Continuity] assesses whether repeated infrastructure ceremony is factored out of business code, and [Rubric §1, SOLID] covers the template-method shape that does it. The class doc states the constraint (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:12-19): IIntegrationEventHandler<T> implementations are registered as singletons by the module scan, so they cannot constructor-inject a scoped service such as IUnitOfWork without a captive-dependency bug. Each handler therefore has to open its own scope per delivery. This base runs the subclass inside an IServiceScopeFactory.CreateAsyncScope()-derived async scope and hands it that scope's IServiceProvider, so a handler body is only its own resolutions plus its own logic, and the scope is always disposed. That last guarantee is the reason to have a base class at all: an await using that every handler hand-rolled would eventually be forgotten in one of them.
    • Concept reinforced, log-and-propagate, identical to the domain-event side. The exception handling matches SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent> exactly, and the doc says so (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:20-25): the subclass body runs inside an exception filter that writes one error log line and then lets the exception propagate unchanged, while OperationCanceledException passes straight through with no log line because host shutdown is not a delivery failure. [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: the third doc paragraph (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:26-34) spells out what propagating buys on each of the two delivery paths, which is the detail that distinguishes this class from its domain-event sibling. On the outbox path the message keeps its retry count, backs off, and dead-letters after Outbox:MaxRetries attempts; on the broker path the inbox row stays unprocessed and the framework broker receive pipeline (BrokerReceivePipeline) retries, redelivers and then moves the message to the dead-letter queue (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:31-33). Delivery is at-least-once either way, so subclasses must be idempotent.
    • Walkthrough
      • HandleAsync (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:45) null-guards the event (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:47), then opens the scope with scopeFactory.CreateAsyncScope() (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:51) and disposes it through await using (scope.ConfigureAwait(false)) (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:52), passing scope.ServiceProvider to the subclass (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:54).
      • The whole block sits inside catch (Exception ex) when (ex is not OperationCanceledException && LogAndRethrow(ex, integrationEvent)) (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:57). As on the domain-event side, the throw; in the catch body is unreachable and commented as such (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:59-61); the filter is the mechanism, so the log write lands ahead of any unwinding and the original stack trace is preserved.
      • HandleScopedAsync (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:75-78) is the abstract member a subclass implements. Its signature takes the event, the scope's IServiceProvider, and the cancellation token; its doc restates that the scope is opened before the call and disposed after it, and that implementations must be idempotent (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:65-70).
