      • LogHandlerFailure (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:87-92) is virtual, not private, and that is the one genuine extension point beyond HandleScopedAsync. The default writes "Integration event handler {HandlerType} failed for event {EventType}. The delivery mechanism will redeliver the event." with GetType().Name and typeof(TIntegrationEvent).Name. Its doc (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:80-86) tells a subclass to override it in order to log the event's own identifiers through a source-generated [LoggerMessage] method, and imposes the two rules that follow from where it runs: the override executes inside the exception filter, so it must not throw and must not rethrow.
      • LogAndRethrow (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:99-104) is the private filter predicate. It calls LogHandlerFailure and always returns false, so the filter never matches and the exception keeps travelling.
    • Why it's built this way: it is the same ADR-003 division of labour as SafeDomainEventHandler<TDomainEvent>, with the retry decision left to the delivery mechanism, plus the scope management that the singleton handler lifetime forces. Sealing both concerns in a base class means a new integration-event handler in any module is a constructor, an override, and nothing else, which is why the applications derive from this class where they derive from its domain-event sibling not at all.
    • Where it's used: it is the base of the integration-event handlers across both apps, for example ADC's UserRegisteredHandler (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Conference/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Application/Users/IntegrationEventHandlers/UserRegisteredHandler.cs:62), SpeakerLinkedToUserHandler (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Identity/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Application/Speakers/IntegrationEventHandlers/SpeakerLinkedToUserHandler.cs:46), SpeakerUnlinkedFromUserHandler (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Identity/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Application/Speakers/IntegrationEventHandlers/SpeakerUnlinkedFromUserHandler.cs:31), the Engagement points handlers (AttendeeCheckedInPointsHandler, EventFeedbackSubmittedPointsHandler, SessionFeedbackSubmittedPointsHandler, UserDeletedPointsHandler), and Store's CustomerErasedHandler (MMCA.Store/Source/Modules/Catalog/MMCA.Store.Catalog.Application/Reviews/IntegrationEventHandlers/CustomerErasedHandler.cs:48). Its behaviour is pinned by ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBaseTests. At runtime the subclasses are reached either through DomainEventDispatcher in monolith mode or through IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> in broker mode.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the base cannot enforce the idempotency it requires, and it cannot stop a LogHandlerFailure override from throwing inside the filter; both stay subclass obligations documented in the remarks (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:33, ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase.cs:82-83).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    DomainEventDispatcher

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Services · MMCA.Common.Application/Services/DomainEventDispatcher.cs:23 · Level 3 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the in-process implementation of IDomainEventDispatcher. It dispatches each event to every registered IDomainEventHandler<T> and, if the event also implements IIntegrationEvent, to every registered IIntegrationEventHandler<T> after running the event through the upcaster chain. It uses compiled expression-tree delegates cached per (event type, handler interface) to eliminate per-dispatch reflection.
    • Depends on: IDomainEvent, IDomainEventDispatcher, IDomainEventHandler<in TDomainEvent>, IIntegrationEvent, IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent>, IEventUpcasterRegistry; externally IServiceProvider, System.Linq.Expressions, System.Collections.Concurrent, and ILogger<T>.
    • Concept introduced, compiled expression-tree delegates for handler dispatch. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] (this is the hot post-SaveChanges path, so reflection cost compounds with every event on a busy request) and [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven] (events fan out to all registered handlers reliably). The problem: IServiceProvider.GetServices(closedHandlerType) (MMCA.Common.Application/Services/DomainEventDispatcher.cs:74) returns object instances, so calling the generic HandleAsync on them would otherwise require reflection on every dispatch. The solution: on first encounter of a (eventType, handlerInterfaceType) pair, BuildInvoker (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:97) uses Expression.Lambda to compile a Func<object, object, CancellationToken, Task> that casts the object arguments to their concrete types and calls HandleAsync directly (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:105-116). Subsequent dispatches of the same pair reuse the cached delegate, with zero reflection.
    • Concept introduced, where upcasting happens on the in-process path. The class doc (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:15-21) states the split precisely: the integration branch runs the event through IEventUpcasterRegistry first, so a retired contract reaches the handlers written against its successor (ADR-090). That also covers outbox rows written before an upgrade, which deserialize back into the old type. The IDomainEventHandler<T> branch is deliberately left untouched, because intra-module handlers keep receiving the original type and the original instance. [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]: only the cross-boundary contract needs version tolerance; an in-module event has no independent deployment to be out of step with.
    • Walkthrough
      • _serviceProvider (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:25), null-checked in the field initializer.
      • _upcasterRegistry (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:32-33), a Lazy<IEventUpcasterRegistry?> over serviceProvider.GetService, resolved on first use and cached. GetService rather than GetRequiredService is deliberate and documented (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:27-31): this dispatcher is constructed directly in tests and in bare providers that never called AddApplication(), and no registry simply means no upcasting rather than a startup crash.
      • DispatchCache (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:41-43), a static ConcurrentDictionary keyed by (Type EventType, Type HandlerInterface) whose value is the tuple (Type ClosedHandlerType, Func<object, object, CancellationToken, Task> Invoker). Caching the closed handler type alongside the invoker keeps Type.MakeGenericType off the per-dispatch path (doc comment, DomainEventDispatcher.cs:35-40). Being static, the warmed cache is shared process-wide, and ConcurrentDictionary makes that thread-safe.
      • DispatchAsync (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:46) null-guards the batch (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:48), then per event always dispatches to IDomainEventHandler<> (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:55) and dispatches to IIntegrationEventHandler<> only when the event is also an IIntegrationEvent (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:60-65). The upcast happens on that branch: _upcasterRegistry.Value?.UpcastToTerminal(integrationEvent) ?? integrationEvent (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:62), and the terminal event's runtime type is what selects the handler set (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:64).
      • DispatchToHandlersAsync (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:69) GetOrAdds the cached (closedHandlerType, invoker) pair with a static factory (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:71-73, static so the lambda allocates no closure), resolves all handlers (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:74), and awaits each through the invoker (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:84). A null resolved handler is logged as a likely DI misconfiguration and skipped rather than throwing (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:78-82).
      • BuildInvoker (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:97) closes the open handler type (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:99), finds HandleAsync on it and throws a named InvalidOperationException if it is missing (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:100-101), builds ((IHandler<TEvent>)handler).HandleAsync((TEvent)event, ct) as an expression (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:105-113), and Compile()s it (DomainEventDispatcher.cs:115-116).
    • Why it's built this way: at-least-once domain-event delivery (ADR-003) requires the dispatcher to run after each SaveChangesAsync, so with many events per request the reflection cost would compound; the expression-tree cache makes dispatch near zero-cost after warm-up. Routing domain and integration events through one dispatcher rather than two keeps the in-process path uniform, which is precisely what lets InProcessMessageBus be a two-line adapter.
    • Where it's used: registered as the singleton IDomainEventDispatcher (MMCA.Common.Application/DependencyInjection.cs:38, beside the IEventUpcasterRegistry registration at DependencyInjection.cs:41); called by DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor after the outbox rows are written (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Interceptors/DomainEventSaveChangesInterceptor.cs:379), by the background OutboxProcessor when re-dispatching persisted domain events (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:830), and by both in-process buses (InProcessMessageBus, InProcessEventBus).

    IWebhookSecretProtector

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Subscriptions · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:9 · Level 3 · interface

    • What it is: the port that protects a webhook signing secret at rest and recovers it for signing. Two members: string Protect(string secret) (IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:14) and Result<string> Unprotect(string protectedSecret) (IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:19).
    • Depends on: the Result pattern from MMCA.Common.Shared.Abstractions (IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:1); nothing else.
    • Concept introduced, a secret that is stored protected and recovered without throwing. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses how secrets are held at rest and whether a key failure degrades safely. Here the subscription row only ever stores the protected form, and the read side is a Result, not an exception: the doc on Unprotect says it "never throws" and answers Webhooks.SecretUnreadable instead (IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:16-18). The interface doc names the operational consequence (IWebhookSecretProtector.cs:5-8): the default sits on the host's data-protection key ring, so a multi-replica host needs the shared key ring of ADR-069, or one replica cannot read a secret another replica wrote.
    • Walkthrough: Protect is synchronous and returns the stored form. Unprotect returns the plaintext on success. The default DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DataProtectionWebhookSecretProtector.cs:16) creates one IDataProtector under the fixed purpose MMCA.Common.Webhooks.Secret.v1, which its comment marks as part of the stored format, so it never changes, and answers a blank input as unreadable before trying to decrypt.
    • Why it's built this way: an unreadable secret is an expected runtime condition (a replica without the shared key ring, a rotated-out key), so modelling it as a Result lets the sender fail one delivery cleanly rather than crash the delivery pipeline. ADR-142 records the split: an unreadable current secret means nothing is sent, an unreadable previous secret is dropped from the signature set.
    • Where it's used: registered as a singleton by AddWebhooks(configuration) (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:80, WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions); Protect is called by WebhookSubscriptionService at create and rotate, and Unprotect by HttpWebhookSender for each stored secret it signs with (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/HttpWebhookSender.cs:123).

    IWebhookSubscriptionService

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Subscriptions · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:11 · Level 3 · interface

    • What it is: the administration API behind the webhook-subscription endpoints: two read-only properties and seven Result-returning operations, always scoped to the caller's tenant.
    • Depends on: the Result pattern (MMCA.Common.Shared.Abstractions), and the wire contracts in MMCA.Common.Shared.Webhooks (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:1-2): CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest, UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest, WebhookSubscriptionResponse, and WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.
    • Concept introduced, a show-once secret in the contract shape. [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] assesses whether a contract makes misuse hard. The return types do that here: only CreateAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:28) and RotateSecretAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:41) return a WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse; every other operation returns the secretless WebhookSubscriptionResponse, so no read path can leak the signing secret (interface doc, IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:6-10). [Rubric §11, Security]: the same doc fixes the tenancy rule, an id belonging to another tenant or to a deleted row answers Webhooks.NotFound, so a caller cannot probe for another tenant's subscriptions.
    • Walkthrough
      • IsEnabled (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:14) reports whether outbound webhooks are on in this host; EventTypes (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:17) lists the event types the host publishes.
      • ListAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:22) returns the caller's subscriptions without secrets.
      • CreateAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:28) and UpdateAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:35) take the request records; update replaces target, description and event types.
      • RotateSecretAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:41) issues a new secret while the previous one keeps signing for the configured grace period (doc, line 37).
      • DisableAsync, EnableAsync, and DeleteAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionService.cs:47, :53, :59); delete is a soft delete and returns a bare Result.
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the operations behind an Application port lets the API layer stay a thin adapter ([Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]), and the IsEnabled flag lets one controller base ship in every host and simply answer not-found where webhooks are off (ADR-142).
    • Where it's used: implemented by WebhookSubscriptionService, registered scoped (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:77); consumed by WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase, which resolves it optionally from the request services and treats it as absent unless IsEnabled is true (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:43-44).

    IWebhookTargetValidator

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Subscriptions · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookTargetValidator.cs:10 · Level 3 · interface

    • What it is: the port that decides whether a webhook target URL may be stored. One method, Task<Result> ValidateAsync(Uri target, CancellationToken) (IWebhookTargetValidator.cs:16), which answers success or Webhooks.TargetNotAllowed (doc, line 15).
    • Depends on: the Result pattern (MMCA.Common.Shared.Abstractions, IWebhookTargetValidator.cs:1); System.Uri.
    • Concept introduced, save-time SSRF feedback versus the connect-time guarantee. [Rubric §11, Security] assesses defences against server-side request forgery, where a stored URL makes the server call into its own network. The interface doc states the policy and its limit (IWebhookTargetValidator.cs:5-9): https only, no local host, every address the host resolves to must be public (unless insecure targets are allowed in Development), and the connect-time check of the delivery client "stays authoritative". This validator is early feedback for the person registering the target; it cannot be the guarantee, because DNS can answer differently at delivery time. The connect-time half is WebhookConnectCallback.
    • Walkthrough: a one-method async port. It is async because the default DnsWebhookTargetValidator (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/DnsWebhookTargetValidator.cs:20) resolves the host through IWebhookDnsResolver before judging the addresses.
    • Why it's built this way: putting the check behind a port keeps DNS out of the Application layer ([Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]) and lets tests substitute a fixed answer ([Rubric §14, Testability]). See ADR-142 for why the policy is checked where the socket opens as well.
    • Where it's used: registered scoped (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:78); called by WebhookSubscriptionService on create and update (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:249-250).

    IWebhookSubscriptionStore

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Subscriptions · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:10 · Level 5 · interface

    • What it is: the persistence port for WebhookSubscription rows, with four reads and three writes, every read filtered by tenant.
    • Depends on: WebhookSubscription (Level 4, MMCA.Common.Domain.Webhooks, IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:1).
    • Concept introduced, an explicit tenant argument instead of an ambient filter. [Rubric §11, Security] and [Rubric §8, Data Architecture] both look at how tenant isolation is enforced. Every read takes string? tenantId, and the interface doc fixes the semantics (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:5-9): reads filter by tenant, a null tenant matches only untenanted rows, and a soft-deleted row is never returned. Passing the tenant explicitly, rather than relying on the tenant-entity marker, is what lets webhooks work in a host that never resolves a tenant (ADR-142).
    • Walkthrough
      • ListAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:16) lists the tenant's subscriptions for the admin API; ListEnabledAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:22) lists only enabled ones, for the fan-out.
      • FindAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:29) returns one row tracked for an update, or null.
      • CountAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:35) counts non-deleted rows, which backs the per-tenant limit.
      • AddAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:41) adds and saves; SaveAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:47) saves an administrative change to a row from FindAsync.
      • RecordOutcomeAsync (IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:56) saves a delivery's last-outcome columns and swallows a concurrency conflict, because a concurrent admin change or another delivery outcome has already moved the row (doc, IWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:49-52). [Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: a lost last-outcome write is cosmetic, so it is not allowed to fail a delivery that already happened.
    • Why it's built this way: separating SaveAsync from RecordOutcomeAsync gives the two writers different conflict policies, strict for an operator's change, best-effort for delivery bookkeeping.
    • Where it's used: implemented by EFWebhookSubscriptionStore (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Webhooks/EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:30), registered scoped (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:76). Consumed by WebhookSubscriptionService, by WebhookFanOutHandler<TEvent> through ListEnabledAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/WebhookFanOutHandler.cs:70-71), and by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler through FindAsync and RecordOutcomeAsync (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Delivery/DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.cs:52, :69). WebhookRegistrationValidator fails startup when delivery is registered without a store (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookRegistrationValidator.cs:18-21), the fail-fast contract of ADR-070.

    WebhookSubscriptionService

    MMCA.Common.Application · MMCA.Common.Application.Webhooks.Subscriptions · MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/Subscriptions/WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:24 · Level 6 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the default IWebhookSubscriptionService. It validates a subscription definition, enforces the per-tenant limit, checks the target, generates and protects the signing secret, and drives the WebhookSubscription aggregate's own methods for every change.
    • Depends on: IWebhookSubscriptionStore, IWebhookTargetValidator, IWebhookSecretProtector, WebhookEventCatalog, ITenantContext, IOptions<WebhookSettings>, and TimeProvider, all through the primary constructor (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:24-31); also WebhookSignature and WebhookErrorCodes.
    • Concept, cheap checks before expensive ones, domain rules in the aggregate. The class doc (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:10-16) lists the contract: caller's tenant only, event types are "*" or registered in the catalog, targets pass the validator, and a new secret is generated, stored only protected, and returned once. [Rubric §4, DDD]: the service owns cross-cutting checks (catalog, tenant limit, DNS) while the aggregate's Create, Update, RotateSecret, Disable, Enable, and Delete own the entity invariants. [Rubric §11, Security]: the plaintext secret exists only in the returned response; the row receives secretProtector.Protect(secret).
    • Walkthrough
      • IsEnabled maps settings.Value.Enabled (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:34); EventTypes maps the catalog (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:37). UtcNow reads the injected TimeProvider (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:39), so disable and rotation instants are testable ([Rubric §14, Testability]).
      • ListAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:42) reads the tenant's rows and maps each through ToResponse (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:179), which never copies a secret.
      • CreateAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:50) runs in a fixed order. A missing eventTypes member binds null and is treated as empty so the domain answers EventTypesRequired (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:58-59). CheckDefinition runs first (line 60), then the tenant count against MaxSubscriptionsPerTenant with a Webhooks.LimitReached conflict (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:66-73; default 25, range 1 to 500, MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Webhooks/WebhookSettings.cs:66-67), then the DNS target check (line 75), so the network call runs last. The secret comes from WebhookSignature.GenerateSecret() (line 81), the aggregate is built with the protected form (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:82-87), saved (line 93), and returned with the plaintext once (line 94).
      • UpdateAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:98) finds the caller's own row, re-runs the definition and target checks, calls subscription.Update (line 124) and saves (line 130). It does not re-check the limit, because an update does not add a row.
      • RotateSecretAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:135) protects a fresh secret and hands the aggregate the previous-secret expiry UtcNow.AddHours(PreviousSecretGraceHours) (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:144-146; default 24, range 0 to 168, WebhookSettings.cs:59-60).
      • DisableAsync and EnableAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:153-158) go through the shared ChangeAsync helper (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:194); DeleteAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:161) returns the aggregate's own Delete() failure unchanged (line 169).
      • FindOwnAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:211) passes the caller's TenantId to the store and also drops a soft-deleted row (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:214), so every not-found case, including another tenant's id, collapses to the one Webhooks.NotFound error built at WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:191.
      • CheckDefinition (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:218) collects errors rather than stopping at the first: a URL that is blank or not absolute gives UrlInvalid (line 222), and every event type that is neither WebhookSubscription.AllEventTypes nor in the catalog is reported, de-duplicated, in one EventTypeUnknown error (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:231-244).
      • ValidateTargetAsync (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:249-250) builds the absolute Uri only after CheckDefinition has proved it parses.
    • Why it's built this way: running the database count and the DNS lookup only after the pure checks keeps a malformed request from costing a query or a network call, and collapsing every ownership miss into one error keeps tenant boundaries opaque. ADR-142 covers the rotation window and the show-once secret.
    • Where it's used: registered scoped as IWebhookSubscriptionService (MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:77) and reached through WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: the tenant limit is a count followed by an insert with no lock between them (WebhookSubscriptionService.cs:66 and :93), so concurrent creates can exceed it; ADR-142 records it as a soft limit.

    InProcessMessageBus

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging · MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessMessageBus.cs:18 · Level 3 · class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the IMessageBus implementation for the modular-monolith and integration-test case. It dispatches integration events synchronously through the in-process IDomainEventDispatcher, and it is the default registration when no broker is configured (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessMessageBus.cs:7-10).
    • Depends on: IDomainEventDispatcher (Level 1), IMessageBus (Level 2), IIntegrationEvent (Level 1). No externals at all.
    • Concept reinforced, same interface, different transport. [Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]: application code injects IMessageBus and never learns whether the events leave the process. Swapping the registration from this class to BrokerMessageBus is the entire "go distributed" change for the publish path.
    • Walkthrough: both overloads (InProcessMessageBus.cs:21 and InProcessMessageBus.cs:28) null-guard and then forward straight to domainEventDispatcher.DispatchAsync([integrationEvent], ...) (InProcessMessageBus.cs:24) and DispatchAsync(integrationEvents, ...) (InProcessMessageBus.cs:31). Neither returns an awaited task; both return the dispatcher's task directly, so there is no extra state machine on this path. No outbox write happens here: the doc comment (InProcessMessageBus.cs:11-16) is explicit that this bus is meant to be invoked by the OutboxProcessor when draining already-persisted entries, or by application paths that have already taken responsibility for outbox persistence elsewhere. It is the in-process counterpart of BrokerMessageBus, not a "persist and dispatch" bus. Code that wants persist-and-dispatch semantics uses IEventBus instead (InProcessMessageBus.cs:15-16).
    • Why it's built this way: keeping the monolith path a single synchronous dispatcher call means integration tests need no broker container and the common (monolith) deployment pays no broker latency. When the outbox is enabled, the OutboxProcessor still supplies the at-least-once safety net around this bus, because the processor is what invokes it.
    • Where it's used: registered as the default scoped IMessageBus in AddServices (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:366, with the rationale comment at DependencyInjection.cs:551-555), and therefore the bus resolved by OutboxProcessor in monolith mode (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:212). It is replaced by BrokerMessageBus in broker-mode service hosts (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:119).

    [Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    OutputCacheEvictionRequested

    MMCA.Common.Domain · MMCA.Common.Domain.IntegrationEvents · MMCA.Common.Domain/IntegrationEvents/OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:29 · Level 3 · record class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the framework's own integration event, a cross-service request to evict output-cache entries carrying the given tags. It is published by the service that owns the data, through the outbox like any other integration event, and consumed by every host that serves output-cached responses built from that data (MMCA.Common.Domain/IntegrationEvents/OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:6-9).
    • Depends on: BaseIntegrationEvent (Level 2), and through it IIntegrationEvent and IDomainEvent; EventNameAttribute (Level 0). The only BCL type in its payload is IReadOnlyList<string>.
    • Concept introduced, a framework-shipped event contract, and why a per-host cache needs a fan-out. [Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability] (cache correctness under scale-out), [Rubric §9, API & Contract Design] (wire contracts and their versioning), and [Rubric §6, CQRS & Event-Driven]. The doc comment states the problem precisely (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:10-15): ASP.NET Core's output cache is per host, because IOutputCacheStore is a local store, so a write in the owning service leaves a stale cached response sitting in front of every OTHER replica and every other service until its TTL expires. Broadcasting the eviction turns a per-process concern into a fan-out one message wide. Note that this is a framework event, not an application one: the events fitness rule records that framework-shipped integration events are the framework's own contract, gated by its conventions and public API baseline, so consumer residency rules and frozen snapshots neither police nor churn on them (MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Contracts/ArchitectureRules.Events.cs:146-153).
    • Walkthrough: the type carries one member and one attribute. [EventName("Common.OutputCacheEvictionRequested.v1")] (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:28) pins the stable wire and storage identity, which is what the outbox writes and what EventNameResolver reverse-resolves, so the CLR type can be renamed without orphaning rows or inbox keys. Tags (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:37) is an IReadOnlyList<string> defaulted to [], holding the output-cache tags to evict exactly as the producing host spelled them in its [OutputCache(Tags = ...)] or policy registration. Defaulting rather than marking it required is deliberate and documented (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:31-36): a message that arrives without the field deserializes into a harmless no-op instead of faulting the consumer and dead-lettering. SchemaVersion is inherited at 1 from BaseIntegrationEvent.
    • Why it's built this way: the doc calls it a frozen-contract candidate (OutputCacheEvictionRequested.cs:16-26). The wire shape is deliberately minimal, a tag list and nothing else, because every host that consumes it must be able to deserialize it forever. Any change is therefore a versioning decision (ADR-010): additive optional fields keep SchemaVersion at 1, while a rename, removal, or retype requires a new event type plus a registered upcaster (services.AddEventUpcaster<OutputCacheEvictionRequested, OutputCacheEvictionRequestedV2, ...>()) and a RegisterUpcastedIntegrationEventConsumer<OutputCacheEvictionRequested>() on every host still receiving the old contract until the queues drain (ADR-090). Riding the ordinary outbox path (ADR-003) means the eviction inherits the same at-least-once guarantee as any business event, and a duplicate eviction is harmless by nature, which is why this event needs no special idempotency handling.
    • Where it's used: consumed by OutputCacheEvictionHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Caching/OutputCacheEvictionHandler.cs:32), registered by AddOutputCacheEvictionHandler (MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Caching/OutputCacheEvictionExtensions.cs:111); its broker consumer is wired by the named shorthand RegisterOutputCacheEvictionConsumer on IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:158-160). It is published in ADC by BookmarkCacheEvictionProcessor (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Engagement/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Infrastructure/Caching/BookmarkCacheEvictionProcessor.cs:71), which paces the evictions that UserSessionBookmarkCacheEvictionHandler only flags (MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Engagement/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Application/UserSessionBookmarks/DomainEventHandlers/UserSessionBookmarkCacheEvictionHandler.cs:58).

    IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging.Consumers | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:11 | Level 4 | class (public static)

    • What it is: a C# extension(IBrokerConsumerBuilder) block (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:13) that adds one named registration shorthand, RegisterOutputCacheEvictionConsumer(), to IBrokerConsumerBuilder, the builder the configureConsumers callback of AddBrokerMessaging receives (class doc, IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:6-10). The generic RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>() and RegisterUpcastedIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>() are members of that interface itself (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Broker/IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:26, IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:40), not of this class.
    • Depends on: IBrokerConsumerBuilder, OutputCacheEvictionRequested. No external messaging library.
    • Concept reinforced, extension(T) members as registration sugar. The extension(T) syntax is the workspace-wide DI-registration idiom (see the primer's conventions section). [Rubric S7, Microservices Readiness]: a host declares what it consumes by event type and never names the consumer class the receive pipeline resolves, so the registration call site stays decoupled from the concrete consumer.
    • Walkthrough: RegisterOutputCacheEvictionConsumer() (IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:28) null-guards the builder (IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:30) and returns builder.RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<OutputCacheEvictionRequested>() (IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:32), so it chains like the interface members. It exists purely so the wiring reads as an intention rather than a type argument, and its doc pairs it with services.AddOutputCacheEvictionHandler() from MMCA.Common.API: subscribing without the handler is harmless but pointless, because the messages complete with a "no handler registered" log and nothing is evicted (IntegrationEventConsumerExtensions.cs:15-26).
    • Why it's built this way: there is one queue per host and no per-consumer endpoint configuration, and a consumer that exhausts its retries is dead-lettered, logged and counted (broker.fault.count) by the framework's receive pipeline, so no separate fault consumer and no fault opt-out parameter exist to register (IBrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:9-14). That leaves this class with nothing to hide but a type argument. The transport-boundary fitness rule keeps Application and Domain off any messaging library (MMCA.Common/Tests/Architecture/MMCA.Common.Architecture.Tests/Layering/MicroserviceExtractionTests.cs:7; ADR-007, ADR-008).
    • Where it's used: inside a host's configureConsumers callback (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:65, invoked at DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:92); the ADC Conference service passes ConferenceBrokerConsumers.Register (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:360) and its comments name RegisterOutputCacheEvictionConsumer as the broker half of output-cache eviction (MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:278-279).

    [Rubric S10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    WebhookSubscription

    MMCA.Common.Domain | MMCA.Common.Domain.Webhooks | MMCA.Common.Domain/Webhooks/WebhookSubscription.cs:38 | Level 4 | class (public sealed)

    • What it is: one outbound webhook subscription, an auditable entity keyed by Guid that names a target URL receiving a signed POST for every published integration event whose webhook type it lists, or every type through AllEventTypes = "*" (class doc, MMCA.Common.Domain/Webhooks/WebhookSubscription.cs:7-9; constant, WebhookSubscription.cs:62).
    • Depends on: AuditableBaseEntity<TIdentifierType> (WebhookSubscription.cs:38), Result and Error for the factory and update; BCL Uri for URL validation.
    • Concept reinforced, the entity factory returns Result<T> and guards its own invariants. The Result pattern is taught in the primer. [Rubric S4, DDD] assesses whether invariants live on the entity: here every property has a private setter (WebhookSubscription.cs:71-101), the constructor is private (WebhookSubscription.cs:66), and both Create and Update run the same ValidateDefinition so a subscription can never hold an invalid URL or event list. [Rubric S11, Security]: the signing secret is only ever held protected (ProtectedSecret, WebhookSubscription.cs:83); the plaintext is returned once, at create or rotate (class doc, WebhookSubscription.cs:15-19).
    • Walkthrough
      • Column widths and limits as public constants (WebhookSubscription.cs:41-59): UrlMaxLength 2048, DescriptionMaxLength 200, EventTypesMaxLength 2048, MaxEventTypes 20, ProtectedSecretMaxLength 1024, TenantIdMaxLength 64, LastFailureMaxLength 500. The EF mapping reads these rather than repeating the numbers (see WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions).
      • Create(url, description, eventTypes, tenantId, protectedSecret) (WebhookSubscription.cs:110) collects the definition errors (WebhookSubscription.cs:117), adds Webhooks.SecretRequired for a blank secret (WebhookSubscription.cs:118-122), returns every error at once (WebhookSubscription.cs:124-127), and otherwise builds the entity with Id = Guid.NewGuid() (WebhookSubscription.cs:129-137).
      • Update(url, description, eventTypes) (WebhookSubscription.cs:145) re-validates and replaces only the URL, description and event types; a failure leaves the subscription unchanged, and secrets and outcome columns are kept.
      • ValidateDefinition (WebhookSubscription.cs:221): Webhooks.UrlInvalid unless IsValidUrl holds (WebhookSubscription.cs:231-238), which demands an absolute https or http URL of at most 2048 characters with no user info and no fragment (WebhookSubscription.cs:273-281); the description is trimmed, blank becoming null, and over 200 characters yields Webhooks.DescriptionTooLong (WebhookSubscription.cs:240-245); an empty or blank-containing event list yields Webhooks.EventTypesRequired (WebhookSubscription.cs:248-253); the types are trimmed, de-duplicated and ordinal-sorted before joining with , (WebhookSubscription.cs:255-260), and more than 20 types or a joined string over 2048 characters yields Webhooks.TooManyEventTypes (WebhookSubscription.cs:262-267). The normalization makes the stored column canonical, so two subscriptions listing the same types store the same string.
      • Reads: IsEnabled() is DisabledOn is null (WebhookSubscription.cs:161); GetEventTypes() splits the column (WebhookSubscription.cs:165-166); Subscribes(eventType) matches the type or "*" ordinally (WebhookSubscription.cs:171-175).
      • Secret rotation: RotateSecret moves the current secret to PreviousProtectedSecret, records its expiry and installs the new one (WebhookSubscription.cs:183-190); GetSigningSecrets(utcNow) returns the current secret first, then the previous one while utcNow is before its expiry (WebhookSubscription.cs:195-198), so a receiver can switch without dropping deliveries.
      • State and outcome: Disable keeps the first disable instant (DisabledOn ??= utcNow, WebhookSubscription.cs:202), Enable clears it (WebhookSubscription.cs:205), RecordDelivered stamps LastDeliveredOn (WebhookSubscription.cs:209), and RecordFailed stamps LastFailedOn and truncates the reason to 500 characters (WebhookSubscription.cs:214-219).
    • Why it's built this way: the table is opt-in, mapped only where a host calls AddWebhooks with Webhooks:Enabled, so a host that never opts in builds an unchanged model (class doc, WebhookSubscription.cs:10-14). TenantId is a plain column stamped at create rather than ITenantEntity, and the store filters it explicitly, null matching only null (class doc, WebhookSubscription.cs:20-23). Url and the comma-joined EventTypes stay strings by design; the three suppressions say why (WebhookSubscription.cs:25-36): the URL is untrusted input validated as text and stored verbatim, and EventTypes is the mapped column while GetEventTypes() is its parsed view. See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: persisted by EFWebhookSubscriptionStore behind IWebhookSubscriptionStore, managed by WebhookSubscriptionService, and read on delivery by DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.

    • What it is: the EF Core mapping for WebhookSubscription, exposed as one ModelBuilder extension method, ApplyWebhookSubscriptionConfiguration (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:28).
    • Depends on: WebhookSubscription for its width constants; externally EF Core's ModelBuilder.
    • Concept: the opt-in table mapping. It is applied by ApplicationDbContext only to the one context whose source Webhooks:DataSourceName names and only while Webhooks:Enabled is true, so a host that never opts in keeps a byte-identical model (class doc, WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:6-11; the call, guarded by _webhookSubscriptionTableEnabled, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/ApplicationDbContext.cs:548-552). [Rubric S8, Data Architecture]: an optional feature adds no table, no migration and no model-cache churn to hosts that do not use it.

The method null-guards (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:30), maps the table under schema, default dbo (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:28, WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:34), keys on Id with ValueGeneratedNever() because the factory assigns the Guid (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:35-36), sets every column width from the entity's constants, with EventTypes and TenantId non-Unicode (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:38-48), and adds the composite (TenantId, IsDeleted) index every tenant-filtered read uses (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:50-51). It returns the builder for chaining (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:54).
    • Why it's built this way: the doc asks for it to run BEFORE the soft-delete, tenant and concurrency-token passes, so the subscription is treated like any other auditable entity (WebhookSubscriptionModelBuilderExtensions.cs:20-24), which is also what gives EFWebhookSubscriptionStore its soft-delete filter and the row version its concurrency catch relies on.
    • Where it's used: ApplicationDbContext (ApplicationDbContext.cs:551); exercised by WebhookSubscriptionModelGateTests and the SQL Server WebhookSubscriptionSqlServerTests.

    WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase

    MMCA.Common.API | MMCA.Common.API.Controllers.Webhooks | MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:40 | Level 6 | class (public abstract)

    • What it is: the abstract base controller for the outbound webhook administration endpoints: list, list the publishable event types, create, update, rotate the secret, disable, enable and delete, each scoped to the caller's tenant (class doc, MMCA.Common.API/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:12-16). A derived controller supplies only the route and API version (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:19-25).
    • Depends on: ApiControllerBase (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:40), IWebhookSubscriptionService, AdministrationPermissions through [HasPermission] (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:39), IdempotentAttribute and NonIdempotentAttribute, and the request and response records CreateWebhookSubscriptionRequest, UpdateWebhookSubscriptionRequest, WebhookSubscriptionResponse and WebhookSubscriptionSecretResponse.
    • Concept introduced, "off means no routes". Every action answers 404 unless the host called AddWebhooks AND set Webhooks:Enabled (class doc, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:27-30). The service is therefore resolved per action from HttpContext.RequestServices rather than injected, since a host without AddWebhooks has none to inject; the private EnabledWebhooks property returns it only when IsEnabled is true (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:43-44). [Rubric S11, Security]: a disabled feature is indistinguishable from an absent one, and the whole controller sits behind ManageWebhooks (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:39). [Rubric S9, API & Contract Design]: the class doc lists the refusal codes a client must expect, 400 for the validation codes, 404 Webhooks.NotFound (also for another tenant's id) and 409 Webhooks.LimitReached (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:32-37).
    • Walkthrough: every action follows one shape: return NotFound() when EnabledWebhooks is null, call the service, and map a failure through HandleFailure or the value through Ok.
      • GetAllAsync on GET (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:49, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:54) lists subscriptions, disabled ones included, never their secrets; GetEventTypes on GET event-types (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:67, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:72-73) is synchronous and returns the service's EventTypes.
      • CreateAsync on POST (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:79, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:87) and RotateSecretAsync on POST {id}/rotate-secret (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:134, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:140) are marked [NonIdempotent] because the response carries the only copy of the secret (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:80, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:135). After a rotation the previous secret keeps signing for Webhooks:PreviousSecretGraceHours (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:127-130).
      • UpdateAsync on PUT {id} (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:106, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:112) replaces URL, description and event types.
      • DisableAsync and EnableAsync on POST {id}/disable and POST {id}/enable are [Idempotent] (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:155-161, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:176-182).
      • DeleteAsync on DELETE {id} (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:197, WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:202) soft-deletes and returns NoContent() (WebhookSubscriptionsControllerBase.cs:210); deliveries already queued complete without sending.
      • Every action is public virtual, so a derived controller can override one without copying the rest.
    • Why it's built this way: the base-controller-plus-thin-derived shape lets each host mount the endpoints under its own route and version while Common owns the behavior; see ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: exercised by WebhookSubscriptionsControllerTests (MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API.Tests/Controllers/Webhooks/WebhookSubscriptionsControllerTests.cs).
    • Caveats / not-in-source: no derived controller exists in MMCA.ADC/Source; whether Store or Helpdesk mounts one is not determinable from the sources checked for this section.

    IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging.Consumers | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:40 | Level 7 | class (public sealed partial)

    • What it is: a single generic broker consumer that bridges a delivered event to the existing IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> contract, invoking every handler registered in the per-attempt DI scope and adding consumer-side inbox idempotency through IInboxStore (class doc, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:8-11). It implements the framework's own IBrokerMessageConsumer (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:44); no external messaging library is involved.
    • Depends on: IInboxStore, IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent>, IIntegrationEvent, EventNameResolver, ConsumerOriginRestore; externally ILogger<T> with source-generated [LoggerMessage] methods and an optional IServiceProvider. The four dependencies arrive through the primary constructor (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:40-44), constrained where TEvent : class, IIntegrationEvent (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:45). The provider is defaulted to null (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:44) so a test constructing the consumer with the first three arguments still compiles and simply restores nothing (parameter doc, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:30-38).
    • Concept introduced, the consumer-side inbox for broker idempotency. [Rubric S29, Resilience & Business Continuity] assesses at-least-once delivery paired with idempotent consumers, and [Rubric S6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses idempotent integration-event handling. Broker delivery is at-least-once: the same message can arrive twice after a consumer crash or a redelivery. The inbox makes that safe, and the API is a three-phase one rather than a check-then-record pair. inbox.TryBeginAsync(MessageId, eventTypeName, ct) (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:93) returns false when the message was already processed, in which case the consumer logs at Debug and returns without running handlers, completing the message (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:95-96). When it returns true, it has also staged the inbox row in this scope's unit of work, unsaved. The comment (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:88-92) is precise about who writes it: the framework's handlers do not save on this scope, because they are singletons that open their own scope per delivery (ScopedIntegrationEventHandlerBase<TIntegrationEvent>), so the row is written by CompleteAsync once every handler has succeeded. A crash between a handler's commit and that write redelivers the event, which is why handlers must still be idempotent. The interface's default implementations (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Inbox/IInboxStore.cs:38, IInboxStore.cs:48, IInboxStore.cs:63) fall back to the older check-then-record behavior, so an external implementation of the interface still compiles and still works.
    • Concept introduced, the inbox key is the event's declared name, not its CLR name. The key is computed by EventNameResolver.GetInboxName(typeof(TEvent)) (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:82), which returns the event's EventNameAttribute name when it declares one and its short type name otherwise (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/EventNameResolver.cs:59-60). The comment (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:79-81) records the compatibility reason: an unannotated event keeps matching the rows already written under its short type name. [Rubric S9, API & Contract Design]: the stable wire identity, not the CLR identity, is what a cross-service dedup key has to be built on, because the CLR name can be refactored.
    • Walkthrough
      • Entry point: ConsumeAsync(TEvent integrationEvent, IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string> headers, CancellationToken) (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:61-64) null-guards both the event and the headers (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:66-67). The explicit IBrokerMessageConsumer.ConsumeAsync(IIntegrationEvent, ...) implementation casts to TEvent and forwards (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:138-142), which is how the non-generic receive pipeline calls a generic consumer.
      • Context restore (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:69-77): when serviceProvider is not null, ConsumerOriginRestore.Apply(headers, serviceProvider, PrincipalAuthenticationType) (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:75-77) runs before the inbox is touched, held in a using var origin so the handle stays open for the whole consume and a handler that opens its own scope from the root factory still runs as the publisher's origin (comment, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:73-74), because the inbox store resolves its context through the scoped factory, and under database-per-tenant that routing is decided by the tenant this restores (ADR-073). A message published with no origin headers restores nothing, and the scope keeps the defaults it already had (comment, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:69-72). The stamped PrincipalAuthenticationType = "IntegrationEvent" (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:51) is what makes the rebuilt identity IsAuthenticated and names the hop it came back from.
      • Idempotency short-circuit (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:93-97): a duplicate leads to LogDuplicateSkipped and a normal return, which completes the message rather than dead-lettering it.
      • Handler loop (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:101-122): counts and invokes each resolved IIntegrationEventHandler<TEvent> in turn (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:106). On any non-OperationCanceledException (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:108) it first calls inbox.Abandon(MessageId) (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:113) so the failed attempt leaves neither a rejected insert on the scope's context nor an inbox row that would make the retry look like a duplicate (comment, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:110-112), then logs LogHandlerFailure naming the failing handler's full type name (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:119) and rethrows (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:120) so the receive pipeline retries in a fresh scope with exponential backoff (MessageBusSettings.RetryLimit, default 5, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:94), then redelivers or dead-letters (comment, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:115-118).
      • No-handler case (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:124-130): if zero handlers were registered for the event in this process, LogNoHandlers fires at Information and the method returns normally, so the message completes with no retry storm while the misconfigured service host stays visible in telemetry.
      • Mark-processed (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:134): inbox.CompleteAsync persists the staged row (for the framework's scope-per-delivery handlers this is the write; a handler that saved on this scope would already have committed it). Either way the message is recorded only on a successful consume, because the failure path above rethrows (comment, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:132-133).
      • Three [LoggerMessage] partials (IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:144-151) are the source-generated, allocation-free log methods, [Rubric S13, Observability & Operability].
    • Why it's built this way: application code keeps writing plain IIntegrationEventHandler<in TIntegrationEvent> implementations, which the module scan already auto-discovers; there is no broker-specific consumer class to write per event type (class doc, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:12-17). The receive pipeline deserializes the message, opens a fresh scope per attempt, resolves this consumer from it and calls ConsumeAsync, and a throw from here drives its retry, redelivery and dead-letter decisions (class doc, IntegrationEventConsumer.cs:18-25). That keeps the transport out of the handlers and out of the Application layer (ADR-021 for the inbox guarantee, ADR-003 for the outbox half, ADR-007 and ADR-008 for the extraction boundary).
    • Where it's used: chosen per subscription by BrokerConsumerBuilder for a current contract registered through RegisterIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>() (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Consumers/BrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:20; closed type at BrokerConsumerBuilder.cs:43-45), registered scoped once per subscription by AddBrokerMessaging (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:103-106), and resolved per attempt by BrokerReceivePipeline. A retired contract gets UpcastingIntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent> instead.
    • Caveats / not-in-source: whether the inbox actually dedups depends on which IInboxStore is registered. AddBrokerMessaging registers EfInboxStore when MessageBusSettings.IsInboxEnabled resolves true, which it does by default for a broker transport (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:132-134, MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:142); an explicit MessageBus:EnableInbox=false opts down to NoOpInboxStore, where the inbox calls do nothing and every redelivery re-runs the handlers, a posture announced once at startup by InboxDisabledWarningService.

    [Rubric S10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    BrokerMessageBus

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerMessageBus.cs:46 | Level 9 | class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the IMessageBus implementation that publishes integration events to the broker MessageBus:Provider selects (RabbitMQ and PostgreSQL built in; an adapter such as MMCA.Common.Azure or MMCA.Common.Aws registers its own transport) through the transport's IBrokerPublisher. Extracted microservices use it in place of InProcessMessageBus (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerMessageBus.cs:14-18).
    • Depends on: IMessageBus (Level 2), IIntegrationEvent (Level 1), IBrokerPublisher and BrokerTopology, taken through the primary constructor along with three optional context accessors, ICurrentUserService?, ITenantContext? and ICorrelationContext?, each defaulted to null (BrokerMessageBus.cs:46-51). It also uses BrokerHeaders, BrokerWire, OutgoingBrokerMessage and MessageHeaders; externally System.Text.Json and System.Diagnostics.Activity.
    • Concept introduced, the transport kept at the edge. [Rubric S7, Microservices Readiness] assesses whether a module can be lifted out of the monolith without rewriting application code, and [Rubric S6, CQRS & Event-Driven] assesses at-least-once delivery of integration events. The IMessageBus interface is defined up in MMCA.Common.Application, and Application, Domain, and Shared must never reference a messaging library directly, which the transport-boundary fitness rule enforces (MMCA.Common/Tests/Architecture/MMCA.Common.Architecture.Tests/Layering/MicroserviceExtractionTests.cs:7). BrokerMessageBus lives in Infrastructure and talks only to the framework's own IBrokerPublisher, so the wire format, topic naming and header set are owned by Common and each transport adapter only moves bytes.
    • Concept introduced, everything a consumer needs travels as headers. The doc comment (BrokerMessageBus.cs:27-35) lists them: the BrokerHeaders transport headers (event name, message id, content type), the W3C traceparent/tracestate of a Producer span on the MMCA.Common.Messaging source, and the request context as MessageHeaders origin headers (who raised the event, their roles, the tenant, the correlation id), read back on the other side by IntegrationEventConsumer<TEvent>. The outbox processor restores each row's captured context onto this bus's scope before it publishes, so the headers describe the original request rather than the background poll. [Rubric S29, Resilience & Business Continuity]: this is what lets a request's identity, tenant and roles survive the hop between processes.
    • Walkthrough
      • PublishAsync(IIntegrationEvent, CancellationToken) (BrokerMessageBus.cs:54): null-guards (BrokerMessageBus.cs:56), then derives everything from the runtime type, not IIntegrationEvent, because the topic, the wire name and the body all describe the concrete class consumers subscribe to (comment, BrokerMessageBus.cs:58-59): eventType, the wire name from BrokerNaming.EventName, the topic from topology.TopicFor, and the MessageId (BrokerMessageBus.cs:60-63).
      • It calls CaptureHeaders() (BrokerMessageBus.cs:65) and adds the event-name, message-id ("D" format, invariant culture) and JSON content-type headers (BrokerMessageBus.cs:66-68).
      • It starts a Producer activity named "{topic} publish" on BrokerWire.ActivitySource and, when a listener created one, tags it with the messaging system, operation send, destination and message id (BrokerMessageBus.cs:70-77). InjectTraceContext(activity ?? Activity.Current, headers) (BrokerMessageBus.cs:81) falls back to the ambient activity (the outbox row's), so a publish with no listener still carries the trace (comment, BrokerMessageBus.cs:79-80), [Rubric S13, Observability & Operability].
      • It serializes the event as its runtime type with BrokerWire.JsonOptions (BrokerMessageBus.cs:83) and hands an OutgoingBrokerMessage(topic, eventName, messageId, body, headers) to publisher.PublishAsync (BrokerMessageBus.cs:85-87).
      • PublishAsync(IEnumerable<IIntegrationEvent>, CancellationToken) (BrokerMessageBus.cs:91): null-guards (BrokerMessageBus.cs:93), then awaits each single publish in turn (BrokerMessageBus.cs:95-98). There is no transactional grouping across the batch here; batch atomicity is the outbox's concern, not the publisher's.
      • InjectTraceContext (BrokerMessageBus.cs:106-118) writes traceparent only for a W3C id, and tracestate only when non-empty: a hierarchical id has no traceparent form (doc, BrokerMessageBus.cs:101-105).
      • CaptureHeaders() (BrokerMessageBus.cs:126-145) builds an ordinal-keyed dictionary (BrokerMessageBus.cs:128) holding only the values actually present, each routed through AddIfPresent (BrokerMessageBus.cs:148-154): MessageHeaders.TenantId (BrokerMessageBus.cs:130), MessageHeaders.UserId formatted with the invariant culture (BrokerMessageBus.cs:131-134), then, when a current-user service is present, MessageHeaders.UserRoles from Context.AmbientOrigin.FlattenRoles and MessageHeaders.UserPermissions from Context.AmbientOrigin.FlattenPermissions (BrokerMessageBus.cs:138-139), and finally MessageHeaders.CorrelationId (BrokerMessageBus.cs:142). The doc (BrokerMessageBus.cs:120-125) explains why absence is not written as an empty header: an absent header reads as "the publisher had nothing to say", and an empty one would turn a non-answer into a false answer.
    • Why it's built this way: this bus does not itself write to the outbox. Transactional-outbox semantics are preserved by the OutboxProcessor: events are persisted to OutboxMessage in the same database transaction as the aggregate change, then the processor drains them by calling this bus (ADR-003; doc comment, BrokerMessageBus.cs:19-25). Keeping BrokerMessageBus a thin publish adapter, with no outbox knowledge, is what lets one set of outbox machinery serve both monolith and broker modes (ADR-007, ADR-008). The context accessors default to null, so a host or test that constructs this bus without them publishes no identity headers (parameter doc, BrokerMessageBus.cs:40-43).
    • Where it's used: swapped in for the default in-process registration by AddBrokerMessaging through services.Replace (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:119), which returns early without touching the container when MessageBus:Provider is in-process (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:72-75; see MessageBusSettings). It is driven at runtime by the OutboxProcessor, which resolves IMessageBus per cycle (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:212) and calls it inside a resilience pipeline that wraps only the broker hop (OutboxProcessor.cs:590-600).

    [Rubric S10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    BrokerEventBus

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerEventBus.cs:31 | Level 10 | class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the IEventBus implementation for microservice (broker) deployments. It persists integration events to the outbox and signals the OutboxProcessor to drain them, but it deliberately does not dispatch in-process, because the consumers live in other processes (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/BrokerEventBus.cs:12-17).
    • Depends on: IEventBus (Level 2), IDbContextFactory, IOutboxSignal, IDataSourceResolver, and IOptions<OutboxSettings>, all through the primary constructor (BrokerEventBus.cs:31-35); it produces OutboxMessage rows from IIntegrationEvent instances.
    • Concept introduced, the broker half of dual-mode event publishing. [Rubric S6, CQRS & Event-Driven], [Rubric S8, Data Architecture] (the transactional outbox), and [Rubric S29, Resilience & Business Continuity]. The doc comment (BrokerEventBus.cs:18-29) is explicit that this class differs from InProcessEventBus only in whether it dispatches synchronously after persistence: in-process mode writes the outbox, dispatches, then marks processed (BrokerEventBus.cs:23), while broker mode writes the outbox, signals the processor, and returns (BrokerEventBus.cs:24). In broker mode an in-process dispatch would be incorrect, since no consumer is present locally, so the processor's broker-publish path is the only correct delivery channel (BrokerEventBus.cs:26-28).
    • Walkthrough: both public overloads funnel into the private PublishBatchAsync, and neither is async: each returns the batch task directly. The single-event overload (BrokerEventBus.cs:38) null-guards and returns PublishBatchAsync over a one-element array (BrokerEventBus.cs:42); the batch overload (BrokerEventBus.cs:46) null-guards, coerces the sequence to an array exactly once (BrokerEventBus.cs:50), and returns Task.CompletedTask early when it is empty (BrokerEventBus.cs:51-52). PublishBatchAsync (BrokerEventBus.cs:65-94) resolves the outbox's logical data source through IDataSourceResolver (BrokerEventBus.cs:67) and gets its context (BrokerEventBus.cs:68). The outbox gate is the context's engine capability, !context.Engine.Capabilities.IsRelational (BrokerEventBus.cs:70): when the resolved engine is not relational (Cosmos for example) it throws an InvalidOperationException naming the misconfigured Outbox:DataSource and Outbox:DatabaseName, and tells the operator to configure SQL Server, PostgreSQL or SQLite (or fall back to InProcessEventBus), rather than silently dropping the events (BrokerEventBus.cs:75-76, with the rationale at BrokerEventBus.cs:72-74). Otherwise it reads the scope's ambient OutboxOrigin once for the whole batch, context.CurrentOutboxOrigin (BrokerEventBus.cs:79-81), because the batch is one caller's work so one origin describes all of it, then builds one OutboxMessage per event through FromDomainEvent(integrationEvent, origin) (BrokerEventBus.cs:83-85), AddRanges them (BrokerEventBus.cs:88; EF's synchronous AddRange is intentional here, as the comment at BrokerEventBus.cs:87 notes, since AddRangeAsync exists only for special value generators), saves once (BrokerEventBus.cs:89), and calls outboxSignal.Signal() (BrokerEventBus.cs:93) to wake the processor immediately instead of waiting for the next poll.
    • Why it's built this way: the one-save shape is the load-bearing detail, and the method's own doc explains it (BrokerEventBus.cs:57-64). A per-event save-and-signal loop cost a round trip per event and, worse, was not atomic: a failure partway through a batch left the earlier events committed and the rest unwritten, so a caller that saw a failure could not tell what had already been published. One save makes the batch all-or-nothing, and one signal is all the processor can consume anyway, because IOutboxSignal caps at a single permit and discards the surplus (see OutboxSignal). Beyond that, it enforces the transactional-outbox invariant of ADR-003 (persist atomically, publish later), while ADR-007 and ADR-008 motivate keeping delivery entirely on the asynchronous broker path once a module is extracted. Throwing on a non-outbox data source makes the "broker mode needs an outbox-enabled store" constraint fail loudly at the first publish rather than lose events quietly, and the same constraint is checked once at startup as well: EnsureOutboxAvailableForProvider rejects MessageBus:EnableOutbox=false under a broker transport with a message that names this class as the reason (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:80, DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:248-254).
    • Where it's used: registered as the scoped IEventBus when AddBrokerMessaging runs, replacing InProcessEventBus (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:125, with the explanatory comment at DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:121-124). Every IEventBus injection in application code resolves this implementation in broker mode.

    [Rubric S10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.

    EFWebhookSubscriptionStore

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Persistence.Webhooks | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Webhooks/EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:30 | Level 10 | class (internal sealed)

    • What it is: the EF Core IWebhookSubscriptionStore over the database Webhooks:DataSourceName names (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Webhooks/EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:13-14).
    • Depends on: IDbContextFactory, IEntityDataSourceRegistry, IDataSourceResolver, IOptions<WebhookSettings> and IConcurrencyConflictDetector, all through the primary constructor (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:30-35); it reads and writes WebhookSubscription through ApplicationDbContext and DataSourceKey.
    • Concept, explicit tenant filtering on a plain column. [Rubric S11, Security] and [Rubric S8, Data Architecture]: the subscription is deliberately not an ITenantEntity, so this store filters TenantId == tenantId on every read, a null tenant matching only untenanted rows, while the soft-delete filter hides deleted rows (class doc, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:19-23). Writes go through the unit of work so the audit interceptor stamps who created or changed a subscription.
    • Walkthrough
      • Context resolves the context for ResolveDataSourceKey() on each access, and Subscriptions is its DbSet<WebhookSubscription> (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:37, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:39).
      • ListAsync and ListEnabledAsync are AsNoTracking, tenant-filtered and ordered by CreatedOn then Id; the enabled variant adds DisabledOn == null (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:42-49, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:52-59).
      • FindAsync(id, tenantId) is tracked, matching on both id and tenant so another tenant's id is simply not found (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:62-63); CountAsync counts the tenant's rows (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:66-67).
      • AddAsync adds and saves through the factory (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:70-76); SaveAsync only saves, because the row came from the tracked FindAsync (comment, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:83-84).
      • RecordOutcomeAsync saves and swallows a concurrency conflict recognized by IConcurrencyConflictDetector (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:89-103): the delivery already happened, so a lost last-outcome stamp is dropped rather than failing the row into a retry (comment, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:99-101).
      • ResolveDataSourceKey() takes the registry's key for WebhookSubscription when one exists, and otherwise the configured source name verbatim on the engine of the framework-default source (EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:107-112), the EFMachineClientStore precedent (comment, EFWebhookSubscriptionStore.cs:105-106).
    • Why it's built this way: the store is internal so hosts depend on the IWebhookSubscriptionStore abstraction, and the data-source resolution follows an existing store rather than inventing a second rule. See ADR-142.
    • Where it's used: registered as the scoped IWebhookSubscriptionStore by AddWebhooks (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Webhooks/WebhookServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:76), consumed by WebhookSubscriptionService and DeliverWebhookInternalCommandHandler.

    InProcessEventBus

    MMCA.Common.Infrastructure | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure.Messaging | MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessEventBus.cs:34 | Level 10 | class (public sealed)

    • What it is: the default IEventBus implementation. When the outbox is on it persists integration events to the outbox, dispatches them in-process through IDomainEventDispatcher, and marks the rows processed; when the outbox is off it dispatches only (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/InProcessEventBus.cs:12-17).
    • Depends on: IEventBus (Level 2), IDbContextFactory, IDomainEventDispatcher, IDataSourceResolver, IOptions<OutboxSettings>, a required TimeProvider resolved from whatever AddServices registers it as, and an optional IOptions<MessageBusSettings>, all through the primary constructor (InProcessEventBus.cs:34-40); it also uses OutboxMessage and OutboxFinalizer.
    • Concept, the monolith half of dual-mode event publishing, and an explicit outbox opt-out. [Rubric S6, CQRS & Event-Driven] and [Rubric S8, Data Architecture]: the "persist to outbox in the same save, then dispatch, then mark processed" sequence is exactly the dual dispatch of ADR-003, and a dispatch failure leaves every entry in the batch unprocessed so the OutboxProcessor retries it (method doc, InProcessEventBus.cs:66-70). The second half of that doc (InProcessEventBus.cs:71-76) is the part to read carefully: with the outbox turned off (MessageBus:EnableOutbox resolving to false: set to false, or left unset, which is the in-process default) the direct-dispatch branch is taken instead, which means no rows, no save, and no processor to retry. That is the in-process default: MessageBusSettings.IsOutboxEnabled resolves an unset EnableOutbox from the provider, ON for a broker and OFF for InProcess (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/MessageBusSettings.cs:180, MessageBusSettings.cs:151), on the reasoning that a single-process application dispatches every event in the same process anyway. A monolith that wants at-least-once delivery across a crash sets MessageBus:EnableOutbox=true explicitly (MessageBusSettings.cs:160). [Rubric S31, Cost/FinOps] is the quiet counterpart here: store-and-forward that nothing needs still costs a table, a save, and a poller.
    • Walkthrough
      • Two readonly fields resolve the constructor's clock and posture: _timeProvider is assigned timeProvider directly with no fallback (InProcessEventBus.cs:42), so the caller (AddServices, or a test constructing this type directly) must supply one; _outboxEnabled falls back to true when no MessageBusSettings options are registered (InProcessEventBus.cs:44). The outbox default is documented as a compatibility choice (InProcessEventBus.cs:31-33): a host that resolves no MessageBusSettings options keeps the outbox path, so the opt-out is only ever taken because configuration asked for it.
      • Both public overloads funnel into the private PublishBatchAsync (InProcessEventBus.cs:78) and are non-async, returning its task directly: the single overload wraps one event (InProcessEventBus.cs:51), and the batch overload coerces the sequence to an array once and returns Task.CompletedTask early when empty (InProcessEventBus.cs:59-61).
      • PublishBatchAsync resolves the outbox target (InProcessEventBus.cs:80) and its context (InProcessEventBus.cs:81). If the resolved engine is not relational or the outbox is off, !context.Engine.Capabilities.IsRelational || !_outboxEnabled (InProcessEventBus.cs:83), it dispatches directly with no outbox persistence and returns (InProcessEventBus.cs:85-86).
      • Otherwise it reads the scope's ambient OutboxOrigin once for the whole batch, context.CurrentOutboxOrigin (InProcessEventBus.cs:89-91), because the batch is one caller's work so one origin describes all of it, then builds one OutboxMessage per event through FromDomainEvent(integrationEvent, origin) (InProcessEventBus.cs:93-95), AddRanges them (InProcessEventBus.cs:98, synchronously by intent, as the comment at InProcessEventBus.cs:97 notes), saves data plus outbox in one call (InProcessEventBus.cs:99), dispatches in-process (InProcessEventBus.cs:101), and marks the batch processed with a single set-based update through OutboxFinalizer.MarkProcessedAsync (InProcessEventBus.cs:103), passing the injected TimeProvider so the ProcessedOn stamp is testable.
    • Why it's built this way: writing the outbox row and the aggregate change in one SaveChangesAsync closes the dual-write gap, and dispatching immediately afterward gives synchronous in-process reactions without giving up the durable retry path. Finishing through OutboxFinalizer rather than a second full save keeps the hottest write path down to one extra statement, [Rubric S12, Performance & Scalability]. The non-relational (Engine.Capabilities.IsRelational) fast path keeps the framework usable on a store without an outbox table (dispatch-only) rather than failing, which is the deliberate opposite of the choice BrokerEventBus makes: with no local consumers, a silent dispatch-only fallback in broker mode would drop the event entirely, so that class throws instead.
    • Where it's used: the default scoped IEventBus registration (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:360), superseded by BrokerEventBus once AddBrokerMessaging is called (MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:125).

    [Rubric S10, Messaging & Integration Architecture] applies: this type sits on the path a message takes once it leaves the process (outbox, bus, consumer, or broker plumbing), which is what section 10 scores.
